That is a second, independent win: the container no longer re-renders on every Agent state transition and every right-panel toggle, neither of which changes anything it draws.
   - **The second defect was a leak with teeth.** `registerCodeActionProvider("*")` and `editor.registerCommand` attach to the `monaco` module, not to an editor instance, so they are global — yet they sat unguarded in `onMount`. One extra lightbulb provider and one extra command handler accumulated per tab switch, drained only at unmount, and each stale copy still called `getSelection()` on an editor Monaco had already disposed. They are now behind `agentGlobalsRegisteredRef`, matching the guards the completion and LSP registrations in the same function already had.
   - Registering once means the surviving copy must reach *current* state, not mount-time state: `activeEditorRef` is assigned on every mount and `runAgentActionRef` after every registration pass, so the one live provider and the one live command always dispatch into this mount. The ref indirection is the price of once-only global registration; both refs and the guard are per-component-instance, so a full unmount/remount of `EditorContainer` resets the guard in the same tick that `disposablesRef` cleanup removes what it guarded.

26. **Auditing 25 found the same defect 130 lines above the fix (fixed 2026-09-10)**
   - `agent-ide.apply-code-action` — the command every LSP quick fix routes through — was registered under the `lspRegisteredRef` once-only guard and closed over `editorInst`. After the first tab switch that instance is disposed, so `applyWorkspaceEdit` returned `false` at its `getModel()` check and **every LSP quick fix silently stopped applying**, while logging `Code action failed: … / Monaco rejected the workspace edit.` — blaming Monaco for a stale reference of ours. Same class as 25, one screen above the code I was fixing, and I walked past it: the commit message for 25 even cites these guards as the pattern to follow. Reading a diff is not reading the function.
   - It now goes through `activeEditorRef` too, and a missing editor reports itself instead of masquerading as a Monaco rejection. It also moved **out** of the `for (const language of …)` loop: one global command id was being registered five times, five handlers each holding the same dead editor. Monaco's registry keeps the last, so behaviour never showed it.
   - **The refs are now cleared when the editor goes away.** Closing the last tab unmounts `<MonacoEditor>` — but the lightbulb provider and this command are registered on the monaco *module* and survive until the whole container unmounts, so they were left holding a disposed instance. Today `getSelection()` on a disposed editor returns `null` and the guards bail, which means correctness rested on a library implementation detail; `QuickActions.tsx` already wraps `getTopForLineNumber` in a bare `try/catch`, which is evidence that this codebase has been bitten by the throwing version. `editorRef` state is cleared with it, since it feeds `MonacoContext` to six consumers that would otherwise compute coordinates from a dead editor.
   - **The context-menu actions never checked whether a run was live**, while the lightbulb did and `QuickActions` did. So right-click → "Explain with Agent" during a run wrote the prompt into the transcript and then had it refused by the backend's run-exclusivity guard: a message the user sent that never gets an answer. The check now sits in the one action function both entry points share (`runAgentSelectionAction` after the move in 27). The panel still opens before the refusal — the run in progress is the answer to "why did nothing happen", and it is more informative than the log line.
   - **Both of these were closed the same day, see 27.**
     - **A latent StrictMode footgun the guards create.** The unmount effect drains `disposablesRef`, but `completionRegisteredRef` / `lspRegisteredRef` / `agentGlobalsRegisteredRef` are refs, which React preserves across StrictMode's simulated remount. If that cleanup ever ran *after* `onMount` had populated the set, every provider would be disposed and never re-registered — dev only, silent. It does not fire today because `@monaco-editor/react` resolves `loader.init()` before creating the editor, so `onMount` lands after the strict cleanup/setup pair, and because `activeFile` starts `null` so no editor exists at container mount. Both are timing accidents, not guarantees. The honest fix is module-scope registration keyed on the `monaco` module rather than per-component refs — which is also what a split view would need, since `registerCodeActionProvider` and `registerCommand` are module-global while `agentGlobalsRegisteredRef` is per instance.
     - **`EditorContainer` has no tests**, and neither of the two defects in 25 nor the three here could have been caught by one without a Monaco harness. Every fix in both entries was found by reading, which is the weakest guarantee in this repo.

27. **The guard's lifetime now matches the resource's (fixed 2026-09-10)**
   - Everything registered on the monaco *module* moved out of `EditorContainer` into `src/components/editor/monacoGlobals.ts`, and the "only once" guard became a `WeakSet<typeof monaco>` instead of three component refs. This is the same fix as `RunLease` in Known Issues 24, one layer up: a guard whose lifetime differs from the thing it guards is not a guard. The refs died with the component while the providers lived on the module, so the two could disagree in both directions — StrictMode's remount preserves refs but re-runs effects (registrations disposed, guard still `true`, nothing re-registers), and a second container would have registered a second copy of a single global resource.
   - **These registrations are deliberately never disposed.** They belong to the monaco module, which lives until the page does; disposing them on a component unmount was the bug, not the cleanup. Only the per-editor things — the two cursor/selection listeners, `Go to Definition`, and the four Agent context-menu actions — stay in `onMount` and in `disposablesRef`, because those really do die with their editor.
   - Having no React closure available is what makes the module correct rather than merely tidy: `getState()` is the only way to read state there, so the stale-capture class from 25 is now unrepresentable. The one fact the module cannot derive is which editor is current, and that arrives through `setCurrentEditor` — called on every mount, with `null` when the last tab closes, and with `null` again when the container itself unmounts. **The third case was missing when this landed and the audit caught it**: the old code's `activeEditorRef` died with the component, a module variable does not, and `App.tsx` wraps `EditorContainer` in an `ErrorBoundary` — any sibling throwing unmounts it with tabs still open, which the tab-based effect does not cover. A module-level variable buys a lifetime that matches the resource and costs an explicit hand-back; forgetting the hand-back on one path was exactly the mistake this refactor was fixing one level down.
   - **It also made the code testable, which was the other open item.** `runAgentSelectionAction` is now a plain exported function; `monacoGlobals.test.ts` drives it with a two-method fake editor and real stores. Three tests: the prompt carries the file that is active *now* (the regression from 25, previously unpinnable), a live run refuses the second prompt and logs why, and no-editor / empty-selection do nothing. 105 → 108 tests.
   - `EditorContainer` drops from 931 to 431 lines, and `handleEditorMount`'s dependency array from five entries to three. The one behaviour change found while moving code: `kind: action.kind.replace(/\./g, ".")` was replacing dots with dots. Written as `action.kind || "quickfix"` now, which is what it always did.
   - The audit also found the new rule already violated next door: `configureTypeScriptSemantic` guarded itself with a bare `let configured = false`, so with two monaco modules only the first would ever get compiler options. Same `WeakSet` treatment. Writing a rule into `AGENTS.md` does not check the existing code for it — grepping does.

28. **The bottom panel's maximum did not know how tall the window was (fixed 2026-09-10)**
   - `clampBottom` was `Math.max(120, Math.min(500, h))` — two literals, no reference to the viewport. The window's minimum height is 600 (`tauri.conf.json`) and the vertical chrome is fixed at 40 (TopBar) + 4 (resize handle) + 24 (StatusBar), so 600 − 68 − 500 leaves the editor **32px**: less than its own tab bar. Nothing in the layout path read `window.innerHeight`; the editor row is just `flex-1 min-h-0`, i.e. whatever is left, and the panel wrapper is `flex-shrink-0`, so flex never takes any of it back.
   - **The restore path was the worse half.** A height saved on a large monitor is re-applied verbatim on a small window at import time, so the editor is 32px on the *first frame*, before the user touches anything, and the debounced writer persists the bad value again on the way out. A drag at least tells the user they did it.
   - Fixed by making the upper bound a function of the window: `maxBottomHeight(viewportHeight)` returns `min(500, floor(viewportHeight − 68 − 200))` with a floor of 120. At the 600px minimum that yields 332; at 900 and above the old 500 still applies, so nothing changes on a normal screen. The constant is `MIN_EDITOR_COLUMN_HEIGHT`, not `MIN_EDITOR_HEIGHT`, because the *column* is not the code area — `EditorTabs` takes ~29px and the save-error banner another ~28 — so 200 leaves Monaco roughly 145.
   - **The clamp is applied at render, not on the stored value, and the first attempt got this wrong.** Shipping it as a viewport-aware `clampBottom` plus a `resize` listener that wrote the clamped value back through `setBottomHeight` fixed the collapse and destroyed the user's preference doing it: shrink the window once and the 500 dragged out on a big monitor is permanently 332, persisted within 250ms, gone when the window grows again. The mount-time re-clamp also guaranteed a localStorage write on every startup, and the clamp kept firing while the panel was *hidden*, silently rewriting a value that was not even on screen. Now `bottomHeight` is the user's intent, clamped only against the window-independent 120/500; `App.tsx` keeps `viewportHeight` in component state and renders `min(intent, maxBottomHeight(viewportHeight))`. Nothing writes to the store on resize.
   - A drag still narrows the intent, because a drag is explicit — the user asked for that size. It starts from the *effective* height rather than the stored one, or the panel jumps on pointer-down whenever the two differ.
   - Tests split by what they are about. `maxBottomHeight` is a pure function of the viewport, so it is tested in the node-environment file: at 600 the editor column keeps at least `MIN_EDITOR_COLUMN_HEIGHT` **and** the bound is below 500 (so the assertion cannot pass vacuously), 500 survives at 900 and 1200, the result is an integer even for the fractional `innerHeight` that non-integer DPI scaling produces, and 120 wins under a 300px window. Asserting the invariant rather than the literal 332 is deliberate: a literal turns any constant change into a failure that points at arithmetic instead of naming the property that broke. The persistence test now asserts the *non-destructive* half — a saved 500 stays 500 under a 600px window.
   - **Not done: the same treatment for `leftWidth` / `rightWidth`.** Their maxima are 500 and 600 against a 1024px minimum width, which leaves the editor 500 − 68 of horizontal chrome even at both extremes. The arithmetic does not currently produce a collapse, so a viewport-aware clamp there would be speculative; recorded here so the asymmetry is a decision rather than an oversight.
   - Also unconverted, and found while auditing: `Explorer.tsx` has its own `(window.innerHeight || 600) - 120` for list height, with no shared constant and no resize listener. Second implementation of the same idea, and the inconsistent one. **Fixed 2026-09-10, see 29.**

29. **The file tree's virtual viewport was taller than the panel it lived in (fixed 2026-09-10)**
   - `react-arborist` needs a pixel height for virtual scrolling and does not measure its container, so `Explorer` passed `(window.innerHeight || 600) - 120`. That number has no relationship to the container: the tree sits in `flex-1` inside `LeftPanel`'s `flex-1`, below two headers, above the bottom panel. With a 900px window and the bottom panel at its default 240 the real height is roughly 530 while the tree was told 780 — so the virtual list was ~250px taller than the visible box, whose parent is `overflow-hidden`. **The last ~10 rows of the tree were unreachable**, and the deeper the tree the worse it got.
   - It also never recomputed. `window.innerHeight` read during render is a global nothing subscribes to, so resizing the window, opening the bottom panel or toggling focus mode all left the old number in place until some unrelated state change happened to re-render.
   - Now a `ResizeObserver` on the tree's own container. Measuring beats arithmetic here for the same reason the last commit's viewport math was fragile: the list does not need to know *why* its height changed, and every enumerated cause is one someone can forget.
   - **The first version of this fix wrote a measured 0 straight into state, which is worse than what it replaced.** `react-arborist` falls back to 500 only for `undefined` (`this.props.height ?? 500`); an explicit 0 renders a zero-row list, so instead of "the last rows are unreachable" the panel goes *empty with no indication why*. That is reachable: the left panel carries `hidden` for the 200ms collapse animation while `Explorer` is still mounted, and under jsdom `clientHeight` is 0 with no `ResizeObserver` at all, so it would have stuck at 0 forever. The measurement now ignores non-positive values and keeps the last good one, and the prop is `treeHeight || undefined` so the library's own default covers the not-yet-measured frame. `Terminal.tsx` already rejected zero-sized observations with the comment "Hidden panels can briefly report invalid dimensions" — the codebase had the answer and I contradicted it.
   - `useLayoutEffect` is kept, but **not** for the reason the first commit message gave. It cannot save a first paint: `loading` starts `true` and `loadRoot` is async, so `Tree` cannot mount in the same commit as the measurement either way. It stays as insurance for the day the tree data is synchronous or cached; the claimed benefit was doc drift, corrected here.
   - The `[]` deps are safe only because the wrapper div renders unconditionally, and the observer cannot self-oscillate only because that div is `overflow-hidden` (which zeroes its automatic minimum size, so nothing inside can push it taller). Both are load-bearing preconditions, now stated in the comment rather than left to be rediscovered.
   - No test. A real one would have to stub `clientHeight`, supply a fake `ResizeObserver`, *and* mock the Tauri bridge, since `Explorer` renders an error branch outside Tauri and never mounts the tree — and it would end up asserting on react-arborist's internal container. `Explorer.tsx` remains 866 untested lines.

30. **The local-model cache I "fixed" could never hold anything (fixed 2026-09-10)**
   - **First I wrote a fabricated bug report.** `commands/agent.rs` kept loaded local models in `local_engines`, and three places computed the key differently: `get_llm_client` and `unload_local_model` used the literal `"active"` when no profile id was passed, `local_model_status` used the real active id. I unified them behind one `engine_cache_key`, added three tests, and wrote up two user-visible consequences — a loaded model reported as not loaded, and the same multi-GB weight set occupying two slots. **Neither can happen.** `create_local_model_engine` had been reduced to an unconditional `Err` ("In-process local inference was removed") in an earlier cleanup, and it was the map's only writer, so `local_engines` was provably always empty. I audited my own commit, the audit found it, and it is exactly the failure this file lists as a repeat offender: a documentation claim not verified against the code. The keys really did disagree; everything I said the disagreement *did* was invented.
   - The honest reading is that the whole surface was dead: the cache, the key, `LocalModelStatus`, and the three commands built on them. `load_local_model` could never succeed either — before my change it failed with "Local engine is not configured" because nothing ever populated the map; after it, it failed with the in-process-removed message. I had converted a button that always failed into a button that always failed differently, which is not what "never ship a control that does nothing" asks for.
   - **So it is all deleted**, not polished: `local_engines`, `engine_cache_key`, `local_engine_for`, `active_profile_id`, `LocalModelStatus`, `expand_home`, `local_model_status`, and the `get_local_model_status` / `load_local_model` / `unload_local_model` commands with their `lib.rs` registrations. `create_local_model_engine` — a constructor that only ever returned an error — became `local_inference_removed(&LocalModelConfig) -> String`, called from the one place a real run passes through: `get_llm_client` now refuses a local profile up front with that message. The dead `EnhancedLlmClientFactory` (zero callers) went with it.
   - The frontend lost the matching controls: the model-type / directory / GGUF / threads / context / GPU-layers inputs and the Refresh / Load / Unload row, all of which configured or reported a runtime that does not exist. Choosing the `local` provider now shows the same instructions the backend gives — serve it through Ollama, LM Studio or vLLM and configure a normal endpoint. Deleting the frontend calls in the same commit is not optional: `tests/ipc-contract.test.ts` fails if an `invoke` has no registered command, which is precisely what that test is for.
   - The three `engine_cache_key` tests are deleted with the function. Keeping tests for deleted behaviour would be the same mistake in a different place — they passed, they were not vacuous, and they pinned an identity that no longer needs to exist. 275 → 272 Rust tests.
   - **What I should have done first**: grep for the map's writers before writing a word about what a wrong key causes. The lesson is narrower than "verify docs" — when a bug report claims a *consequence*, the consequence needs its own evidence, separate from the code smell that suggested it.
   - Left in place deliberately: `LocalModelConfig` and the `model_type` / `model_path` / `n_ctx` / `n_gpu_layers` / `n_threads` fields on the persisted profile. `model_type` still drives `is_local()`, which drives endpoint and API-key validation; the rest are now unread, and stripping them is a saved-config format change that deserves its own commit rather than a footnote in this one.

31. **The command allow-list had no test, and it is an authority decision (fixed 2026-09-10)**
   - `agent_tool_permissions` decides which shell commands the Agent may run this turn. The rule was an `if/else` expression inline in a function that also does a disk scan, so the only way to check it was to launch the desktop app. That is the wrong place for an unverified rule: getting it wrong either hands the Agent a command the user did not authorize, or silently drops one it needs.
   - Split into `allowed_agent_commands(tasks, allow_command_run)` — the tasks arrive as an argument, so the rule is pure and directly testable, while `discover_project_tasks` stays in the caller. Three tests: nothing is allowed when unauthorized *even though the project declares tasks* (authorization is a veto, not a filter), long-running commands are dropped (`npm run dev` never exits, so allowing it hands the Agent a way to wedge the run), and each of the three positional `bool`s lands on its own permission field. That last one exists because `agent_tool_permissions(a, b, c)` takes three same-typed flags: swapping two compiles silently and would grant write access to a user who only allowed command execution.
   - **The first version of the split introduced a regression I caught before committing**: hoisting the scan above the authorization check meant every run read `package.json` and `Cargo.toml` even when commands were forbidden. The scan is now behind its own `if`, which looks redundant next to the one inside `allowed_agent_commands` but is not — one avoids I/O nobody will use, the other is the rule under test. Worth recording because the same reflex ("lift the impure part out") is what a future refactor here will reach for.
   - 272 → 275 tests. `commands/agent.rs` still has 41 commands and now two covered helpers, which is progress and not yet a claim of coverage.

32. **"What did the user originally ask for" was decided in three places (fixed 2026-09-10)**
   - `verify_workspace`, `repair_workspace` and `agent_repair_prompt` each carried a byte-identical `match` deciding the task description that goes into the repair prompt. Three copies of one rule is three chances for one of them to drift, and the value matters: it is the only thing telling the model what the failing checks were *supposed* to achieve. Lose it and the model repairs blind — it sees errors and no goal.
   - Now one `resolve_original_prompt(requested, last_turn)`, with the three rules under test. The one worth naming is the middle one: **a whitespace-only request counts as absent.** The frontend sends `Some("")` from an empty input, so a naive `Option` check would pass an empty description through as if the user had specified it. The third test asserts the no-conversation case yields the explicit `(original task not recorded)` marker rather than an empty string — a placeholder in the prompt is diagnosable, blank space is not.
   - The cost is one extra uncontended `lock().await` in the two commands that did not already hold the orchestrator: the fallback used to be computed lazily inside the match arm. Accepted deliberately, and it is only cheap because of Known Issues 16/23 — the orchestrator lock is never held across an `await` any more, so acquiring it is a few instructions rather than a wait behind a model call. Both new acquisitions are scoped to a block that ends before the next `await`, and neither function holds the lock elsewhere, so there is no nesting of a non-reentrant `tokio::sync::Mutex`. I checked that specifically; it is the failure mode that would have turned this tidy-up into a hang.
   - 275 → 278 tests.

33. **`AgentStatus.contextFiles` was always empty on the wire (fixed 2026-09-10)**
   - `get_agent_state` — the state snapshot the frontend reconciles a restored session against — declared a `context_files: Vec<String>` field and constructed it as `Vec::new()`, unconditionally, at its only construction site. The TypeScript `AgentStatusResponse` declared it too (as `context_files`, snake_case, unlike every camelCase sibling), and nothing read it: the sole consumer, `reconcileBackendRun`, uses `currentRunId` / `lastRunId` / `ideMode`.
   - Deleted from both sides. The same class as the deleted permission toggles and the `Edit` mode: a field that looks like information and carries none. Worse here than a dead local, because it is on an IPC boundary — the next reader would take an empty list as "no context files", not "this was never populated".
   - Checked before deleting, in the order that matters: **one** construction site, no other Rust reader, no frontend reader, and the field is absent from the `agent-state-changed` payload (`state_payload()` is a separate shape, so the event is unaffected). The `context_files` fields on the *request* DTOs are real inbound data and stay.
   - No test added. There is nothing left to assert — the honest guarantee here is the type, and `tsc` plus the IPC contract test already cover "the two sides agree".

34. **The "did this apply actually work" log level was decided four times (fixed 2026-09-10)**
   - `apply_diffs`, `apply_diff`, `apply_diff_hunk` and `undo_last_apply` each carried their own `if failed.is_empty() { "success" } else { "warn" }`. **All four agreed** — this is drift prevention, not a bug fix, and worth saying plainly rather than dressing up as a find.
   - It is still worth one place and one test, because the rule guards the product's premise: the user has to be able to see what the Agent actually did. An apply that failed on some files but is logged as `success` tells them everything landed when it did not. `apply_log_level(failed)` now owns it.
   - The test asserts the *property*, not the string: zero failures is `success`, and any failure must be a level the user notices — `matches!(level, "warn" | "error")`. Choosing between warn and error later is a legitimate change; letting a partial failure read as success is not. An `assert_eq!(…, "warn")` would have failed the legitimate change and passed a hypothetical `"info"` regression, which is backwards.
   - The `reject_*` commands keep their fixed `"info"`: rejecting is the user's own decision, so there is no success/failure to report. Not folded into the helper.
   - 278 → 279 tests.

35. **Pasting a folder into itself had no guard at either layer (fixed 2026-09-13)**
   - `copy_path` resolved both paths, refused an existing destination, then called `copy_dir_recursive`, which does `create_dir_all(dest)` **first** and only then `read_dir(src)`. With `dest` inside `src`, the enumeration can meet the directory it just created. Whether that recurses is filesystem-dependent — I am not claiming it always does — but the outcome is unpredictable rather than merely slow, and at best it is a partial copy of a directory into itself.
   - **Two clicks away in the UI.** `Explorer`'s paste target is the directory that was right-clicked (`targetNode?.isDir ? targetNode.path : workspacePath`), and Paste is offered on any folder. Copy a folder, right-click that same folder, Paste → destination `a/a Copy`. Right-click a child instead → `a/b/a Copy`. Neither the frontend nor the backend checked containment.
   - Fixed in the **backend**, not the menu: `copy_path` is a command the CLI and any future caller can reach, and a guard in the context menu would only cover the one path I happened to look at. `if src_path.is_dir() && dest_path.starts_with(src_path)`. It sits *before* the `dest_path.exists()` check on purpose — when `dest == src` the existing check already refuses, but it says "Destination already exists", which points at the wrong problem.
   - `Path::starts_with` is component-wise, which matters: a string-prefix comparison would reject copying `a` into `ab/`, since `"…/ab"` starts with `"…/a"`. The third test pins exactly that, and it is the one that would catch the plausible wrong implementation.
   - **`commands/fs.rs` had no tests at all**; it now has four, with the `TestEnv` pattern the other modules use. Assertions are on "nothing happened" (the destination does not exist) rather than on error prose, and the file case is covered too — only directories can contain themselves.
   - 279 → 283 tests.
   - **Found while exploring `Explorer.tsx` for extractable logic; not fixed here, recorded so it is not lost:** paste always renames (`foo Copy.ts` even when pasting into a folder where `foo.ts` does not exist), collision detection substring-matches the backend's `"Destination already exists"` prose so rewording that string silently breaks the retry loop, `joinPath` always emits `/` so destinations on Windows are mixed-separator, name dialogs validate only non-empty (`../x` and `a/b/c` reach `joinPath` intact), and `loadRoot` discards every loaded subtree on any create/delete/rename/paste while react-arborist still believes those folders are open — so an expanded folder renders open-and-empty until toggled twice.

36. **Context management: the turns are now addressable, and cuttable (2026-09-13)**
   - Asked whether a user can pick any request node in the session and fork a new session from it, or compress from there. The answer was no, and the reason was more basic than "the feature is missing": **there was nothing to point at.** `ConversationTurn` was `{prompt, outcome}` with no identity, and the transcript the user sees is a different list from the one the model receives.
   - What the two lists actually are, because the gap is the design constraint here:
     - Backend `conversation: Vec<ConversationTurn>` is the context. Capped at 6 turns, `prompt` truncated to 400 chars and `outcome` to 300, and `outcome` is not the assistant's reply — it is a derived one-line summary of diff state. A turn is recorded when a run **succeeds**: `send_agent_prompt` and `run_agent_step` push one, continuing a paused pipeline and a repair loop update the turn they continue (see Known Issues 116), and cancel or error add nothing. Step turns carry `derived: true` and are skipped by `last_task_prompt()` (117). It reaches the model as one flattened "Earlier turns" context *section*, not as chat messages.
     - Frontend `messages: ChatMessage[]` is the display. Unbounded, never persisted, pushed by each of five call sites with ad-hoc `Date.now()` ids, and it survives no reload while the Rust process keeps its 6 turns.
     - So a webview reload leaves an empty transcript with a full backend context, and a cancelled run leaves a bubble with no turn. **Per-bubble actions would therefore be a lie** — the user would click the third bubble and cut a different turn.
   - Built: `ConversationTurn` gains a backend-assigned `id` (monotonic, never reused), `truncate_conversation_from(turn_id)` drops that turn and everything after it, and two commands — `get_agent_conversation` and `truncate_agent_conversation` — expose and cut it. The UI is a collapsed strip above the transcript, "Context sent to the model · N turns", listing the real turns with a per-row *Cut from here*. That strip also closes a plainer gap: until now nothing showed the user what history the model was actually getting.
   - **Addressed by id, not index, and that is the load-bearing part.** Head eviction shifts every index, so the third row the user sees can be a different turn by the time the click arrives. One of the four tests exists only to pin this: it records 6 turns, notes an id and its index, records 2 more so the head is evicted, asserts the index has moved, then cuts by the original id and checks the right turns disappeared. An unknown id is refused and changes nothing — the turn may have aged out, and silently cutting something else is worse than an error.
   - Assertions sit on `conversation_digest()`, not on `conversation.len()`: the digest is what reaches the model, the vector is its internal representation. Cutting from the first turn must make the digest `None` rather than an empty string, or the prompt gains an empty "Earlier turns" section.
   - **Not built: forking into a *new* session.** There is exactly one conversation in this product — no session list, no session id, and `clearAgentSession` does not even clear `messages` or call the backend. Cutting the context at a turn is what "go back to that point and continue from there" means in a single-conversation model, and it delivers the useful half now. A real fork needs a session concept (list, persistence, switcher) and is a much larger change than it looks; recorded as the next step rather than smuggled in half-done.
   - **Also not built: compressing the conversation.** `ContextCompressionMode` sounds like it would apply here and does not — it is per-*section* text reduction that only branches for `active_file_content` and `git_diff`, and never touches the conversation digest, which is bounded solely by the hard 6-turn drain. Summarising older turns via an LLM call is a real feature and a separate one; the honest version of "compress" available today is the cut.
   - 283 → 287 tests.

37. **The Agent could not delete a file at all (fixed 2026-09-13)**
   - Asked for built-in cross-platform file / system / browser tooling. Checked what the Agent actually has first: five tools — `read_file`, `search_text`, `list_files` (unconditional), `write_file` (behind `allow_write`), `run_command` (behind a non-empty allow-list). No move, rename, delete, mkdir, copy, stat, HTTP or browser tool.
   - **And `run_command` cannot stand in for them.** It matches against `allowed_commands`, which the desktop derives from project-declared tasks minus long-running ones. `del x` and `mv a b` are not `package.json` scripts, so they are refused — the existing test pins `rm -rf /` → "not authorized". So the Agent's only way to "delete" a file was to overwrite it with an empty string, which leaves the file there and reads as an edit. That is the gap worth closing first, and it is genuinely a cross-platform one: `rm -rf` and `del /q` are not the same command, and that is our problem to absorb, not the model's to guess.
   - `workspace_delete_file`, behind the **same** `allow_write` gate as writing. Not a separate toggle: whole-file overwrite can already destroy the contents, so a second switch would only imply that writing is safer than it is. Same boundary too — `resolve_for_agent_write`, which rejects paths outside the workspace plus `.git/`, `.agent-ide/`, `node_modules/` and credential files. A deletion must not be permitted anywhere a write is not.
   - **Directories are refused.** Recursive delete has a completely different blast radius, and the undo record is a list of file→content pairs; rebuilding a tree needs machinery that does not exist. Half-undoable authority is worse than none.
   - The tool reads the file *before* removing it and fails if it cannot: without those bytes there is no undoable deletion, so refusing is the only honest outcome.
   - **The undo side needed nothing.** `restore_snapshots` already writes `previous` back when it is `Some` and removes the file when it is `None`, so a snapshot carrying the deleted content recreates the file exactly. That was the fact this design hinged on and I checked it before writing anything.
   - **What did need adding is the label.** `AgentFileWrite.removed` exists because `updated == ""` cannot distinguish "deleted" from "emptied", and the review card must not say the wrong one — a user who reads "emptied" thinks the file is still there. `record_tool_writes` maps it to `operation: "delete"`, which `DiffView` already renders verbatim, so the frontend needed no change. The per-file merge takes the **last** operation: write-then-delete is a delete, and one of the two new tests exists for exactly that, because keeping the first would report a destructive net effect as an ordinary edit.
   - No filesystem-level test for `delete_file_tool` itself; its boundary is the same `resolve_for_agent_write` that `write_file_tool` uses and that is already covered. The two new tests sit on the labelling and the merge, which is where the new logic is.
   - **Not built, and these are the honest answers to the rest of the request:**
     - **Move / rename.** Wants the same vocabulary work plus a second path on one `FileDiff`, which has a single `file` field — "moved A → B" is not expressible today. Next step, and now cheap because the `removed` precedent is set.
     - **System operation tools.** Nothing exists and nothing is half-built. This is an authority question before it is a coding one: "run any OS command" is exactly what the derived allow-list refuses on purpose. It needs its own decision about what the user is consenting to, not an implementation.
     - **Browser tools.** MCP is already the extension point — `services/mcp` with a configurable server list, `McpToolPolicy`, and an `McpPanel` UI — so a browser or filesystem MCP server can be plugged in today without new backend code. Building a browser automation stack in-tree would be the largest feature in this repo and would put page content into prompts; MCP is the right seam for it.
   - 287 → 289 tests.

38. **Every file operation collapsed the tree (fixed 2026-09-13)**
   - Asked whether the experience can stand next to VS Code. This was the most visible thing standing in the way, and it fired on *every* create, delete, rename and paste: each of those bumps `explorerKey`, `loadRoot` replaced `rootData` with a fresh batch of top-level nodes carrying `childrenLoaded: false`, and every expanded subtree vanished. react-arborist keeps expansion state internally, keyed by node id, so it still believed those folders were open — the user saw **an open folder with nothing in it** and had to collapse and expand twice to get the contents back. No mainstream editor does this, and it is the kind of defect that makes an otherwise working tool feel unfinished.
   - `loadRoot` now collects the expanded directory paths before reloading, re-lists them, and splices the results back in. **Re-listing rather than caching the old subtrees is the point:** the operation that triggered the reload is often a file created *inside* one of those directories, so restoring the previous children would hide the new file — trading a visible bug for a quiet one.
   - The two decisions worth keeping: a directory absent from the reload map stays *unexpanded* rather than getting an empty `children` array, because "empty folder" and "not expanded yet" must not render the same; and a per-directory `list_directory` failure is swallowed, because the directory may be the one that was just deleted and one missing subtree must not fail the whole refresh.
   - `loadRoot` reads the previous tree through a ref, not from its dependency array. Depending on `rootData` would give it a new identity on every tree change, and the mount effect depends on `loadRoot` — that is an infinite reload loop, so the ref is load-bearing and says so in a comment.
   - **`Explorer.tsx` has its first tests.** The two decisions above are pure functions in `explorerTree.ts` (`loadedDirectoryPaths`, `attachLoadedChildren`) precisely so they could be tested without the Tauri bridge, `ResizeObserver` and react-arborist that the component needs to render — the same reason `monacoGlobals.ts` was split out of `EditorContainer`. Five tests, including the nested case and the "nothing to reattach" case. 112 → 117 frontend tests.
   - Still open from 35, and now the honest ranking for closing the experience gap: no move/rename in the tree at all (the clipboard only knows `copy`, and `operation` is written but never read); paste always renames even without a collision; name dialogs validate only non-empty, so `../x` reaches `joinPath` intact. Beyond the tree, the structural gaps are split editors, multi-root workspaces and multiple Agent sessions — each a real feature rather than a polish item. **The middle two were fixed in 39.**

39. **Paste renamed files that did not need renaming, and the name dialogs accepted paths (fixed 2026-09-13)**
   - Two of the three tree defects listed in 38, both small, both the kind of thing that separates a tool that works from one that feels finished.
   - **Paste.** `copyNameCandidates` started at `foo Copy.ts`, so pasting `foo.ts` into a folder that had no `foo.ts` still produced `foo Copy.ts` and the user had to rename it back. Every file manager keeps the name when it is free. The fix is one entry: put the **original name first** in the candidate list. The existing retry loop already advances on the backend's "destination already exists", so keeping the name when free and falling back to `foo Copy.ts` when taken both come out of the same mechanism — no new branching.
   - **Name dialogs.** They checked non-empty and nothing else, so the string went straight into `joinPath`: `a/b/c` silently created a nested path and `../x` landed in the parent directory. `validateEntryName` now rejects path separators, `.` and `..`, `:` (NTFS stream syntax), `" < > | ? *`, control characters, a trailing dot (Windows drops it silently, so the name you get is not the name you typed), and Windows device names with or without an extension.
   - **This is not a security fix and is not written up as one.** `resolve_for_write` still clamps every path to the workspace, so `../x` could not escape. What it could do is put the file somewhere the user did not intend with no indication of why — a correctness and legibility problem, which is the honest framing.
   - **Deliberately not checked: duplicate names.** The backend already refuses an existing destination with a clear message, and a front-end case-insensitive check would be wrong on Linux, where `README.md` and `readme.md` are two files. The validator only covers cases with no backstop.
   - The error appears on submit rather than disabling the button, because a disabled button with no explanation is the worse failure — the user cannot tell which character is the problem.
   - Both functions live in `explorerTree.ts` alongside the reload helpers, for the same reason: they are the decisions, and they are testable without the Tauri bridge. Nine more tests, 117 → 126.

40. **Move: the last tree operation that did not exist (2026-09-13)**
   - The clipboard type admitted exactly one value — `operation: "copy"` — and that field was **never read**. So this was not a half-built feature, it was a field that had been reserved and forgotten, and the tree had no way to move anything: rename could change a name in place, nothing could change a directory.
   - **The backend already supported it.** `rename_path` resolves both sides, refuses an existing target, creates the destination's parent and calls `fs::rename`, which is a move when the parent differs. So this turned out to be a frontend feature plus one guard, not a new command — worth checking before designing, because the obvious plan was to add a `move_path` command that would have duplicated `rename_path` exactly.
   - Cut is now a context-menu item, the clipboard bar says "Cut" instead of "Copied", paste branches on the operation, and **the clipboard clears after a cut-paste** — leaving it would let a second paste chase a source that no longer exists.
   - `resolveMoveDestination` refuses three cases that the backend would also fail, but incomprehensibly: moving an item onto itself, moving a directory into its own subtree (`fs::rename` returns a bare EINVAL), and moving something into the folder it is already in (the backend says "target already exists", which reads like a name collision when in fact nothing needs doing). Separators are normalised before comparing because `list_directory` returns `\` on Windows while `joinPath` emits `/`; the comparison stays **case-sensitive**, since a case-insensitive one would refuse a legal move on Linux where `A` and `a` are two directories.
   - `rename_path` gained the same containment guard as `copy_path` (Known Issues 35). `fs::rename` refuses this itself, so the guard buys a readable message rather than a behaviour change — and it makes the two commands agree, which is the part worth having. Two tests, including the `ab`-is-not-inside-`a` case that a string-prefix comparison would get wrong.
   - Also fixed here, a latent trap in the same function: paste resolved its target as `targetNode?.isDir ? targetNode.path : workspacePath`, so a file node fell back to the **workspace root** rather than that file's folder. Unreachable through the menu today because Paste is only offered on directories, but it would have put a pasted file somewhere nobody pointed at. It now uses the file's parent.
   - Not addressed, and not claimed: destinations are still built with `/` separators on Windows (Known Issues 35). The backend resolves them, so this is cosmetic until something string-compares a destination against a `list_directory` path.
   - Rust 289 → 291, frontend 126 → 131. With Known Issues 38, a cut-paste refreshes the tree **and keeps the folders you had open**, which is the pair actually needed to make this feel like a file manager.

41. **"LLM ready" meant "a profile exists", not "the endpoint answers" (2026-09-13)**
   - Reported as *the configured / connected status indicators are gone*. Two thirds of that report do not hold, and the write-up says so rather than quietly fixing something adjacent:
     - The **configured** indicator was never removed. `StatusBar.tsx` has rendered a dot plus "LLM ready" / "LLM not configured" the whole time, and `SettingsPanel.tsx` still shows the `LLM Service Configured` card.
     - What commit `216db9d` deleted was the **local-model file status** row (`Loaded` / `Ready to load` / `Model file not found` plus Refresh/Load/Unload). That was GGUF file presence and in-process engine state, and it was dead — `create_local_model_engine` returned an unconditional `Err` (Known Issues 30), so those buttons could not succeed.
     - A **connected** indicator never existed as state. `testLlmConnection` returned a `Promise<string>` and stored nothing; the result became a transient toast in the Settings panel and was gone on the next render.
   - The real defect the report points at: `llmConfigured` answers "is a profile saved", and the UI presented that as readiness. A profile with a typo'd endpoint, an expired key or a model name the provider does not serve produced a **green dot saying "LLM ready"** — false confidence, which this project's conventions single out as worse than no indicator.
   - Fix: `llmConnection: LlmConnectionState` in the store, three states — `unknown` / `ok` / `failed` — recorded by `testLlmConnection` on **both** paths. Failure is recorded *and* rethrown, so the Settings panel still shows its message while the status bar stops claiming readiness.
   - **The result is stored with the target it was measured against**, a fingerprint of `id|endpoint|model|api_key_masked` for the profile the test actually hits (`chatProfileId`, falling back to the active one). Without it, verifying a profile and then editing its endpoint leaves a green dot vouching for an address nothing ever reached. The key's mask is in the fingerprint because an expired key is exactly the "configured but unreachable" case, and rotating one changes no other field.
   - **The comparison happens at render, not on write.** The first version of this reset `llmConnection` to `unknown` inside a `commitLlmTarget` helper that every config writer called. My own audit of that commit found the hole immediately: `testLlmConnection` computes its target *before* an `await` that takes seconds (the probe is a real completion plus a deliberate 500 ms sleep), and its `set` was unconditional — switch the chat profile mid-test and the arriving `ok` overwrote the freshly-invalidated state, producing the same false green the change existed to remove. Keeping every writer honest is a discipline; comparing once at the point of display is a property. `llmIndicator` / `llmConnectionIndicator` / `llmConnectionCheckedAt` now take the current fingerprint and downgrade to `unknown` when it does not match — and they suppress the stale `detail` and timestamp too, since those describe a different endpoint. This is the same lesson as the panel-height clamp (Known Issues 36): store the measurement, derive the presentation.
   - The reset is deliberately **not** "any config write invalidates it": a periodic `fetchLlmConfig` that returns identical values keeps the verified state, because nothing about the target changed. That is the property the test asserts, rather than "reset was called".
   - `unknown` renders amber and says `LLM configured` — configured is what is actually known. The status bar text distinguishes all three (`LLM connected` / `LLM configured` / `LLM unreachable`), because a colour alone is unreadable to a colour-blind user, the same reasoning as the E/W/I letters next to the problem counts. The Settings card's chrome follows the same tone: it used to be green with a green filled bullet whenever a profile existed, which would have put a green frame around a red `Failed` row — the deleted status-bar dot growing back three panels over.
   - Judgement lives in `stores/llmConnection.ts` (pure, no store or React import) and the components only map a tone to a class. `llmIndicator` and `llmConnectionIndicator` share one wording table so the status bar and the Settings card cannot drift into telling different stories about the same state.
   - Two more defects the audit surfaced, both older than this change and both fixed here:
     - `chatProfileId` was only ever filled when null, never validated against the profile list, so deleting the profile selected for chat left a dead id in the store. The backend resolves an unknown id by **silently falling back to `profiles.first()`** (`llm_profiles.rs`), so the frontend named one target while every prompt and every connection test went to another. `resolveChatProfileId` now drops an id that is no longer in the list.
     - `updateLlmConfig` had **no callers anywhere** in `src/`, and it wrote `llmEndpoint` / `llmModel` without refreshing `llmProfiles` — leaving the store internally inconsistent, and its stale profile entry would have kept the fingerprint from changing. Deleted rather than repaired, per the no-dead-code convention.
   - Not done, and not implied: nothing tests connectivity on its own. The state says when it was last checked and by whose action; it never claims to be live. A background poll would need a rate limit and a cost story (the test is a real completion request), so it is a separate decision.
   - Frontend 131 → 154: 12 for the pure module (including "a stale target hides the detail, not just the colour"), 6 in the store (both outcomes recorded, stale target no longer reads as verified, identical refresh still does, a deleted chat profile is dropped), 5 rendering the states in `StatusBar`.

42. **A deleted file's contents were stored, and shown as one blank line (2026-09-13)**
   - Found while designing a `move_file` tool for the Agent, by reading how a delete renders today. `HunkBlock` in `DiffView.tsx` had three branches — new file, side-by-side, and a fallback that splits `hunk.content` — and the shape "old content, no new content" fell through into the fallback. For a direct tool write `content` is the empty string (`record_tool_writes` sets it), so a delete card rendered **one empty row**.
   - Nothing was lost: the backend stores the content in `hunks[0].original` and the undo checkpoint restores from it, both covered by existing tests. What broke was the part this product exists for — seeing what the Agent did — and it broke for the least reversible operation, where the user most needs to look before deciding.
   - Deleted lines now render red under `- Deleted file`. A hunk that merely **empties** a file gets `- All content removed`, because the file still exists and the other banner would be a different false claim. The two are told apart by the diff's own `provenance.operation`, which is why `HunkBlock` now receives it; hunk-level provenance does not carry the operation.
   - `hunkKind` moved to `diffPresentation.ts`, pure and tested (6 tests), for the reason `explorerTree.ts` and `monacoGlobals.ts` exist: the decision was untestable inside a component that needs the agent store, the editor store and the problem store to render at all. Frontend 154 → 160.
   - The `move_file` design work that surfaced this is recorded but not implemented: `FileDiff` has a single `file` field, so "moved A → B" is inexpressible, and a delete+create pair would produce two unrelated cards whose hardcoded rationales both name the wrong tool. The undo side needs nothing new — `restore_snapshots` already means "write `previous` back, or remove the path when it is `None`" — but two records for one rename are not atomic, and on a case-insensitive filesystem `Foo.ts` → `foo.ts` would produce two records pointing at one file, whose undo restores the source and then deletes it. **Implemented in 43.**

43. **The Agent can move a file, and the review entry says where it came from (2026-09-13)**
   - The tree could move files since 40; the Agent could not. `workspace_run_command` only accepts the project's own declared checks, so `mv` / `move` were unreachable — and those two are not the same command, which is exactly the cross-platform difference this IDE should absorb rather than make the model guess at.
   - **Not delete+create.** That pairing was the obvious shortcut and it is wrong in three separate ways: two review cards with no link between them, hardcoded rationales that would name `workspace_delete_file` and `workspace_write_file` for a call to neither, and no atomicity — a failed second step leaves the content only in the log. On a case-insensitive filesystem `Foo.ts` → `foo.ts` would even produce two records pointing at one file, and undoing them in order restores the source and then deletes it. So: one `AgentFileWrite` carrying `moved_from`, and one `fs::rename`.
   - **`FileDiff` still has one `file`**, so the other half of the fact goes in `provenance.moved_from` (`movedFrom` over IPC) with `operation: "move"`. `create` was rejected as the label: it reads as "content was just written here" and leaves the source's disappearance unexplained.
   - **Undo is a move back, not a content rewrite.** `FileSnapshot` gained `move_back_to`; `restore_snapshots` renames and only then, if `previous` is present, writes content. Three consequences, each one a reason for the choice: the file keeps its identity rather than being re-created from bytes; **binary files can be moved at all**, where `workspace_delete_file` is stuck with UTF-8 because its undo *is* the content; and a file that was edited after the move still restores to its content as of the move, because the merge in `record_tool_writes` now adopts the following write's `previous` when a moved entry has none.
   - Refusals, each because the alternative is unrecoverable or unreadable: an existing destination is never overwritten (that content has no record anywhere), directories are out (same reason as deletion), and both paths go through `resolve_for_agent_write` so a credential file can be neither source nor destination. A case-only rename is allowed on Windows and macOS and treated as an existing destination on Linux, where `foo.ts` and `Foo.ts` really are two files.
   - Authority: `allow_write` **and** `allow_create`, because a move both removes a path and creates one. Missing either, the tool is neither advertised nor claimed, and a direct call still fails — the pattern `write_file_tool` follows and `delete_file_tool` does not.
   - A pure move has no content diff, so its card is the banner `→ Moved from <path>` and nothing else; rendering the file as newly added would misrepresent an unchanged file. Rust 291 → 298, frontend 160 → 166. **Three of the decisions above were wrong and are corrected in 44** — read that entry before trusting this one on undo ordering, on the case-only rename, or on the merge rule.

44. **The move tool's undo was only correct for one move (fixed 2026-09-13)**
   - Self-audit of 43 found three sequences where undo silently destroyed a file and reported success. All of them come from one line: `restore_snapshots` replayed a batch **front to back**, while the inverse of a sequence of renames is the reversed sequence of inverse renames.
     - `move A→B`, then write a new `A`: forwards, the move-back overwrote the new `A` (correct so far), and then the create's own undo — `remove_file(A)` — deleted it. Both entries were reported as restored and the original `A` existed nowhere, because a move records `previous: None` by design. **This is exactly the failure 43 gives as the reason to reject delete+create**, reintroduced through a different door.
     - `move A→B`, `move B→C`: forwards, the first rename fails (nothing at `B`) and the second lands the file at `B`. Half-restored, and reported as one failure and one success.
     - `move A→B`, `move C→A`: forwards, the first move-back overwrites `A` — which by then holds `C`'s content — destroying it with no snapshot anywhere; the second then renames that file to `C`. Zero failures reported.
   - Fix: iterate in reverse. Per-file content snapshots are order-independent, so nothing else changes, and all four sequences (including `write A`, then `move A→B`, which used to leave an Agent-authored file behind) now return the workspace to its pre-run state. Four new tests, one per sequence.
   - `restore_move` also **refuses to overwrite** whatever sits at the source path instead of letting `fs::rename` replace it. After a reverse replay the source should be free; if it is not, the world has changed since the run and an honest failure beats a silent one.
   - **The merge rule from 43 was wrong.** `record_tool_writes` merges by file path, so `delete B` followed by `move A→B` collapsed into one entry that dropped `moved_from` — undo restored `B`'s old content and never brought `A` back, and the card read `edit` with the rationale "Written directly by the Agent through `workspace_write_file`", a tool that was never called. The merge key is now `(file, is_a_move)`, so a move never merges with a non-move: two honest cards, two correct snapshots, and the reverse replay orders them correctly. This also deletes 43's "adopt the next write's `previous`" special case — a move followed by an edit is now two entries, which is both simpler and more accurate.
   - **The case-only rename claim in 43 was false.** `resolve_for_write` canonicalizes existing paths, and Windows returns the on-disk casing, so `foo.ts` → `Foo.ts` resolves to one path and was rejected as "the same path" long before the case branch ran. Worse, where that branch *was* reachable it defeated the overwrite guard: case sensitivity is a **per-directory** property (`fsutil file setCaseSensitiveInfo`, case-sensitive APFS volumes), not a property of the compile target, so on such a directory `Foo.ts` and `foo.ts` are two real files and the branch let `fs::rename` replace one of them with nothing recorded. Deleted. A case-only rename now needs an intermediate name, and the same-path error says so instead of inviting a retry of the identical call.
   - A destination directory created for the move is removed again when the rename then fails (cross-device, permissions, a Windows sharing violation), so a refused move leaves no trace. 43's flat "no half-done state" was true of the file and false of the tree.
   - Not fixed, recorded instead: after an undo, a tool-write card returns to `pending` and its Apply can only fail — a move card has no content to apply (`build_updated_content` rejects an empty `original`), and a delete card would write an empty file rather than delete. It fails loudly rather than corrupting anything, but the right answer is that tool-write records should not become reviewable proposals at all. That is a change to the review model, not to this tool.
   - Rust 298 → 303.

45. **An undone tool-write record was offered back as a proposal (2026-09-13)**
   - Recorded as open at the end of 44, fixed here. `undo_last_apply` cleared every applied hunk status and recomputed the diff status, so a tool-write card came back as `pending` with Apply and Reject buttons. Applying one is not a no-op: a delete record's hunk is `content → ""`, so it takes the content-replacement path and writes a **0-byte file instead of deleting it**; a move record has no content at all and only fails. The user's mental model — "Apply puts the deletion back" — and the code disagree, and the code wins.
   - The distinction the review area never had: **a tool-write card is a record of something that already happened, not a proposal.** `record_tool_writes` has always stamped `protocol: "workspace_tool"`, and until now **nothing read it** — the only thing separating "already on disk" from "waiting for you" was the `applied` status itself, which is exactly what undo destroyed.
   - Undone records now land in a terminal status, `reverted`: still listed, because undoing a change does not mean it never happened, but with no buttons and no place in any pending count. Model-proposed diffs still return to `pending`, which is correct for them — they *are* proposals, and undo just makes them undecided again. The existing test that asserts that (`undo_restores_the_file_and_lets_the_diff_be_applied_again`) is the regression guard.
   - Also fixed while adding the status: `refresh_review_state` had its own inline copy of the three reviewable statuses instead of calling `is_reviewable_diff_status`. Two lists that must agree, one of which is easy to miss when adding a status — the audit of 43 flagged exactly this shape of duplication, and it would have made "no open work" and "cannot be applied" disagree.
   - The frontend needed the status in `DiffEntry["status"]` and `DiffHunk["status"]`, a banner, and nothing else: `REVIEWABLE_DIFF_STATUSES` in both `useAgentStore.ts` and `agentExperience.ts` are allow-lists, so an unknown value is already treated as not-reviewable. Rust 303 → 304, frontend 166 → 167.

46. **Undo picked cards by file path, so it could revert the wrong one (2026-09-13)**
   - Self-audit of 45. `undo_last_apply` selected cards with `restored.contains(&diff.file)` — a list of **file names**, not of the cards the popped checkpoint actually applied. One file can easily carry two cards: the Agent tool-writes `a.ts` in one run and writes it again in the next, or writes it and the user later applies a model diff for the same file. Undoing once then rewrote **both** cards.
   - Before 45 that was a recoverable wrong state (a card said `pending` when its change was still on disk). With a terminal `reverted` it became a permanent false statement: the older card claims "you undid this" forever, while its change is exactly what is on disk. The new status made a pre-existing sloppiness load-bearing — worth stating plainly, because it is the second time in this pair of entries that adding a stricter state exposed an older approximation.
   - Fix: `ApplyCheckpoint` carries `diff_ids`, filled at every push site (`record_tool_writes` with the cards it created, `apply_diff` / `apply_diff_hunk` with the one card, `Auto-apply` with the ids the batch actually applied), and undo matches on id **and** requires the file to have restored successfully. No migration needed: the undo stack is in-memory only.
   - Two smaller things the audit found, both fixed here: `status_from_hunks` had no `reverted` arm, so it would have turned an all-reverted card back into `pending` — correct today only because every caller is gated, which is the kind of "correct by luck" that breaks on the next caller. And `getHunkStatusCounts` in `DiffView.tsx` initialises one counter per status behind an `as Record<...>` assertion; without a `reverted: 0` entry the increment is `undefined + 1` → `NaN`, invisible to `tsc` for exactly the reason AGENTS.md warns about that assertion.
   - Recorded, not fixed: the same over-matching still affects **model** diffs (an older applied card returns to `pending` when a later checkpoint for the same file is undone). It is now id-filtered too, so the case is gone in practice; what remains is that a card whose change is still on disk has no state of its own — that is the `applied`-versus-disk-truth question, a bigger design item than this fix. Rust 304 → 305.

47. **The file tree's rows were draggable and dropping did nothing (2026-09-13)**
   - Not a missing feature, an inert affordance — the thing this project's conventions call out as worse than an absent control. Evidence, not inference: react-arborist marks every row draggable unless `disableDrag` is set (`interfaces/tree-api.js`: `const check = this.props.disableDrag || (() => false)`), and its drop hook ends in `safeRun(tree.props.onMove, …)`, which no-ops when the handler is absent (`dnd/drop-hook.js`). The tree had no `onMove`. So the row lifted, the drop indicator appeared, and nothing happened.
   - `onMove` now moves through the same path as cut-paste: `resolveMoveDestination` for the destination and its three refusals (onto itself, into its own subtree, into the folder it is already in — each with a readable reason instead of the backend's bare EINVAL), then `renamePath`.
   - Multi-select drags are handled properly rather than by taking `dragIds[0]`: arborist allows multi-select by default, so dropping three files while handling one would silently lose two. They move **sequentially**, because concurrent renames race the file-watcher refresh, and a failure has to be able to name which item failed. Failures take precedence in the toast — a success the user can already see in the tree, a refusal they cannot.
   - `findNodeById` (recursive, in `explorerTree.ts`, tested) exists because the drop handler receives ids, not nodes, and the destination needs the real path plus its `isDir` flag. Scanning only the top level would fail to find a second-level folder and then do nothing — the same silent no-op, one layer down. Frontend 167 → 169.

48. **The tree had no keyboard shortcuts (2026-09-13)**
   - Every file operation existed only behind a right-click. Arrow keys and Enter worked (react-arborist's own bindings plus the `onActivate` fix from an earlier entry), but F2, Delete and Ctrl+C/X/V did nothing — the keys a user coming from any file manager tries first.
   - Wired to the row that has focus, tracked in a **ref** rather than state: `onFocus` fires on every arrow-key move, and putting that in state re-renders the whole tree for a fact only a keystroke handler reads.
   - The decision lives in `explorerShortcut` (pure, 3 tests) because the hard part is not the mapping but the **combinations to leave alone**: anything with Alt belongs to the OS and window manager, `Ctrl+Shift+C` is a different command in VS Code and quietly treating it as Copy is worse than not supporting it, and `Shift+Delete` is not "delete" here. Backspace counts as delete because Mac keyboards have no Delete key; that is safe to claim because react-arborist only consumes Backspace when an `onDelete` handler is passed, and this tree passes none.
   - Paste is the one action that works with no row focused — it targets the workspace root, matching what right-clicking blank space already does. The name dialog short-circuits the whole handler: without that, typing a name would also delete the file behind the dialog. Frontend 169 → 172. **48's implementation was wrong in three ways; see 49.**

49. **The new shortcuts cut the wrong file (2026-09-14)**
   - Self-audit of 48, and the worst of the three findings is destructive. `Ctrl+X` reaches react-arborist's container **first** (my handler was on an ancestor, bubble phase). Arborist does not recognise `x`, so it falls into its type-ahead branch: `focusSearchTerm += e.key` then `tree.focus(firstVisibleNodeStartingWithX)`. That fires `onFocus`, which is where 48 recorded "the focused row". By the time my handler ran, the recorded row was **a different file** — in this repo, pressing `Ctrl+V` with `vite.config.ts` visible moves focus there, so the paste lands in the wrong directory, and `Ctrl+X` cuts a file the user never selected.
   - Fixed at the root, twice over: the handler moved to the **capture phase** and calls `stopPropagation` on the keys it claims, so arborist never sees them and focus does not move; and the target now comes from `TreeApi.focusedNode` instead of a second copy of the same state. One source of truth, no ordering dependency.
   - That also fixed a second finding: `TreeApi.onFocus()` — the path taken when focus *enters* the tree by click or Tab — dispatches focus without calling the `onFocus` prop. So the ref stayed `null` while a row visibly had the focus ring: F2 and Delete did nothing, and paste silently targeted the workspace root. Reading the api cannot go stale that way.
   - Third: keyboard delete worked once and then the whole binding went dead. Deleting bumps the tree's reload key, the focused row unmounts, and nothing held focus afterwards (the viewport div has no `tabIndex`), so later keystrokes went to `body`. Focus now moves to the next sibling or parent **before** the delete — which is exactly what arborist's own Backspace branch does, and the hand-rolled binding had dropped that step.
   - Two drag findings from the same audit, both fixed: dragging a folder together with something inside it moved the folder, then failed on the child at its old path and reported a raw backend error while hiding the success — `withoutDraggedDescendants` now drops nodes another dragged folder already carries. And a drop into the folder a file already lives in raised an error, even though arborist's insertion-line cursor is what invited the gesture; ordering is not supported, so that drop is now a silent no-op. `resolveMoveDestination` returns a `reason` for this instead of the caller matching on error text, which would break on the next wording change.
   - Also: the toast now reports both sides (`Moved 4; <first failure> (+1 more)`), because "any failure hides the successes" reads as a total failure when four of five files moved. And `handleMove`'s `parent.isDir ? … : parentOf(…)` branch was dead — arborist's `compute-drop` only ever names a folder or the root — so it is gone rather than left as decoration.
   - Frontend 172 → 175.

50. **The context panel's token count did not include the IDE runtime block (2026-09-14)**
   - Found while mapping what the user can actually see about their context, before deciding what to build next. `withIdeRuntimeContext` on the frontend appended the problems list, terminal output, failed-check output and warn/error log lines **to the prompt string**. Everything downstream therefore missed it: `estimate_agent_context` runs the real assembly and so reported a number that excluded up to ~10 000 characters (20 problems, 4 000 chars of terminal, 8 log lines, 4 000 chars of failed output), and the budget allocator could not trim it because it never saw it. The panel that exists to answer "what am I sending" was systematically wrong in the one place where the answer is largest.
   - Fix: `ide_runtime` is now an `AgentContext` field and a context section, with `SendPromptRequest.ideRuntime` / `EstimateContextRequest.ideRuntime` carrying it. The frontend passes the same string to both, from one memoised options object, so the estimate and the real prompt cannot diverge — the previous arrangement made divergence the default.
   - Priority 4, quota 0.15: ahead of the conversation digest and the file body because the user pressed "Fix with Agent" *because of* those problems, but a smaller share than the file body, which is the thing being changed. It participates in the same two-pass allocation as everything else, so a tiny budget trims or drops it instead of letting it push the real work out.
   - The pipeline path (`run_agent_step`) passes `None` deliberately: those steps are the Agent working through its own plan, and the IDE's problem panel at that instant is not the step's input.
   - `withIdeRuntimeContext` is deleted rather than kept as a wrapper — one composed call site each in `ChatView` and `useFixWithAgent` now pass the section explicitly. The "Fix with Agent" chat bubble also stops echoing the whole runtime blob: the request is what the user wrote, and the attached evidence is visible in the context panel with its size.
   - Rust 305 → 306.

51. **Three follow-ups the audit of 50 found, all in the same commit's blast radius (2026-09-14)**
   - **An empty runtime block still became a section.** `buildIdeRuntimeContext` returns `""` when there is nothing to report *or* when all four toggles are off, and `"" ?? null` is `""`, so Rust received `Some("")` and pushed a section: the panel showed a green `IDE runtime  8 tok` row and the model got a bare `=== IDE Runtime Context ===` header. "Turned off" and "on but nothing to report" became indistinguishable **in the panel whose only job is to say what is being sent**. Every other optional section already guarded on emptiness; this one now does too, on the Rust side so no caller can reintroduce it.
   - **The estimate did not refresh when the problems or logs actually changed.** The effect's dependencies were the four toggles, but the builder reads the problem / log / task / terminal stores imperatively. So `npm test` failing and filling the panel with 20 problems moved nothing on screen until something unrelated re-ran the effect — understated at exactly the moment the user is about to press "Fix with Agent". Fixed by memoising the *string* with those store slices as dependencies; the existing 250 ms debounce absorbs the churn.
   - **"Fix with Agent" ignored the user's toggles.** It called `buildIdeRuntimeContext()` with no options, i.e. hardcoded defaults: someone who turned Terminal and Logs off in Chat still shipped both, and that path does not show the context panel. The toggles, their persistence and the mapping to the four runtime flags moved out of `ChatView` into `utils/chatContextOptions.ts` so both paths read one source. This is the second time in two entries that a **second copy of the same state** was the defect — first the connection target, now the context toggles.
   - Also: `ideRuntime` was accepted by `AgentStepRunParams` and silently dropped (the backend passes `None` for pipeline steps on purpose), which is the type-level version of an inert control — `Omit`ted now. And `docs/agent_ide_design.md` still listed the old section order; `SECURITY.md` and the test counts had been updated but that one was missed, which is exactly the doc-drift the conventions warn about.
   - Recorded, not fixed: `ide_runtime` is **not** suppressed when the active file is a credential file, while `selection` and `active_file_content` are. Terminal output and problem messages can quote a `.env`-driven failure, so this is a new outbound channel the existing guard does not cover. It needs its own decision (suppress the section, or scrub the credential values), not a reflex.
   - Rust 306 → 307, frontend 175 → 179.

52. **What Codex's computer use actually does, and which parts are worth copying (2026-09-14)**
   - Read from a working install on this machine (`~/.codex/config.toml`, the `openai-bundled` plugin caches, and the `@oai/sky` runtime under `AppData/Local/OpenAI/Codex/runtimes/cua_node`) rather than from documentation. Recorded here because Agent IDE has an open request for system-operation and browser tools, and this is the closest thing to a reference implementation available.
   - **The model is not given click/type tools.** It is given a JavaScript runtime (`node_repl`) and writes code against `sky` — `list_windows`, `get_window_state`, `click`, `type_text`, `press_key`, `scroll`, `drag`, `set_value`, `launch_app`. The consequence for us: the tool-count problem disappears, but the authority problem gets worse, because "run this JS" cannot be gated per action.
   - **Every input method takes a `window` and window-relative coordinates.** Nothing addresses the screen. That single choice makes "act on the wrong window" a type error rather than a race, and it is why the helper can reject an action as `outside window bounds`.
   - **Screenshots carry an id, and actions may cite it.** `click` accepts `screenshotId`; the helper rejects `unknown screenshotId`. So a click derived from a stale observation fails loudly instead of landing somewhere else — the same discipline as our diff `base_hash` staleness check, applied to pixels.
   - **The turn is the unit of authority.** Codex's `notify` hook is the computer-use binary itself, invoked as `codex-computer-use.exe turn-ended`; it signals a named Win32 event and drops a zero-byte marker file per turn under `~/.codex/computer-use-turn-ended/<thread>/`. Esc writes an interrupt marker that the JS side checks **before sending** the next request, kills the child and latches the turn. That is a real kill switch, not a UI hint — and it is the piece we would need first, because our `CancelRegistry` currently protects model calls, not side effects on the OS.
   - **Approval is per application, once.** The helper can answer `approvalRequest` instead of a result, producing "Allow Codex to use \<app\>?" with `persist: ["session", "always"]`, and the grant travels as `x-oai-cua-approved-app`. Per-action safety is **instructions only**: a four-tier confirmation taxonomy plus a blocklist in the skill (no terminals, no auth dialogs, no password managers, never press `Meta`/`Win`). We should not mistake that for enforcement; the enforced parts are the app grant, the window scoping, the bounds/staleness checks and the Esc latch.
   - **The browser is a separate stack from computer use, and the better one.** Backends are `iab` (the app's own browser) and `chrome` via a **Chrome extension plus native messaging** (`chrome-native-hosts.json` names `com.openai.codexextension` and an `extension-host.exe`), with CDP used *inside* the extension (`chrome.debugger`) and raw CDP scoped to the tab's origin. Its own instructions say to prefer this over computer use for anything in a browser. It also has what computer use lacks: named permissions (`browser-origin-access`, `full-cdp`, `file-download`, …), per-session origin allowlists (`~/.codex/browser/sessions/<id>.toml`), and `browser_management_get_audit_trail` returning before-snapshots of tabs, windows and bookmarks.
   - **The asymmetry is the lesson for us.** The browser stack is auditable and origin-scoped; the OS stack has no audit trail at all. For a product whose stated reason to exist is that every change is visible and undoable, an OS-level tool surface would have to carry its own record (what was clicked, in which window, with which screenshot as evidence) before it could ship — and OS actions are mostly *not* undoable, which is a harder problem than file writes. A browser-only surface, origin-scoped and logged, is the honest first step; MCP remains the seam.
   - Also observed, and worth knowing before trusting any of it: on this machine the `chrome` backend is advertised but its `extension-host` and native-messaging manifest directory do not exist, and the third-party `brosdk-mcp` skill (a Go MCP server bundling its own Chrome) last exited with an error. Availability of these paths cannot be inferred from configuration being present.

53. **Browser control, step one: the transport and the URL guard (2026-09-14)**
   - `services/browser.rs` drives a Chrome the **user** started with `--remote-debugging-port`, over CDP's **HTTP** endpoints (`/json/list`, `/json/new`, `/json/activate`, `/json/close`). Two Tauri commands are registered: `browser_list_tabs`, `browser_open_url`.
   - HTTP rather than WebSocket on purpose: those four endpoints cover "open a page, see what is open, switch, close", which is the part that is actually missing today (open a preview, open a doc). DOM snapshots, clicking and typing need a CDP **session** over WebSocket, i.e. a new dependency, and they are only worth adding together with the authority and record model below — not before it.
   - Not launching our own browser, also on purpose: a second browser means a second profile, a second set of logins and a second extension set, while the page the user wants to look at is in *their* browser. The cost is that Chrome must have been started with the flag, so the unreachable-endpoint error says exactly that instead of surfacing "connection refused".
   - The endpoint is pinned to `127.0.0.1`. CDP has **no authentication at all**: anything that can reach the port can read every page and its cookies. Making the host configurable would be handing that out over the network.
   - `normalize_target_url` refuses anything but `http`/`https`, and refuses credentials in the URL.
