Each refusal is a case where "clicked it and regretted it" is already too late: `javascript:` executes in the *current* page's origin (session theft), `file:` reads local files outside the workspace, `chrome:`/`about:` reach browser settings, `data:` is the classic phishing carrier with no honest origin in the address bar, and `user:pass@host` both leaks credentials into history and disguises the real host. Control characters are refused because browsers silently strip them, which is how a `javascript:` scheme gets hidden inside a plausible string.
   - **Deliberately not done in this step, and this is the important part:** the Agent cannot reach any of it. A navigation is not undoable, and this product's premise is that every Agent action is visible and undoable. Before an Agent-facing tool exists it needs (a) a per-site authority, not a single "allow browser" bit — the model from Codex's own browser stack, which keeps a per-session origin allowlist; (b) an action record with the same standing as a `FileDiff`, because the record is the only thing we can honestly promise when undo is impossible; (c) a turn-scoped kill switch that stops side effects, not just model calls — our `CancelRegistry` currently protects the latter. Until those three exist, `browser_open_url` stays a user-driven command.
   - Consequence to be honest about: the two commands are registered but have **no UI caller yet**, so this is not a shipped feature — it is the transport plus the guard, with tests (6). The next step is the command-palette entries plus the three items above, in that order. Computer use (mouse, keyboard, screenshots) is not started at all: per Known Issues 52 the OS surface has no audit trail anywhere in the reference implementation either, and OS actions are mostly irreversible, so it needs the record model first. Rust 307 → 313. **The palette entries landed in 54.**

54. **The browser commands have a caller now (2026-09-14)**
   - Two palette entries close the gap 53 flagged: `Browser: Open Selected URL in Chrome` and `Browser: List Chrome Tabs`.
   - Opening takes the **editor selection** rather than a prompt: Tauri's webview does not give a usable `window.prompt`, and a selection is how a user already points at a URL sitting in code or in a log. `selectionUrlOrError` refuses a selection containing whitespace before the call — sending a whole line of code to the backend would come back as "Only http:// and https:// URLs are allowed", which does not tell the user that the problem is their selection. Scheme, credential and control-character checks stay in Rust, where the actual authority is.
   - Results go to the **log panel**, and the panel is switched to it, rather than an alert. The failure message here is actionable ("start Chrome with `--remote-debugging-port=9222`"), so it has to stay on screen long enough to be read and copied — an alert that is dismissed takes the instruction with it.
   - `describeTabs` folds the list into one line with the rest as `+N more` and truncates a long title; both are cases where the raw data makes the summary unreadable. Full titles and URLs go in the log entry's details, so nothing is lost. 8 tests, frontend 179 → 187.
   - Still not done, unchanged from 53: the Agent cannot reach any of this, and computer use is not started. The three prerequisites (per-site authority, an action record with the standing of a `FileDiff`, a turn-scoped kill switch for side effects) are the next work, in that order. **The first two landed in 55.**

55. **The Agent can drive the browser now, behind two gates and a record (2026-09-14)**
   - `workspace_browser_open` and `workspace_browser_tabs` are the first Agent tools whose effect cannot be undone. The two prerequisites 53/54 named are what makes that acceptable: a **per-site authority** (`browser_origins`, matched by `origin_of` + `origin_allowed`) and an **action record kept separately from writes** (`AgentExternalAction`). The record's *standing* is weaker than a `FileDiff`'s — see 56, which is the audit of this commit.
   - **Two gates, and the empty list is not "unconfigured".** `allows_browser()` demands both `allow_browser` and a non-empty allowlist. Treating an empty list as "allow everything" is the failure mode that matters: on the day something goes wrong, a default-open list reads exactly like a list the user approved. Neither gate alone advertises the tools, and `handles()` returns false — so a model naming the tool gets "unknown tool" rather than a tool that always fails, which is the distinction the run-command gate already makes.
   - **The external log is separate from the write log on purpose.** A file write carries `previous` plus an undo checkpoint; a navigation carries neither. Putting them in one list would make "undo" mean two things in the same UI, one of them a lie. `publish_external_actions` emits one action-log entry per run, escalated to `warn` when any attempt was refused or failed, and it says "These cannot be undone." out loud.
   - **Refusals are recorded, not just returned.** "The model tried to open a site it was not authorized for" is one of the few things a user actually wants after the fact, and a message that only exists in the tool's return value vanishes with the conversation. `browser_open_refused` covers both a rejected scheme (before any network call) and a rejected origin.
   - **The allowlist is in the tool description.** A model that cannot see its own authority spends rounds probing sites that are certain to be refused.
   - **Browser use is not part of the preset ladder.** All three presets keep it off with an empty list. `run-commands` exists so the Agent can run the test suite; it must not silently also approve outbound navigation — the two authorities have nothing to do with each other, and the ladder metaphor would imply otherwise. A frontend test asserts this rather than just widening the literal.
   - `invoke()` is synchronous and CDP is HTTP, so `block_on_browser` uses `Handle::try_current()` + `block_in_place`; with no runtime (a unit test calling the tool directly) it says so instead of panicking.
   - Still not started: **computer use** (mouse, keyboard, screenshots). The remaining prerequisite is the third one — a turn-scoped kill switch that stops *side effects*, not just model calls; `CancelRegistry` only covers the latter. Per Known Issues 52 the reference implementation's OS surface has no audit trail either, and OS actions are almost all irreversible, so the record model has to come first there too. Rust 313 → 319; frontend stays 187 (assertions added to existing tests).

56. **Audit of 55: the gates held, the record did not (2026-09-14)**
   - The audit could not find a way to reach `open_url` with an unauthorized origin — no construction site of `WorkspaceToolPermissions` grants browser use by default, and the scheme guard runs before any request. Everything below is about the *record*, which is the only compensating control this feature offers.
   - **A hung endpoint could park a run forever and take the record with it (fixed).** `reqwest` has no default timeout, and these calls run inside `ToolInvoker::invoke`, which is synchronous — the cancel flag is checked only *between* tool calls. So a port that accepts the connection and never answers (wrong service on 9222, Chrome blocked on a dialog, a half-open socket after sleep) blocked a worker thread forever: `Stop` set the UI to Idle while `drive_run` never returned, so `publish_external_actions` never ran and **every** browser action of that run — including the refusals — was lost. Now a 10 s request/connect timeout, and a timeout reports differently from "nobody is listening" so the user is not told to re-add a flag they already added.
   - **Three of six record paths did not exist (fixed).** `browser_tabs_tool` used `?` on its transport error and returned early on the unauthorized branch, and `browser_open_tool` also skipped the record when the switch was off. Since an empty external list makes `publish_external_actions` return early, a failed listing produced a run where nothing at all was reported. There are now `browser_tabs_refused` / `browser_tabs_failed`, and both tools record the "not authorized" attempt. The reason this shipped is instructive: the tests pinned exactly the two paths that worked.
   - **The tabs record said how many, not what (fixed).** The allowlist gates whether the read tool exists, it does not filter results — a user who allowlists only `http://127.0.0.1:1420` still hands over the title and URL of every open page, including magic links and OAuth callbacks carrying tokens. "Listed 7 open page(s)" cannot be replayed, so the record now names the distinct origins disclosed.
   - **Open, and the honest version of a claim I made:** the external record started as an *emitted event*, not state. `take_external_actions` cleared the list and the only thing that happened next was `emit`, so a closed window, a frontend reload or a log rollover lost it, and a restart never showed it again. Calling that "an action record with the standing of a `FileDiff`" was wrong. **Fixed in 57**; 55 has been corrected. (My write-up of 57 also listed a poisoned lock among the causes it fixed — that one is on the *drain* side, upstream of the new list, and remains untouched. It was padding: nothing panics under that mutex.)
   - **Also open:** a redirect leaves the authorized origin (the check is on the URL the model supplied; Chrome then follows 301/302/meta/JS redirects, and the success record names the requested URL, not the final one — `/json/list` could close this loop); and `continue_agent_pipeline` clones the permissions captured at run start, so revoking Browser Use in Settings does not affect a paused-then-continued run. Both are now stated in SECURITY.md instead of being implied away; the same staleness applies to `allowCommandRun` and predates this feature.

57. **Giving the irreversible-action record the standing it was claimed to have (2026-09-14)**
   - `AgentOrchestrator.external_actions: Vec<ExternalActionRecord>` now holds them, `record_external_actions` appends inside one critical section with the drain, and `get_agent_external_actions` reads them back — the same shape `diffs` + `get_agent_diffs` has. "Standing of a `FileDiff`" in this codebase means *on the orchestrator and re-readable*, not on disk: nothing about a run is persisted, and the frontend already reconciles against the backend for exactly that reason.
   - The event is now the **second** copy rather than the only one. That ordering matters: the previous design made the durability of an irreversible action's only record depend on whether a window happened to be listening.
   - Bounded at 200, newest kept, like `undo_stack`. A long run where the model repeatedly probes refused origins would otherwise grow the list without limit and take the renderer with it.
   - Each record carries `run_id` from `current_run_id ?? last_run_id`, so after a reload the list can be told apart from the restored session rather than looking like it belongs to the run now on screen.
   - Frontend: `utils/externalActions.ts` normalizes (never `as` on a Tauri payload — a record missing `detail` is still shown, only an entry with neither `kind` nor `target` is dropped, and a missing `id` gets a positional one so two records cannot collide as React keys). The Diff view lists them above the diffs with **no button** — a control that pretended to reverse a navigation would be the worst possible answer — and counts refusals separately.
   - `reset()` deliberately does *not* clear them. The frontend does not own this list; clearing a copy the backend still holds would produce a record that vanishes and then reappears on the next event. One source of truth, the same lesson as the LLM connection target and the chat context toggles.
   - Rust 319 → 320, frontend 187 → 193.

58. **Audit of 57: the record survived, its attribution did not (2026-09-14)**
   - **A superseded run filed its navigations under the next prompt (fixed).** `record_external_actions` read `current_run_id` at drain time. Sequence: run A opens a page → user hits Stop (`abandon_run` clears `current_run_id`, keeps `last_run_id = A`) → user sends prompt B (`begin_run` sets both to B) → A's task finally returns, `finish_run` no-ops because A no longer holds the claim, but `publish_external_actions` runs anyway and stamps **B**. The field that exists to prevent that confusion was producing it. Now the run id is captured onto `WorkspaceToolPermissions.run_id` at the moment the lease is granted — the only moment it is unambiguous — and the record uses that, with no fallback. `None` stays `None`; a guess would be a silently wrong answer in exactly the situation where the record matters.
   - **`repair_workspace` drained neither log (fixed).** It was the one run exit that called neither publish helper, while `drive_repair` reuses the previous run's tool invoker — which shares the write and external `Arc`s. Anything the repair loop wrote or opened waited for the next prompt, and the next prompt installs a *fresh* `Arc`, so those records were unreachable forever. The reachability caveat is worth stating: the repair client is built without `attach_workspace_tools`, so those tools are not advertised in that request; getting there needs a provider that calls a tool name it was not offered. Nothing in the code refused it, and the same hole existed for file writes, which is the more likely one.
   - **`slice(-10)` hid up to 190 records (fixed).** The cap exists for the case where the model repeatedly probes refused origins, and that is precisely the case where the review area would have shown 10 of 200 with no indication. Now the whole list renders newest-first in a scroll box. Silently truncating the only place the user is told to look is the same defect class as the emit-only design 57 replaced.
   - **`runId` had no consumer (fixed).** It was normalized and read by nothing, so a stopped run's navigations sat in the list looking like the current run's. `isFromOtherRun` now tags them "earlier run"; when either id is missing it says nothing, because a wrong attribution is worse than none.
   - **Corrected a doc claim rather than the code:** external actions do *not* have the same standing as a `FileDiff` after a restart — diffs are mirrored into localStorage and re-displayed with a "no longer known to the backend" warning, external actions are not persisted at all. Restoring them client-side is defensible (they happened; forgetting them is a loss) and is the open item here.
   - Also still open from the audit: a run that only navigates leaves no badge on the Changes tab (`pendingChanges` counts diffs only), so the user has to already suspect something to go look. Rust 320, frontend 193 → 195.

59. **Stop now stops side effects, not just model calls (2026-09-14)**
   - This was the third prerequisite named in 53 for computer use, and the timeout bug in 56 was the same weakness surfacing early: cancellation reached the LLM loop and the gap *between* tool calls, but a call already inside `invoke()` went ahead and ran the command or opened the page. The user pressed Stop, the UI went Idle, and the world kept changing.
   - **One flag, not two.** `WorkspaceToolPermissions::fresh_cancel()` mints the `Arc<AtomicBool>` before the tool surface is built and hands it to `try_begin_run`, which publishes it to the `CancelRegistry` and puts it in the `RunLease`. So the tool layer and Stop look at the same switch. The alternative — a second flag on the permissions, kept in sync — is the "second copy of the same state" shape that produced the false-green connection indicator and the duplicated chat-context toggles; it would have failed the same way.
   - **Mint, never reset.** `fresh_cancel` replaces the `Arc` instead of storing `false` into it. Resetting is what the original global cancel flag did, and it let a new prompt un-cancel a still-draining old run. `continue_agent_pipeline` mints one too: sticking with the paused run's switch would mean a run stopped before the pause is stopped again the moment it resumes.
   - **Only side-effecting tools are refused.** Reads change nothing, and refusing them adds noise to a transcript that is about to be abandoned. Refused browser attempts are still recorded (`browser_open_cancelled` / `browser_tabs_cancelled`) — the navigation did not happen, but "after Stop, the model still tried to go out" is worth keeping. A refused write records nothing, because a review card for a write that never landed would claim a change that is not on disk.
   - **Known gap, stated rather than papered over:** `repair_workspace` inherits the previous run's tool surface (`drive_repair` clones `tool_invoker`), so its side-effect gate is not wired — the lease gets a fresh switch, but the tools hold the old one. Wiring it means rebuilding the tool surface for the repair run, which is the next step on that path. **This was worse than "not wired" and is fixed in 60.**
   - Computer use (mouse, keyboard, screenshots) still needs an OS-action permission model narrower than "allow browser" — per Known Issues 52 the reference implementation asks per app, which is the shape to copy. Rust 320 → 322.

60. **Audit of 59: the switch was half-installed and reported a stop as an action (2026-09-14)**
   - **A cancelled attempt was counted as an action that happened (fixed).** I named the record `browser_open_cancelled` without checking the two predicates that consume `kind`; both only knew `_refused` / `_failed`. So a navigation the gate had just *prevented* was announced as "Agent performed 1 browser action(s) … These cannot be undone." That is a false statement in the one surface this feature exists to make trustworthy — and worse than the pre-59 behaviour, where the count was at least true. Both classifiers now include `_cancelled`, the header reports performed-vs-not separately, and the frontend test pins it.
   - **The repair loop could be strangled by an earlier Stop (fixed).** `fresh_cancel` mints and never resets, and nothing clears the flag a stopped run left behind. `repair_workspace` minted a switch for its lease but kept using the *previous* run's tool surface, which still held the previous flag — set `true` forever by that Stop. Every write, command and navigation in a brand-new repair run would have been refused, and each refusal recorded against the repair run. Handing a mint to a path that does not rebuild its tools is the half that does nothing; `repair_workspace` now rebuilds MCP + workspace tools from its own permissions, exactly as `continue_agent_pipeline` does.
   - **The claim was broader than the code (corrected).** The gate sits at the *entry* of `WorkspaceToolInvoker::invoke`, and `executor.rs` already checks the same flag immediately before each call, so "stops side effects that have already reached the tool layer" was wrong: a spawned command still runs to completion, an in-flight CDP request is still bounded only by the 10 s timeout, and `McpToolInvoker` — where arbitrary side effects actually live — has no switch at all. What landed is defence in depth at the built-in tool boundary. SECURITY.md and AGENTS.md now say that, and the computer-use prerequisite is downgraded from "met" to "partially met: built-in tools, entry-only". Per Known Issues 52 the reference implementation latches the turn *and* kills the child process; matching that needs a child-process handle in `run_command_tool` and a gate on the MCP invoker.
   - Left open deliberately: nothing tests the wiring in `commands/agent.rs` (mint-before-clone, same `Arc` into `try_begin_run`). The unit tests flip the switch on a local object, which cannot catch a mis-ordered mint. That needs a test seam around the command layer, which does not exist yet.

61. **Stop now kills a command that is already running (2026-09-14)**
   - 60 established that the gate only refused calls that had not started. The obvious next hole was the one that costs the user real time: a `npm test` the Agent had already launched ran to completion after Stop — `Command::output()` blocks until the process exits, so there was nothing to interrupt. `run_project_command_cancellable` now polls the run's switch every 50 ms while the child runs and kills it, and `workspace_run_command` passes the switch through. `run_project_command` keeps its old signature by handing in a flag that is never set, so terminal-panel runs are unchanged.
   - **Reading the pipes needs its own threads.** Switching from `output()` to `spawn()` means we own the deadlock: a full pipe buffer blocks the child on write while we wait for it to exit. Two reader threads drain stdout/stderr.
   - **The first version killed the child and still waited 29 seconds.** The test caught it. `child.kill()` takes down the shell, but the grandchild (`ping`, and in real life `node`) keeps the pipe open, so `read_to_end` blocked exactly as long as before. Two fixes: `kill_process_tree` uses `taskkill /PID … /T /F` on Windows so the whole tree goes, and the cancelled path does not join the readers at all — they end when the pipe finally closes, and a leaked buffer is cheaper than a false "stopped".
   - **A killed command is an error, not a result.** Returning a `RunProjectTaskResult` with `exit_code: None` would let the repair loop read "no failures" out of a command that never finished. The tool reports `Stopped: … was killed because the run was cancelled.`
   - Still honest limits, now in SECURITY.md: a Unix command that forks past its shell can outlive the kill (that needs `setsid` + process-group signals), an in-flight CDP request is bounded only by its 10 s timeout, and MCP tools still have no switch — that is the next piece, and it is the one that matters for computer use, since per Known Issues 52 the OS surface would live behind MCP. Rust 322 → 323. **MCP landed in 62.**

62. **The side-effect switch now covers MCP, and there is one owner for it (2026-09-14)**
   - MCP is the largest side-effect surface in the product (filesystem servers, git, HTTP) and it was the last one ignoring Stop: the user pressed it, the UI went idle, and queued MCP calls kept going out one by one. `McpToolInvoker` now holds the run's switch and refuses to issue a call once it is set, logging the refusal at `warn`. A call already in flight is not cancellable — MCP as we speak it has no cancellation — so the gate is "no new calls", and SECURITY.md says exactly that.
   - **Ownership moved to the command layer.** `fresh_cancel()` minted the switch *inside* `WorkspaceToolPermissions`, which only worked because the workspace tool surface was built after the permissions. MCP is attached *before* them, so that shape could not reach it. Now each command mints one `Arc<AtomicBool>` at the top and hands the same one to three places — `attach_mcp_tools`, `WorkspaceToolPermissions::adopt_cancel`, `try_begin_run` — instead of a struct minting a switch other subsystems then have to find. Same invariant, stated where it is actually true.
   - `adopt_cancel` replaces `fresh_cancel`, and the "mint per run, never reset" rule stays: the mint site is the top of each command, so `continue_agent_pipeline` and `repair_workspace` still get their own switch rather than inheriting a latched one.
   - Not tested, and worth being blunt: `McpToolInvoker` needs an `AppHandle`, so it cannot be constructed in a unit test. The refusal branch is three lines with no branching beyond the flag, but it is covered by nothing. Testing it means a seam for the emit side, which is the same missing seam ROADMAP 60 named for `commands/agent.rs`. **Built in 63.**

63. **The MCP executor emits through `RunEvents`, so its gate is testable (2026-09-14)**
   - `McpToolInvoker` held an `AppHandle` and called `emit` directly, which is exactly the shape `agent/events.rs` was written to remove from the orchestrator two years of commits ago. The consequence was the same: the whole executor was unconstructible in a unit test, so the Stop gate added in 62 shipped with zero coverage — a control that might be inert and nobody would know. It now holds `Arc<dyn RunEvents>`; the desktop passes `Arc::new(app_handle.clone())`, tests pass `RecordingEvents`.
   - Two tests, and the second matters as much as the first: after Stop the call is refused **and** a `warn` entry naming the tool reaches the log (a refusal that only lives in the return value disappears with the conversation), and with the switch clear the gate does not interfere — an empty registry must fail with "unknown tool", not "run was stopped". Without that second test, `if true` in the gate would pass the first one.
   - This is the general lesson, now recorded twice: **`AppHandle` in a struct is a testability boundary, not a detail.** Any type that both decides something and emits should take `Arc<dyn RunEvents>`. The remaining holder is the command layer itself (`workspace_tool_logger`, `finish_agent_run`, `publish_*`), which is what ROADMAP 60 flagged and is the next seam to build if the wiring there is to be pinned. Rust 323 → 325.

64. **Computer use, first slice: read-only desktop observation (2026-09-15)**
   - `workspace_computer_windows` lists the visible top-level windows — title, app, geometry, foreground flag. `services/computer.rs` holds the pure logic plus a `windows-sys` enumeration (`EnumWindows` + `GetWindowTextW` + `QueryFullProcessImageNameW`); `windows-sys` rather than `windows` because this needs three functions, not the whole WinRT projection, and the version is pinned because it is unsafe FFI.
   - **Why observation and not screenshots or clicking.** A screenshot is *useless today*: `ChatMessage.content` is a `String` and the LLM client has no multimodal content, so the model cannot read the image. Shipping it would be an inert control — the thing AGENTS.md forbids — and it would have been easy to do and feel like progress. Input injection is irreversible and can dismiss any confirmation dialog; it needs a narrower authority model and a replayable record, so the shape gets proven on a surface where a mistake costs a disclosure rather than a click.
   - **The allowlist filters results, not just the tool's existence.** This is the browser-tabs mistake from 56 fixed *before* shipping rather than after: there, allowlisting one origin still handed over every tab. Here, windows outside the allowlist never reach the model and only the hidden *count* does — the model is told it is looking at a subset, so it cannot mistake a filtered list for the whole desktop.
   - Window titles are the asset: document names, page titles, the other party in a chat. So the authority is per-app (`normalize_app_name` accepts `Code.exe` / `code` / a full path), the disclosure is recorded in the external action log with the app names and hidden count, and refusals are recorded too. A window whose process cannot be read becomes `unknown`, which matches no entry — failures do not disclose.
   - **The platform is part of the gate.** `allows_computer()` includes `cfg!(windows)`, so on macOS/Linux the tool is not advertised at all rather than advertised-and-always-failing; a tool that always fails gets retried, and its failure reads as "the desktop is empty".
   - Not on the preset ladder, same reasoning as browser use, and asserted in the frontend test. Rust 325 → 332.

65. **Audit of 64: a TOML header, and three claims the record did not keep (2026-09-15)**
   - **The new dependency table swallowed two others (fixed).** I put `[target.'cfg(windows)'.dependencies]` in the middle of `[dependencies]`. A TOML table header ends the previous table, so `flamegraph` and `criterion` — the last two entries — silently became Windows-only, and `cargo build --features performance-tools` on macOS/Linux would activate nothing without any error. Verified with `cargo metadata`, not inferred. Nothing consumes those crates today, so the impact was latent; the defect is that a build config split platforms invisibly. The table now sits at the end of the file with a comment saying why.
   - **`unknown` was disclosed under `*`, and the test that looked like it covered this covered an unreachable value (fixed).** `app_allowed` short-circuited on `*` *before* normalizing, so a window whose process handle could not be opened — overwhelmingly an *elevated* process, i.e. the sensitive ones — was disclosed. My test asserted `!app_allowed("", …)`, and `window_app` never returns `""`. So the invariant I wrote in three places ("failures do not disclose") was false, and the assertion that appeared to pin it pinned nothing. `UNKNOWN_APP` is now refused outright, `*` included, and the test uses the value that actually occurs. Cost: a real `unknown.exe` is invisible; accepted.
   - **A Stop-refused desktop call left no external record (fixed).** `COMPUTER_WINDOWS` was added to the side-effect gate list but the record beside it was still `matches!(tool_name, BROWSER_OPEN | BROWSER_TABS)`. Same defect shape as 60: adding a tool without checking the consumers of the tool name. There is now `computer_windows_cancelled`, pinned by a test.
   - **The header called it a browser action (fixed).** `publish_external_actions` and the Diff view both hardcoded "browser action(s)", so a desktop listing with browser use *off* announced "1 browser action(s) — cannot be undone". Now "external action(s)".
   - **Corrected two claims instead of the code.** An *unauthorized* call never reaches the tool: `handles()` is false and both `select_external_calls` and `CompositeToolInvoker::invoke` filter on it, so `computer_windows_refused` (and the browser equivalent for "switch off entirely") cannot appear in a real run — the in-tool check is defence in depth for a direct caller, and SECURITY.md now says so rather than implying refusals are always recorded. And matching is by **executable name only**: pasting a full path into the allowlist does not scope it to that install, which a user could easily assume.
   - The unsafe FFI came out clean on the audit's own review (callback soundness, buffer arithmetic, `CloseHandle` on every path, `BOOL` comparisons). That is the part I expected to be wrong; the defects were in the boring layers — a table header, a test asserting an impossible value, and three sentences. Rust 332 → 333.

66. **The Changes tab now points at the record it is supposed to point at (2026-09-15)**
   - Open since 58: a run that only acted *outside* the workspace produced no pending diff, so the tab badge — derived from `pendingChanges` alone — stayed empty. The user had to already suspect something to go look at the only record of an irreversible action. A compensating control nothing points at is weaker than the docs claimed.
   - `changesBadge(pendingChanges, performedExternal)` is a pure function with its own tests, not an inline ternary, because there is a *judgment* in it and the judgment is the part worth pinning: the two counts are **not merged**. A pending change is "you can still decide"; an external action is "this already happened". One number would state them as one thing, so the external case gets its own colour and its own `title` ("N external action(s) that cannot be undone").
   - Refused, failed and Stop-cancelled attempts are excluded — `summarizeExternalActions` already separates them, and badging a *prevented* action as something that happened is the exact mistake ROADMAP 60 fixed in the header text.
   - **Deliberately not started this cycle: multimodal LLM content.** I mapped it first (one `ChatMessage` with `content: String`, one shared OpenAI-compatible request builder, no per-model capability table, and no Anthropic `/v1/messages` shape anywhere) and the cheapest sound design is an added `images: Option<Vec<ImagePart>>` plus a manual `Serialize` that emits content blocks only when images are present — that leaves all 16 construction sites and 7 `.content` readers untouched, where changing `content` to an enum touches all of them. It needs a capability decision per model and a degradation path, and it needs a *producer* in the same cycle or it is dead code by this project's own rule. Screen capture is that producer and it needs another Win32 surface plus a PNG encoder. Recorded here so the next cycle starts from a decision rather than a blank page. Frontend 195 → 199. **Landed in 67, with a cheaper producer.**

67. **Multimodal input, with the cheapest honest producer (2026-09-15)**
   - Built the design 66 recorded, and picked a different producer than the one 66 assumed. `workspace_read_image` lets the Agent look at an image *already in the workspace* — a mockup, a diagram, a screenshot committed to the repo. It reuses `workspace::resolve_existing` and `reject_credential_path`, needs no new OS authority and no encoder, and "look at `docs/mockup.png` and implement it" is useful today. Screen capture stays for later; what multimodal needed proving first was the **wire format and the degradation**, and a cheap producer proves both.
   - **The wire shape stays a bare string unless there are images.** `ChatMessage` gained `images: Vec<ImagePart>` and a hand-written `Serialize`: no images → `content` is the same JSON string it always was; images → OpenAI content blocks. Doing it unconditionally would break *every* request against the older compatible endpoints (local llama.cpp servers accept only the scalar form) — breaking the common path for a rare capability is the worst trade available here. An empty text does not become an empty block, because some providers reject that with an error that has nothing to do with images.
   - **Capability detection errs towards "cannot see".** `model_supports_images` matches known vision model-name substrings, because an OpenAI-compatible endpoint has no capability query and `provider` is a user-typed string. A false positive costs a 400 whose message is unrelated to images, which the user cannot connect to anything; a false negative costs a dropped image *with an explanation*. `adapt_images_for_model` strips the images and appends "[N image(s) were not sent: the configured model (X) does not accept image input.]" — dropping silently would leave the model reasoning about "as shown above" with nothing there, which is worse than either.
   - **The image travels on a side channel, not through `invoke`'s return type.** `ToolInvoker` gained `take_images()` with a default empty implementation, so MCP and every test double are untouched; `WorkspaceToolInvoker` drains it from the permissions object, `CompositeToolInvoker` chains its children, and the executor attaches whatever it gets to that one tool-result message — **including on the failure path**, because an undrained image would land on the *next* tool result and pair a picture with the wrong question.
   - `base64` is 25 lines here rather than a dependency (RFC 4648 vectors plus non-ASCII bytes are in the tests), and one image is capped at 4 MiB with the type restricted to png/jpg/gif/webp — the intersection every provider accepts. An unknown extension is refused locally instead of guessed, for the same reason as the capability check.
   - Rust 333 → 346.

68. **Audit of 67: the images were attached where the one vision provider rejects them, and sent eleven more times than once (2026-09-15)**
   - **Images rode on a `role:"tool"` message (fixed).** 67's bullet says the executor attaches them "to that one tool-result message"; OpenAI Chat Completions accepts image blocks only on `user` messages. So the one model family for which `model_supports_images` actually returns true is the one whose API most likely rejects the placement — the feature would have failed on its only real target while passing every unit test, because the tests assert our serialization, not theirs. Images now go on a following `user` message ("Attached N image(s) from the tool call(s) above."), and the tool result stays text-only. That also drops the reason 67 gave for draining on the failure path, but the drain stays: `take_images` must not carry a picture into the next round.
   - **Every image was re-sent on every subsequent round, and the trimmer charged nothing for it (fixed).** `messages` is the accumulating transcript, so an image attached in round 1 was serialized again in rounds 2…12. One 4 MiB file is ~5.33 MiB of base64, so a full tool loop meant **11 × 5.33 ≈ 58.7 MiB** of extra request egress from a single `workspace_read_image` call — invisible in the token counter, which measures `content` and scores an image at 0. Fixed by clearing `images` on all messages right after each `stream_chat_with_tools` call: an image is sent exactly once, in the round it was produced. A per-run image budget is still open; so is the trimmer's 0-cost accounting, which now understates a round by megabytes rather than a run.
   - **The local and mock endpoints dropped images while the transcript claimed they arrived (fixed).** Those paths flatten messages to a text prompt and never look at `images`, so the model saw "Attached docs/mockup.png (image/png, …) to this turn" — the tool's own success text — with no image. The transcript actively asserted the attachment succeeded, which is worse than the capability check's honest note. `drop_images_with_note(messages, "this endpoint takes a flattened text prompt")` now runs before the local/mock fork, pinned by `a_text_only_endpoint_says_the_images_were_not_sent`.
   - **A symlink turned the credential deny-list into an egress channel (fixed).** `reject_credential_path` ran on the *argument*, not on the resolved path, so `docs/mockup.png -> ../.env` passed the name check and `resolve_existing` followed the link. This was pre-existing in `read_file_tool`, but `read_image` widened it: 4 MiB instead of the text reader's 64 KB, and the action log records only that an image was attached, not what was in it. Both tools now deny on the argument **and** on the resolved path.
   - **The 4 MiB cap was enforced after reading the whole file (fixed).** `std::fs::read` then check length: a 2 GB file was fully resident before being refused. Now `std::fs::metadata().len()` is checked first.
   - Still open, recorded rather than papered over: only the *model* is told when images are dropped — there is no action-log entry, so the user sees a normal answer and cannot tell the picture never arrived; `workspace_read_image` is advertised in the tool list unconditionally, unlike browser and desktop tools which are gated behind a switch plus an allowlist; and nothing tests the command-layer wiring in `commands/agent.rs`, which is where the last three cycles' defects actually lived.
   - Rust 346 → 347, frontend 199.

69. **A degradation only the model knew about (2026-09-15)**
   - Left open by 68: when images were dropped, the *reason* went into the message text, which only the model reads. The user got an answer that looked entirely normal, with no way to tell whether the model ever saw the picture — and "did it actually look at the mockup?" is the first thing you would want to rule out when the answer is wrong. The project's whole premise is that the user can see what happened; a silent capability loss is the same class of defect as a silent write.
   - `LlmClient` now carries `image_drops: Arc<Mutex<Vec<ImageDrop>>>`, deliberately shaped like the existing `tools_rejected` flag rather than as a new channel: one instance is borrowed by every stage of a run, so one read at the end sees every drop. The command layer turns it into an `image_input_degraded` warning naming the count and the reason. Threading `Arc<dyn RunEvents>` down into `LlmClient` was the alternative and was rejected — `stream_chat_with_tools` is two layers below anything that has a handle (`stream_with_tool_loop` → `execute_step`/`execute_stage`, neither of which takes events), so it would have meant changing four signatures to report one fact the caller can just as well pull.
   - **Recording happens one layer above the chokepoint, on purpose.** `build_chat_request` still calls `adapt_images_for_model` as a belt, so no future request path can send an image to a text-only model; the *accounting* sits in `adapt_and_record_images`, called by the two request builders. `adapt_and_record_images` is idempotent — a second pass over already-stripped messages records nothing — which is what keeps a user from being told two images were dropped when there was one.
   - **Both degradation warnings moved into `finish_agent_run`.** `tool_capability_degraded` was previously emitted only from the two success branches, so a run that fell back to the text protocol *and then failed* reported nothing — and that is the run where "the model had no tools" is the most useful thing to know. A degradation is a fact about a request that already went out; the run's later outcome does not undo it. This also gives the pipeline path (`continue_agent_pipeline`), which never reported tool degradation at all, the same coverage.
   - Not done: there is still no per-run image budget, and the context trimmer still charges an image 0 tokens. Both are now bounded by "one image per round" rather than by the length of the tool loop, so they cost a wrong number rather than a runaway one.
   - Rust 347 → 349.

70. **Audit of 69: the report reached three of the four run paths, and one deleted line (2026-09-15)**
   - **`repair_workspace` reported neither degradation (fixed).** It hand-rolls its own finish sequence (`emit_usage_action_log` / `publish_tool_writes` / `publish_external_actions` / `finish_run`) instead of calling `finish_agent_run`, so putting the warnings *inside* `finish_agent_run` gave it nothing. Repair attaches the full tool surface, `workspace_read_image` included, and can lose `tools` to a 400 exactly like any other run — so automatic repair could send a picture-free prompt and say nothing. Same defect shape as 58 and 60: this path has now been the one left out three times, which is itself the finding. Both emits are now explicit there.
   - **The CLI dropped images silently (fixed).** `agent_cli` has no `RunEvents` sink, so an action-log entry has nowhere to go — but "no channel" is not a reason for "no report". The wording moved into a pure `image_degradation_report(&[ImageDrop]) -> Option<(String, String)>`, and the CLI prints it to **stderr** after the run: JSON and NDJSON consumers read stdout, so a warning there would corrupt the output they parse.
   - **The formatting is now testable, which is why it moved.** ROADMAP 68 recorded that nothing tests `commands/agent.rs`; that is because a function taking `AppHandle` cannot be called from a unit test. Rather than restate the gap, the *judgement* (aggregate the counts, say nothing when there were no drops) went into a pure function next to `ImageDrop` with its own test. `emit_image_degradation_log` is now a five-line adapter, the same trick that made `RunUsageSnapshot::action_log_summary` testable after its three branches had shared one wrong sentence.
   - **Corrected two claims in 69 rather than the code.** "The client is cloned per stage" is false — nothing clones `LlmClient`; every driver takes `&LlmClient`. The `Arc` is right anyway because the type is `Clone`, but the stated mechanism was invented. And "by the time the belt runs the images are already gone, so it records nothing" mis-stated why: `build_chat_request` is a free function that *cannot* record, and the test named as the guard never calls it. What the test actually pins — `adapt_and_record_images` being idempotent — is what the comment now says.
   - **One deleted line that had nothing to do with the change (fixed).** A botched `edit_file` whose `old_string` matched in the wrong function removed `agent-state-changed` from `undo_last_apply`. Impact was small because the frontend re-queries `pending_undo` itself, but it contradicted the orchestrator's own "every state change ships through this event" rule, and it landed unreviewed inside a commit about images. Restored with a comment. The lesson is the tool discipline: an `old_string` of two generic lines matches somewhere else, and the fix is to include enough context to be unique.
   - Left open, deliberately: the warning is filed at `stage: "Diff Review"` like every other `emit_review_action_log` entry, so it lands in the Logs panel rather than beside the stage that lost the image, and being `warn` it is not lifted into Problems (only `error` is). That is consistent with `tool_capability_degraded`, and changing it means giving the review-log helper a stage parameter — worth doing, not worth doing blind. Also still open: a drop is recorded when the request is *built*, so a run cancelled between build and send reports a drop for a request that never left.
   - Rust 349 → 350.

71. **The layer where the last four cycles' defects lived is now testable (2026-09-15)**
   - Every audit since 58 found its defect in `commands/agent.rs`, and always the same kind: a count that included something that did not happen, a header that named the wrong thing, a record published on three exit paths out of four. None of it was catchable, because each helper took `&AppHandle` — and an `AppHandle` cannot be constructed in a unit test. The 12 tests in that file covered the pure functions around the wiring and nothing in it.
   - `finish_agent_run`, `publish_tool_writes`, `publish_external_actions`, `emit_usage_action_log` and both `emit_*_degradation_log` now take `&dyn RunEvents` instead. This is the rule `agent/orchestrator.rs` and `McpToolInvoker` already follow, extended one layer out; `AppHandle` implements the trait, so all 16 call sites are unchanged apart from coercion, and the two `app_handle.emit(…)` calls became `events.emit_json(…)`. Now recorded in AGENTS.md, because the next person to add a helper here will reach for `AppHandle` by default.
   - **The first test covers the defect ROADMAP 60 fixed.** It builds real permissions with browser authority, pulls the cancel switch, and calls `invoke` — no test-only mutator, no hand-built record: the tool itself writes `browser_open_cancelled`, then the command layer counts it. The assertions are that the summary says "performed **0** external action(s)" *and* "1 refused, failed or stopped", and that the record still lands on the orchestrator. The old bug — `_cancelled` counted as performed — announced "1 browser action(s) — cannot be undone" for a navigation that never happened, in the one place this product asks to be trusted. What 60 left *open* is still open: the mint order in the commands (mint → `adopt_cancel` → attach tools → `try_begin_run`) is not covered, because this test flips the switch on a local object.
   - **The second pins the reporting seam from 69 and 70.** A `mock://` client with an image in the message really drops it, and `finish_agent_run` must emit `image_input_degraded` naming one image; a client with no drops must emit nothing under that phase. A warning that always appears is the same as no warning, so the negative half is load-bearing.
   - Both tests exercise the production path end to end rather than asserting the shape of a struct. That is the point: the defects were never in the data, they were in the sentence the user reads.
   - Rust 350 → 352.

72. **Audit of 71: making the helpers testable did not make the composition testable (2026-09-15)**
   - **`repair_workspace` now goes through `finish_agent_run` (fixed).** The audit's central point was sharper than the commit: of the four cycles whose defect lived in `commands/agent.rs`, *two* (58 and 70) were not in a helper at all — they were in the repair path's hand-copied finish sequence. Injecting `RunEvents` into the helpers does nothing for that. The sequence is now one call, so the divergence cannot recur by omission; the release of the run claim is inside it, still before the `?`, which is the property 70's fix depended on.
   - **The negative half of the image test was nearly vacuous (fixed).** The "no degradation → no warning" half used a client that had never sent anything, so `image_drops` was empty for a trivial reason and the assertion only excluded an *unconditional* emit — a property already covered one layer down by `image_degradation_report(&[]).is_none()`. It now sends an image-free message through the same `mock://` endpoint, so the drop path really runs and records nothing.
   - **Three numbers and one scope claim in 71 were wrong (corrected in place).** "14 tests" was the post-change count describing the pre-change state (it was 12). "Nine call sites" was the count of `finish_agent_run`'s exit branches, not of the six helpers' 16 call sites. And "the test that would have caught 60" overstated it: 60's *fixed* defect is covered, but 60's *open* item was the mint order in the commands, which a test flipping a switch on a local object cannot reach.
   - **Considered and rejected: folding the terminal `agent-state-changed` emit into `finish_agent_run`.** It would put five more emissions under test, and the audit is right that the `undo_last_apply` line I deleted in 69 was exactly this class of accident. But the branches emit *after* mutating state (`state_mgr.set(Idle)` follows the finish call), so a folded emit would ship a payload that still says Busy. Doing it properly means moving the state mutation into the finish helper too, which is a bigger change than this cycle's remit — recorded here rather than half-done.
   - Also from the audit, taken: the two tests build current-thread runtimes rather than full multi-thread ones, and the `_rx` binding now says why it must stay alive (the mock stream errors if the receiver is gone).
   - Rust 352, unchanged: this round fixed a composition and a weak assertion rather than adding cases.

73. **A per-run image budget, and one fewer imaginary problem (2026-09-15)**
   - Open since 68: the per-image 4 MiB cap says nothing about repetition. A round executes every tool call the model emitted, so the old exposure was not "one image per round" — it was **unbounded**; twelve rounds of a single 4 MiB image each is already ~48 MiB raw (~67 MB of base64), and nothing stopped several reads landing in one round. `MAX_RUN_IMAGE_BYTES = 16 MiB` now bounds it, tracked on `WorkspaceToolPermissions` as an `AtomicUsize` that is *not* the length of the image log — the log is drained every round by `take_images`, so counting it would restart the budget each round, and the bill is per run.
   - **Checked before the read, charged after a successful parse.** The check happens on the `metadata` size so an over-budget image is never loaded into memory, for the same reason the per-image cap moved to `metadata` in 68. The charge lands only once `image_part_from_bytes` succeeds, so a `.txt` or an empty file cannot drain the budget that legitimate images need — a test pins exactly that, because the natural implementation (charge where you check) gets it wrong.
   - **The refusal names three numbers** — used, requested, limit — because the model's next move should be "attach a smaller one" or "attach fewer", and an error that only says "over the limit" produces a retry of the same call. The boundary test asserts that exactly-at-limit is allowed: a budget that refuses its own last byte would look like a bug to whoever hits it.
   - **Deliberately not done: charging images to the token estimate.** 68 recorded that "the trimmer charges an image 0 tokens", and I was about to fix it before checking. `bound_transcript` only ever sees a *prior stage's* transcript, and the executor clears `images` right after each request, so no image is ever alive when the trimmer runs — the number it produces is correct today. The other estimator (`services/context.rs`) works on already-flattened `String` sections and there is no image on `SendPromptRequest`, so the context panel has nothing to charge either. Both would have been changes in service of a defect that cannot occur; what remains true is that if a *prompt*-attached image is ever added, both estimators need the addend, and that is the moment to write it.
   - Rust 352 → 355.

74. **Audit of 73: the budget was per run on two paths out of four (2026-09-15)**
   - **`continue_agent_pipeline` and `repair_workspace` inherited the previous run's spend (fixed).** Both build their tool surface by *cloning* `orch.tool_permissions`, and `Clone` shares the `Arc`. Both already replace exactly one piece of inherited state — the cancel switch, via `adopt_cancel`, because inheriting *that* was a past bug — and nobody did the same for the new counter. So a repair following an image-heavy prompt could be born with no budget, and the refusal would tell it "this run has already attached 16777216 bytes" about a run that had attached none, with advice ("attach fewer") it could not act on. `reset_image_budget()` now sits next to `adopt_cancel` and is called explicitly on both paths, so which path counts as a new run is a written decision rather than an oversight.
   - **"Checked before the read" was the half nothing tested (fixed).** Deleting the pre-read check left all 355 tests green, because the post-parse charge refuses anyway — after the file has been read and base64-encoded. The commit message claimed both halves were covered. The ordering is now pinned by an equivalent observable: with the budget nearly spent, reading a **non-image** file must fail with the *budget* message; if the check moved after the parse it would fail with "png, jpg, gif, webp" instead. No 16 MiB fixture needed.
   - **`charge_image_bytes` was check-then-add while its comment claimed there was no window (fixed).** Two concurrent callers could both pass the check and both add. Unreachable today — tool calls in a round run sequentially and one run holds the lease — but an invariant stated in a comment has to be true, so it is a `fetch_update` with the check inside the closure.
   - **The arithmetic that justified the constant was wrong (corrected).** "Twelve rounds at 4 MiB is ~64 MB" is 48 MiB, and the framing understated the old exposure: several `workspace_read_image` calls can land in *one* round, so the pre-fix bound was not 12 × 4 MiB, it was unbounded. Also fixed "16 MiB ≈ 21 MB base64" (≈ 22 MB).
   - **A test that could pass while the code kept only two of three numbers (fixed).** With `used == limit`, `error.contains(&limit.to_string())` is also satisfied by the *used* substring, so "the refusal names used, requested and limit" was not actually pinned. The assertions now use three distinct values via `check_run_image_budget_with_limit`.
   - Still open, recorded: the run budget does not bound a **single request**. Four 4 MiB images read in one round are sent in one request (~22 MB of base64), which is over the ~20 MB figure the per-image cap's own comment cites as the provider limit; that needs a per-round check, not a per-run one. And the budget is a hard constant while token and spend ceilings are per-profile — deliberate for a first cut, but the asymmetry is a choice, not an accident.
   - Rust 355 → 357.

75. **The other half of the image cap: one request (2026-09-15)**
   - Left open by 74, and the more likely failure of the two: the run budget lets four 4 MiB images through, and if the model reads them in *one* round they all ride the same request — ~22 MB of base64, over the ~20 MB most providers accept. The result would be a 413 whose message says nothing about images, on a run the user authorised for exactly this. `fit_images_in_request` now caps a request at 12 MiB of base64, leaving the rest of a ~20 MB envelope for the transcript that travels with it (a rationale for the number, not an enforced invariant — nothing measures the serialized body).
   - **Truncation is from the tail, not "keep whatever fits".** The model is told which images arrived and pairs them with calls by order; skipping a large image and keeping a later small one would silently re-pair *every* image with the wrong question.
   - **The first image is kept unconditionally.** It has already passed the 4 MiB per-image cap, so it always fits the 12 MiB envelope in practice; the branch exists so that a future smaller envelope cannot produce "you read an image and got a message with no image", which is the one outcome worse than a truncated set.
   - **The executor reports through the client's existing drop log** (`note_dropped_images`) rather than a second channel. The executor has no `RunEvents`, and a user hunting "did it see the mockup?" should not have to know that two different layers drop images for two different reasons — both now surface as the same `image_input_degraded` warning.
   - Rust 357 → 358. **Superseded in 76: the overflow is deferred to the next round rather than dropped, and the message names which images are missing.**

76. **Audit of 75: the advice it gave the model was impossible to follow (2026-09-15)**
   - **A dropped image was already paid for, and "read them again" walked into the run cap (fixed).** The run budget is charged at *read* time, so four 4 MiB reads spend the whole 16 MiB. The request cap then kept two and told the model to re-read the others — which `charge_image_bytes` refuses, with advice ("attach fewer") it cannot act on, after burning a round. The overflow is now **carried to the next round** instead: `fit_images_in_request` returns the images rather than a count, the executor keeps them in `pending_images` across iterations, and they are attached to the next request. Nothing is re-read and nothing is charged twice. If the loop ends before they fit, *that* is the only case they are lost, and it is reported.
   - **The message never said which images were missing (fixed).** It explained why and what to do, but tail truncation is only usable by the model if it knows the tail is what went missing — and above it sat several tool results each saying "Attached docs/x.png … to this turn". The text is now "Attached the first N of M image(s) … The last K did not fit in this request and will be attached in the next step — do not read them again", built by a pure `round_image_note` with its own test. The wording is the thing that keeps failing in this codebase, so it lives where it can be asserted.
   - **`note_dropped_images` had no test, and the executor path had none either (partly fixed).** The public entry point now has one: 0 images must not produce a degradation, and a reported drop must come out of `image_degradation_report` with the same sentence as a model-side drop. The executor's own loop is still only reachable with a real `LlmClient` and a mock tool call, which caps at one round, so the *carry across rounds* is covered by the pure function rather than end to end. Recorded rather than pretended.
   - **The boundary was not pinned and the fix was one character wide (fixed).** `half = MAX/2 + 1` meant flipping `<=` to `<` changed nothing observable. The test now uses `MAX/2` twice and asserts both fit, then adds a byte and asserts the second is deferred.
   - **`cargo fmt` is not in the four verification commands, and the new code was not fmt-clean.** CI runs `cargo fmt --check` and the repo was already dirty in ~15 files, so the job was red before this cycle too. I intended to format only the three files this cycle touched; `cargo fmt -- <paths>` formats the whole crate regardless of the trailing arguments, so the commit carried a repo-wide reformat alongside a behaviour change — an unreviewed scope leak of exactly the kind these audits keep finding, and the honest place to record it is here. The tree is now fmt-clean (verified) and the suite passed *after* the sweep, so the outcome is right; the process was not.
   - **`cargo fmt --check` is now the first of five verification commands in AGENTS.md.** A check that lives only in CI is a check that drifts: every local run was green while 15 files were unformatted.
   - Corrected in 75 in place: "12 MB" (the constant is 12 MiB = 12.58 MB), and the claim that the request envelope is enforced — it is a rationale for the number, nothing measures the serialized body.
   - Rust 358 → 360.

77. **The side-effect gate now has one source instead of two hand-copied ones (2026-09-15)**
   - Open since 60, restated by the audits of 71 and 72: the finish side of a run became testable, the *start* side did not. The switch was handed out twice — once to `WorkspaceToolPermissions::adopt_cancel`, once to `orch.try_begin_run` — as two adjacent lines copied into four commands. Nothing checked that the two were the same `Arc`, and the compiler cannot: a mismatch type-checks, and the consequence is Stop leaving tool calls running while the UI says idle.
   - **`claim_run_for(orch, run_id, permissions)` reads the switch back out of the permissions** rather than taking it as a second parameter. One read, so divergence is structurally impossible; the mint and `adopt_cancel` stay where each command needs them (before the tool surface is cloned from the permissions). It also absorbs `permissions.run_id = orch.current_run_id.clone()`, the other line all four had copied, so the id is claimed in the one place it becomes known.
   - **Pinned by `Arc::ptr_eq`, plus the behaviour that matters**: setting the lease's flag must make `permissions.cancelled()` true. `ptr_eq` is the strong half — it fails on two separate flags even when both are `false` — and the behavioural half is the weak one, since a *separate* flag that happened to be `true` would satisfy it. Both together say "same allocation, and pulling it is observable from the tool side".
   - The four commands still differ in shape (two build the surface before the lease, two after; two derive permissions from the request, two clone the previous run's). Unifying that is the `build_run_surface` refactor the audit sketched — deliberately not done here: it touches the most sensitive path in the app, and this cycle's point was to make the *invariant* checkable, not to restructure four command bodies at once.
   - **One inconsistency fixed on the way**: `continue_agent_pipeline` was the only path that never wrote its permissions back to `orch.tool_permissions`. **Corrected in 78** — the consequence I claimed for it (a *pause → continue → repair* sequence building the repair surface from the pre-pause authority) cannot occur, because repair overwrites every field continue had changed. The write-back is consistency, not a bug fix.
   - Rust 360 → 361.

78. **Audit of 77: the guarantee stopped at the built-in tools, and the file that states it had outgrown its own budget (2026-09-15)**
   - **The MCP leg was still hand-passed, while AGENTS.md said it could not be (fixed).** `claim_run_for` tied the lease to the *built-in* surface, but `attach_mcp_tools` took a separate clone of the command-local switch. All four paths happened to pass the right `Arc`; nothing structural or tested held it, and the entry declared the mismatch impossible — about the largest side-effect surface in the product. Now the mint is **moved** into `adopt_cancel` and every later consumer reads `permissions.cancel_switch()`: MCP, the built-in surface, and the lease. Handing the flag to a fifth consumer no longer compiles, which also restores (and widens) the move-based property 77 quietly gave up.
   - **`send_agent_prompt` and `run_agent_step` were reordered** so the permissions exist before the MCP attach. That is the only way the MCP invoker can read the switch from the authority rather than from a local variable; the mode read that computes `allow_write` moved up with it, and no lock is now held across the `attach_mcp_tools` await.
   - **A claimed consequence that cannot happen (corrected, not coded around).** 77 said the missing write-back in `continue_agent_pipeline` made a later repair use pre-pause authority. Repair overwrites the switch, the image budget and the run id immediately after cloning, and every other field is either copied identically or a shared `Arc`, so the two surfaces are observationally identical. The audit was right to demand evidence for a stated symptom — this is the rule the ROADMAP itself keeps citing, applied to me.
   - **`AGENTS.md` was 9 045 bytes against an 8 000-byte injection cap**, so the Agent was receiving a copy truncated mid-sentence inside "Documentation claims must be verified against the code" — the rule that would have caught the paragraph above. Rewritten to 6 519 bytes with every rule intact, and the header now says *bytes*, says ~7 500, and says measure rather than guess.
   - **Also corrected in 77**: the test's justification was inverted. `Arc::ptr_eq` is the strong half (it fails on two separate flags); the behavioural half is the weak one, since a separate flag already set to `true` would satisfy it.
   - Still open, recorded: `try_begin_run` stays `pub`, so a fifth command could re-copy the two lines and the new test would stay green — the structural fix is to have it take `&WorkspaceToolPermissions`, which the orchestrator already owns. And `permissions.run_id` is stamped *after* the built-in surface is cloned on two of four paths, harmless only because nothing inside `workspace_tools` reads that field today.

79. **Two CDP endpoints nobody called, and the design for screen capture (2026-09-15)**
   - `browser.rs` carried `activate_endpoint` and `close_endpoint` with **no production caller** — only a test asserting the URL shape, which is the "control that does nothing" rule applied to a helper: it reads as though the Agent can focus or close a tab, and it cannot. Deleted rather than wired: closing a user's tab is an irreversible action on something the Agent did not create, and it would need its own authority line, not a free ride on `allowBrowserUse`. The loopback assertion it was carrying moved onto `new_tab_endpoint`, which does have a caller.
   - **Where browser use and computer use actually stand**, since the docs describe pieces and never the whole: browser use is *open a URL* and *list tabs* — no DOM read, no click, no type, no wait-for-load, and it needs the user's own Chrome started with `--remote-debugging-port`. Computer use is *enumerate visible windows* — read-only, Windows-only. Both carry the full authority apparatus (switch + non-empty allowlist, irreversible-action records including `_refused` / `_failed` / `_cancelled`, the Stop gate, an action-log line per call). The apparatus is the part that has been audited five times; the capability surface is deliberately thin.
   - **Screen capture design, decided here so the next cycle does not start from a blank page** (the shape ROADMAP 66 used for multimodal):
     - Its old blocker is gone. 66 refused it because `ChatMessage.content` was a `String`, so a captured image had no consumer. Images now have three budgets and honest degradation reporting, so a capture has somewhere to go.
     - **Capture one window, never the screen.** `PrintWindow` on a specific `HWND` chosen from the same enumeration `workspace_computer_windows` already gates. A full-screen grab cannot be scoped by any allowlist the user has agreed to, and "allow this app" is a promise about that app only.
     - **A separate grant from observation.** Window *titles* and window *contents* are different disclosures — a title says "Signal is open", a capture shows the messages. Reusing `allowComputerUse` would silently upgrade an authority the user already gave. That means a new toggle plus a backend reader in the same commit, or it is an inert control.
     - **PNG via a pinned `png` dependency.** The hand-rolled base64 was 25 lines; a PNG encoder is not, and BMP is not in the media-type intersection every provider accepts (`png/jpg/gif/webp`).
     - **It charges the same image budgets** as `workspace_read_image` — per image 4 MiB, per run 16 MiB, per request 12 MiB — and a capture that would exceed them is refused before the bitmap is copied, not after.
     - **The record is a disclosure record**: `computer_capture` with app, window title and pixel size, in the external-action log, because a screenshot cannot be taken back.
   - Rust 361, unchanged: this is a deletion plus a decision.

80. **The gate's last open door, closed (2026-09-15)**
   - Left open by 78: `try_begin_run(run_id, cancel)` still took a flag, so a fifth command could re-copy the two lines 77 removed and every test would stay green. It now takes `&WorkspaceToolPermissions` and reads `cancel_switch()` itself. There is no longer *any* way to give the lease a different flag from the tool surface — not by convention, by signature.
   - The orchestrator gains no new dependency for this: it already owns a `WorkspaceToolPermissions` field. And the 13 test call sites got better rather than noisier — `test_switch()` became `test_permissions()`, so the tests now travel the production path (mint → `adopt_cancel` → read back) instead of fabricating a switch the real code would never produce.
   - **Two stale doc references deleted**: both `try_begin_run`'s comment and the old test helper pointed at `WorkspaceToolPermissions::fresh_cancel`, a function that has never existed under that name. A comment naming a function that is not there is the cheapest possible way to send the next reader looking for a mechanism that isn't the real one.
   - **Overstated when written, corrected in 81**: "there is no longer *any* way to give the lease a different flag" holds at the moment of the claim, not forever — a stray `adopt_cancel` afterwards would still split them — and at the time the sentence was written `attach_mcp_tools` still took a bare flag.
   - Rust 361, unchanged: a signature change and a test-helper rename, no new cases.

82. **Window capture, built to the design 79 recorded (2026-09-15)**
   - `workspace_computer_capture` takes one named window, encodes it to PNG and attaches it to the turn. It is the second image producer, and the first one whose input is not a file the user could have opened themselves.
   - **A separate grant, not a widening of Desktop Observation.** Its own switch, its own app list, its own toggle in Settings. Reusing `computerApps` would have turned "you may see that Signal is open" into "you may read the messages" without the user doing anything — and the code now pins the distinction from both sides: enabling observation leaves `allow_capture` false and `capture_apps` empty, and enabling capture does the same to observation.
   - **`PrintWindow(PW_RENDERFULLCONTENT)`, never a screen grab.** A screen grab has no scope any per-app allowlist could describe. The flag is defined locally because windows-sys 0.59 exports only `PW_CLIENTONLY`; without it, DWM-composited windows (Chrome, Electron, terminals) capture as a black rectangle. `GetDIBits` is asked for a **negative** height so rows arrive top-down — a flipped image is a failure the model would never report, it would just read the picture wrong — and alpha is forced opaque because `PrintWindow` leaves it zero for many windows, which would otherwise produce a fully transparent PNG.
   - **Ambiguity captures nothing.** Zero or several matches are both refused, the several-match case listing candidates so the model can narrow down; windows outside the allowlist are not named in the refusal at all, only counted. Guessing which window to capture is an unrecoverable disclosure of the wrong thing.
   - **Two size gates in the right order**, and the same three image budgets as `workspace_read_image`: pixels are checked *before* any bitmap is copied (4 M pixels ≈ 2560×1440), the encoded PNG is checked against the 4 MiB per-image cap, and the run budget is charged on the real encoded size only after a successful parse. A capture and a file read compete for one 16 MiB allowance, because they cost the same money.
   - **The PNG dependency is deliberate.** Hand-rolling base64 was 25 lines; hand-rolling PNG means zlib, CRC32 and per-row filters. BMP would need no dependency but is not in the media-type intersection providers accept.
   - **A defect this slice's test found in the *existing* desktop path**: `normalize_app_name` stripped `.exe` *before* lowercasing, so an allowlist entry written `Chrome.EXE` normalized to `chrome.exe` while enumeration produced `chrome` — the entry never matched. That is precisely the "configured but never fires" failure the function's own comment says it exists to prevent, and the old test only used lowercase extensions. Fixed, and the test now includes `CODE.EXE` and a quoted, padded spelling.
   - Rust 361 → 367, frontend 199 (the new assertions landed inside existing cases).

83. **Audit of 82: the `app` filter could never match a real window (2026-09-15)**
   - **The tool's primary selector was inert (fixed).** `matches_target` normalized the *model's* filter and compared it against the **raw** `window.app`, which the enumeration produces with its extension intact (`chrome.exe`). So `app: "chrome"`, `app: "chrome.exe"` and a full path all failed, and because both filters must match, `app` + `title_contains` failed too — only a bare `title_contains` worked, on a tool whose description tells the model to use `app`. It failed *closed*, so nothing leaked; but per the project's own rule it was a shipped control that does nothing. Both sides are normalized now.
   - **The test that should have caught it asserted an impossible value.** The helper built windows with `app: "chrome"` — a spelling `window_app` cannot return. That is the ROADMAP 65 defect class exactly, and this time it actively concealed a broken feature: fixing the helper to `chrome.exe` made two tests fail immediately. The replacement walks all four spellings a user might write *and* goes through `select_capture_target` so the allowlist step is included.
   - **`GetDIBits` was called with the bitmap still selected into the DC (fixed).** MSDN says `hbmp` must not be selected. Current GDI tolerates it, which is why a manual test passes — and why the failure would have been "capture is black on that one machine" with nothing in the log. Deselect now happens first.
   - **A partial `GetDIBits` produced a silently half-black screenshot (fixed).** Only `rows == 0` was an error; `rows < height` left zeroed rows that the alpha-forcing loop then stamped opaque, so the model would read a black band as content. Exactly the class the commit claimed to have handled for the flip and alpha cases. `rows != height` is an error now.
   - **The budget order did not match what 82 said** ("charged after a successful parse") — capture charged first, then parsed, the reverse of `read_image_tool`. Reordered so the two paths agree, which also removes the one exit that charged the budget without recording an action.
   - **The capture gate had no test**, on the most privacy-sensitive authority in the app: `allows_capture()` could have been edited to read `computer_apps` and the suite would have stayed green. There is now a test that observation does not advertise or accept capture, that the switch alone with an empty list does not either, that capture alone does not enable window enumeration, and that the refusal record names only `desktop`.
   - Recorded rather than fixed: with `captureApps = ["*"]` a capture-only run can use `title_contains: "e"` to read back a titled window inventory — capability the observation grant exists to gate. It is arguably subsumed (it could screenshot them anyway) but SECURITY.md now says so instead of implying the two grants are fully independent. **The `spawn_blocking` gap is closed in 84.**
   - Rust 367 → 368.

84. **Capture no longer blocks the runtime it shares with the token stream (2026-09-15)**
   - Left open by 83: `PrintWindow` plus a PNG encode of up to 4 M pixels ran synchronously inside `async fn invoke`, on a Tokio worker. The visible symptom would have been the whole UI stalling for a few hundred milliseconds — token streaming, `get_agent_state`, every other command — with nothing to connect the stall to a screenshot. It is now a `spawn_blocking`, and a panic inside it becomes a failed capture rather than a poisoned run.
   - The tool function had to become `async`, which is why the gate test now drives it through a current-thread runtime. That is the honest cost: the seam between "authority decision" and "blocking work" is where the `async` boundary lands.
   - Rust 368, unchanged.

85. **The cross-round image carry is covered end to end (2026-09-15)**
   - Open since 76: the deferral was pinned only at the pure-function level, because the mock provider "looked like" it could emit tool calls for one round. It can do more than that, and the reason is a consequence of the deferral design itself: `mock_tool_call` decides "did this turn already call a tool" by looking only at messages **after the last `user` message**, and the image attachment *is* a new `user` message. So attaching an image re-opens the loop, and a genuine multi-round run is reachable in a test.
   - The test drives `stream_with_tool_loop` with an invoker that hands out two images too large to share one request, and reads the actual wire messages through `RequestRecorder`. Images per request come out `[0, 1, 1, 0]` — nothing in the initial prompt, one per following request, none in the last, which is also the "each image is sent exactly once" invariant from 68. The markers prove the *tail* image is the one that rode the second request, and the note in that request says "first 1 of 2 … do not read them again".
   - **The test corrected me on its final assertion.** I wrote "everything arrived, so there should be no degradation records" — but `mock://` is a text-only endpoint, so each image that reaches a request is honestly reported as not-sent. Two records, one per image, is the *stronger* statement: a dropped tail image would leave only one. The first version of the assertion would have passed for the wrong reason if the carry were broken and the loop had simply run twice for other reasons.
   - Left in place, worth knowing: because each attachment re-opens the mock's one-round guard, a mock-driven run whose tool returns an image every round will loop to `MAX_TOOL_ITERATIONS`. That is the mock's behaviour, not the product's — a real provider decides for itself — but a CLI smoke test that wires an image tool will see 12 rounds, not 1.
   - Rust 368 → 369.

86. **Input injection: the design, and the parts I am refusing to build (2026-09-15)**
   Recorded before any code, the way 79 was for capture, because the authority question here is the whole problem and the FFI is the easy part.
   - **Why it is not "capture with a different API call".** Observation and capture disclose; input *acts*, on software that is not ours, with no undo and no diff. Worse, it can dismiss the very confirmations that make everything else in this product safe — including a dialog the user is reading right now. A session-wide switch is the right *floor* for it and the wrong *ceiling*.
   - **Three constraints I will implement, all of them refusals:**
     - **Window-relative coordinates only.** A click is `(window, x, y)` clamped to that window's rect, never a screen point. A screen-absolute click cannot be scoped by any allowlist the user agreed to — it is the input equivalent of the full-screen grab that 82 refused.
     - **Verified foreground, immediately before each send.** `SendInput` goes wherever focus is, so the tool must name a window (same selector as capture), focus it, and then re-read the foreground window and confirm it is still the intended one. If it is not — the user alt-tabbed, a dialog stole focus — abort and record the abort. The alternative, `PostMessage` to a specific `HWND`, does not steal focus but is silently ignored by most modern apps, so it would produce a tool that reports success and does nothing.
