     - **The Stop gate is checked per action, not per call.** A typed string is a loop of key events; a run cancelled mid-string must stop mid-string, and the record must say how far it got.
   - **A third grant** (`allowComputerInput` + `inputApps`), separate again — being allowed to *read* a window is not being allowed to *drive* it — plus a per-run action cap in the shape of `MAX_EXTERNAL_ACTIONS`.
   - **The open question, stated rather than guessed:** a session switch is weaker than this deserves; the right shape is per-action human approval. And checking before writing it down: **nothing in the backend waits for a human today.** A grep for a pending/awaiting-approval state finds nothing — the MCP "approval policy" is a static choice made before the run (`auto_approved_only` / `allow_all`), and the review area gates *diffs after they exist*, not a tool call in flight. So per-action approval is not a matter of reusing parts; it is a new mechanism — a pending-approval state a tool call can await, a UI affordance that names the exact action, a timeout, and a defined interaction with Stop (a pending prompt must be cancellable, and cancelling must count as refusal, not as approval). That mechanism is the prerequisite, and it is most of the work.
   - Therefore the first slice is deliberately **the smallest useful action** (one click, one typed string, into one named window) *behind* that approval prompt — not a broad input API behind a toggle. Shipping the toggle first would deliver the dangerous half with the reassuring half missing, which is the one sequencing this project should never choose.
   - **What I will not build:** typing into "whatever has focus", screen-absolute clicks, key combinations sent while the target is not verifiably foreground, and any attempt to guess whether a window is a credential prompt — a heuristic that fails silently is worse than a refusal, and the honest boundary is that the user names the apps.

87. **Two open items closed by looking instead of coding (2026-09-15)**
   - **`workspace_read_image` is ungated on purpose** — 68 left this as a question, comparing it to browser and desktop tools which need a switch plus an allowlist. The comparison was wrong: those two reach *outside* the workspace, and that is what their gates are for. `read_image` reads a file inside the workspace that `workspace_read_file` can already read as text, resolved through the same `resolve_existing` and the same credential deny-list checked twice. What is genuinely different is cost, not authority — an image is expensive per byte and lands in a paid request — and that is what the three image budgets are for. Closing it as decided rather than leaving a TODO that implies a missing control.
   - **Two hazards the capture slice could have had, checked and absent.** A stale `localStorage` permissions object lacking `captureApps` would make `permissions.captureApps.join("\n")` throw when Settings opens — but `persistAgentSession` persists only session fields (`state`, `mode`, `steps`, `pipeline`, tasks, SDD artifacts, error, run id); permissions are never written to storage and always start from `READ_ONLY_PERMISSIONS`. And the new `computer_capture*` record kinds needed no frontend change because `externalActions.ts` is kind-agnostic: `isRefusedAction` matches the `_refused` / `_failed` / `_cancelled` suffixes, and the summary derives "performed" by subtraction. Recorded because the recurring defect in this project is *adding a name without checking its consumers* — this time the consumers were already generic, and knowing that is worth as much as a fix.

88. **Run-level facts stop pretending to belong to Diff Review (2026-09-15)**
   - Noted in 74 and again in 81: the degradation warnings went out through `emit_review_action_log`, which hard-codes `stage: Some("Diff Review")`. So did the token-usage line and the external-action summary. All four are facts about a *run*, not about a stage, and the stage field is what `TaskPipeline` groups by — so "1 image(s) were not sent to the model" appeared under the review stage while the stage that actually lost the image showed nothing.
   - `emit_run_action_log` is the same call with `stage: None`; the four run-level emitters use it, and the review helper keeps its meaning for what it was named after (apply, reject, undo). `diffSummary` still rides along on the run-level entries — that is context, not membership.
   - Pinned by reading the emitted payload rather than the summary text: the `image_input_degraded` entry's `stage` must be JSON `null`. Asserting on the wording would have passed with the wrong stage still attached, which is exactly how this survived two write-ups.
   - Rust 369, unchanged: a field value and its assertion, no new cases.

89. **The approval prompt already exists and has never been able to appear (2026-09-15)**
   - Looking for what per-action approval would cost, I found most of it already written and inert. `ConfirmDialog.tsx` is mounted in `App.tsx`, renders from `pendingConfirm`, has a "type to confirm" path and a *remember this* checkbox — and **nothing can ever open it**: `requestConfirm` has no caller outside the store, `pendingConfirm` has no producer, and the `agent-confirm-approved` / `agent-confirm-denied` events it dispatches have no listener anywhere in the tree.
   - That is the "control that does nothing" rule violated in the most expensive direction: a reader of `App.tsx` reasonably concludes destructive operations are confirmed. They are not. Marked at the mount site rather than left silent, because the misleading part is the mount, not the component.
   - **Not deleted, on purpose** — this is the one case where the delete-unused-code rule and the no-inert-control rule point in opposite directions, and the tiebreaker is that the dialog is ~80% of the UI that ROADMAP 86 named as the prerequisite for input injection. Deleting it this cycle to rebuild it next cycle would be churn; leaving it undocumented would be a lie. So: documented, and it is now the first step of the approval work rather than a discovery waiting to be re-made.
   - **What the wiring still needs** (revised estimate, lower than 86 assumed): a backend `ApprovalRegistry` (request → `oneshot`, resolve, refuse-all, timeout defaulting to refusal), one Tauri command to resolve a decision, an event → `requestConfirm` bridge, the dialog's two window events routed to that command, and Stop refusing everything pending. The first consumer should be `workspace_browser_open` — an action that already exists and has been audited five times — so the mechanism proves itself before input injection inherits it. *(Built in 90. One claim above was wrong: the dialog has **no** "type to confirm" path — the only input in it was a checkbox, and `requireExplicitConfirm` gated the weaker "remember this choice" option rather than a stricter confirmation. Both are gone in 90.)*

90. **The approval prompt now has something to ask about (2026-09-16)**
   The mechanism 86 named as the prerequisite for input injection, with `workspace_browser_open` as its first consumer. `agent/approval.rs`: `ApprovalRegistry` (a `HashMap<String, oneshot::Sender>`), `ApprovalGate` (registry + `Arc<dyn RunEvents>` + timeout), `ApprovalRequest`, `ApprovalOutcome`.
   - **Every way of not answering is a refusal, and they are four different sentences.** `Approved` / `Denied` / `TimedOut` / `Unattended` — the last being "this run installed no channel". Collapsing the last two into one message would lose the distinction between "the user said no" and "there was nobody to ask", which is the difference between a decision and a missing mechanism. `refusal_detail()` is one place, so the record and the tool's return value cannot disagree.
   - **The order of the gates is itself a safety property, and it has a test.** `allow_browser` → origin allowlist → *then* ask. An action that would be refused anyway must not produce a prompt: prompts that are safe to dismiss train the user to dismiss them, and the entire value of this mechanism is that every prompt is worth reading. `an_unauthorized_origin_never_bothers_the_user` asserts zero `agent-approval-requested` events for a disallowed origin.
   - **Stop refuses everything pending, before taking the lock.** Same reason `CancelRegistry` exists and the same ordering: Stop cannot queue behind the work it cancels. Without this, a dialog still on screen after Stop authorizes a navigation while the UI already reads idle.
   - **Ending the wait emits `agent-approval-closed`.** This is the half that is easy to omit and hard to notice: with only a request event, a timed-out dialog stays on screen and the user clicks Approve on something nobody is waiting for. The frontend closes only when the id matches, because by then the next request may already be up. `resolve_agent_approval` also returns *whether anyone was still waiting*, so a late click cannot render as "approved".
   - **The gate is a required argument, not a builder call.** `agent_tool_permissions(...)` takes the `ApprovalGate` positionally. `.with_approval()` on its own would have been the fifth instance of this project's most common defect — adding a mechanism and forgetting one of the places that must install it — and here forgetting it fails *closed but silently*: every irreversible action refused as `Unattended`, which looks like a broken feature rather than a missing wire. The compiler now asks. The two clone-derived paths (`continue_agent_pipeline`, `repair_workspace`) inherit the gate with the permissions.
   - **The inert parts of the dialog were deleted rather than wired.** The "remember this choice" checkbox had no consumer and could not get one this cycle; `requireExplicitConfirm` existed only to show it, and showed it backwards. Both are gone, along with the two `window` CustomEvents nobody listened to — the dialog now calls `resolveConfirm`, which invokes the command. 89 said the dialog was ~80% of the needed UI; the honest number is closer to 60%, since what it had least of was the part that talks to the backend.
   - **`tests/ipc-contract.test.ts` caught the one thing all five checks would have missed.** The two event names are Rust constants, and the contract scanner only matched string literals in `emit*(...)` — so it reported "the frontend listens for two events nothing emits", which is precisely the failure it exists to catch, arriving for the wrong reason. Fixed in the scanner (resolve `const NAME: &str = "…"`) rather than by copying the names back into literals: two copies of an event name is the drift this file exists to prevent. Pinned with its own test.
   - **Also checked, and deliberately not "fixed": the CLI never drains `take_external_actions()`.** It looked like ROADMAP 58/70's shape (a path that records and never reports) and I nearly wrote the reporting for it. It is unreachable: the CLI calls neither `with_browser` nor `with_computer` nor `with_capture`, so `allows_browser()` and friends are false, the tools are never advertised, and `handles()` drops a direct call — no external action can be recorded there at all. The fix belongs with the flag that would grant the authority, not before it. Recorded because "I almost added code for an impossible case" is worth as much as a fix.
   - Rust 369 → 377 (5 in `approval.rs`, 3 in `workspace_tools.rs`), frontend 199 → 210 across 26 files. `browser_open_tool` is now `async` and awaits `open_url` directly instead of `block_on_browser`, which also removes one `block_in_place` from the runtime that carries the token stream (the defect 84 fixed for capture).

91. **Audit of 90: the record blamed the user for pressing Stop (2026-09-16)**
   The mechanism held up — the audit found no bypass of the gate and no deadlock (nothing holds the orchestrator lock across a tool call, so a 120 s wait does not block `get_agent_state` or `stop_agent`). What it found was in the *record*, which is the only thing this capability can promise.
   - **Stop was recorded as a human denial (fixed).** `refuse_all` sent `Denied`, whose text is "The user denied this action." — so pressing Stop with a dialog open wrote into the permanent record that a person refused that navigation. It also broke the taxonomy: `WorkspaceToolInvoker::invoke`'s own Stop gate uses the `*_cancelled` suffix, and this path wrote `*_refused`, so one event had two names. There is now an `ApprovalOutcome::Cancelled` with its own sentence and `record_suffix()`, and a dropped channel maps to it rather than to `Denied` (nobody decided anything there either). The test that "covered" this asserted the enum, not the record — it now asserts `kind == "browser_open_cancelled"` and that the detail does not say "user denied".
   - **The cancel flag was not re-checked after approval (fixed).** The entry gate is taken *before* the wait, so it could be two minutes stale. Stop landing between "decision delivered" and "navigation performed" finds nothing pending to refuse, `abandon_run` marks the UI idle — and the page opened anyway. One `permissions.cancelled()` check after approval, with a test that pulls the switch before answering so the ordering is deterministic rather than lucky.
   - **Two claims whose consequence the code did not produce (corrected in place).** "A late click cannot render as approved" — the boolean was returned and then discarded by `void resolveConfirm(...)`; it now logs, and SECURITY.md says what actually happens instead of implying a user-visible guarantee. "The compiler now asks" — true for the two direct paths, but the two clone-derived paths inherit whatever is on the orchestrator, whose initial value is `read_only()` with no channel; that is harmless because `read_only()` grants no browser authority, which is a different fact than the signature enforcing it.
   - **Two comments this same commit falsified.** `block_on_browser`'s doc still said `ToolInvoker::invoke` is synchronous, and SECURITY.md repeated it six lines above the new bullet describing that tool as suspending on an await. Both now describe the split: `browser_tabs` still blocks in place, `browser_open` awaits.
   - **`Stop refused N pending approval(s)` is now reported.** `refuse_all()`'s count was dropped on the floor; Stop answering a question the user was still reading is exactly the kind of thing the action log exists for. `stop_agent` takes an `AppHandle` for it.
   - **Four fifths of the dialog's op-type surface was still inert.** `file_delete` / `command_run` / `git_push` / `git_force` had labels, icons and union members, and no backend path produces any of them — the same "looks like these operations are confirmed" illusion 89 was written about, one layer down. Deleted; `DestructiveOpType` is now `"browser_open"`. `clearConfirm` lost its last caller in 90 and is gone too.
   - **It is a security prompt, so it has to be answerable from the keyboard.** `role="dialog"`, `aria-modal`, focus starting on **Deny**, Escape denying. A stray Enter must not authorize, and "dismiss" must never mean "approve".
   - Rust 377 → 379, frontend 210 → 212.

92. **The heavier disclosure now asks too (2026-09-16)**
   90 put a navigation behind a prompt and left window capture — the biggest disclosure this product has — behind a session switch alone. That ordering was backwards, so `workspace_computer_capture` is now the second consumer of the approval gate.
   - **The prompt names the window that was selected, not the filter the model sent.** `app: "chrome"` can match any Chrome window; a prompt echoing the request would be asking the user to approve something neither of them has identified. So capture is split in two: `resolve_capture_target` selects (and checks the pixel cap, so a window that would be refused anyway never produces a prompt), the user is asked with `CaptureTarget::describe()`, and only then are pixels copied.
   - **The approved window is re-identified before it is read.** An `HWND` is a raw pointer and cannot cross an `await`, so the enumeration runs again after approval — which means "is this still the same window" had to become an explicit check. `capture_approved_target` refuses unless title and app both match; a resize is not a different window, and the pixel cap is re-checked against the new size. Without this, approving "Docs — pricing" and capturing "Signal — Alice" needed nothing more than a focus change between the two steps. This is the failure the whole prompt exists to prevent, and it would have been invisible in a test that only checked "capture happened".
   - **A cross-language contract test for op types.** The `opType` string is the third name in this system with no compiler on either side, and its failure mode is the most expensive: the frontend normalizer drops an unknown value (correctly — a dialog must not mis-describe an action), the backend then waits out its full two minutes, and the user sees "the Agent is stuck". `tests/ipc-contract.test.ts` now extracts every `ApprovalRequest::new("…")` literal from the Rust sources and fails if `normalizeDestructiveOpType` does not accept it. The recurring defect in this repo is adding a name without checking its consumers; this is that check, for the one name that spans both languages.
   - **Both tool descriptions now say a human decides.** A model that does not know a call can block for two minutes and come back refused will retry it in a loop. Saying so in the advertised description is cheaper than any backend guard against that.
   - **`workspace_browser_tabs` and `workspace_computer_windows` deliberately stay unprompted**, recorded here rather than left as an inconsistency for the next audit to find: both disclose a *list* whose scope the allowlist already fixes, and both are called often enough that a prompt each time would train people to approve without reading — which would cost more than it buys.
   - Rust 379 → 381, frontend 212 → 213.

93. **Audit of 92: I re-found the window by title, which this repo already documents as wrong (2026-09-16)**
   The audit found the identity check I shipped was weaker than the one the codebase had already reasoned its way to, and that my justification for it was false.
   - **`services/computer.rs` says it in a comment on the very struct I was calling**: "连 `HWND` 一起留着：截图要对着**这一次枚举里**的那个句柄画，不能事后再按标题找一遍 —— 中间窗口可能已经关了或换了标题，那时'按标题再找'会截到另一个窗口." I read past it and wrote the second title lookup it forbids.
   - **The premise was wrong, not just the conclusion.** "An `HWND` cannot cross an `await`" is false: the *pointer type* is not `Send`, but its **value** is an `isize` and crosses fine. So the fix is what the old comment implied — carry the handle value, and after approval re-read *that window* (`describe_window`) instead of re-running the selector. `ApprovedWindow` now holds the value; `verify_approved_window` refuses if the window closed or if the handle now belongs to a different app, because Win32 recycles handles and "`IsWindow` says valid" is not "still the same one".
   - **What the old check actually allowed**, spelled out because 92 claimed it closed the hole: approve window A ("Docs — pricing"), A closes or its tab changes, another Chrome window is titled "Docs — pricing" — and a page's `document.title` *is* its window title, so the model could arrange that with `browser_open` — the selector re-matched B, `title == title && app == app` passed, and B's pixels went to the model. The ambiguity refusal only covers *concurrent* duplicates, not sequential substitution.
   - **Title equality is now deliberately not required**, with the cost written down. Titles tick (unread counts, playback position, dirty markers), so refusing on any change would have turned approved captures into repeated prompts — the fatigue 92 cited as the reason `computer_windows` stays unprompted, reintroduced two paragraphs later. Identity is the handle; a changed title means the content changed, so the record names the title **at capture time** and adds `(approved as "…")` when it differs from the approved one.
   - **The two tests 92 shipped could not see the defect they were named for.** Both exercised pure helpers whose call sites had no coverage: deleting the identity branch entirely left the suite green. The replacement test pins the decision (closed / recycled handle / ticked title / respelled app name) rather than a comparison operator, and `SECURITY.md` now states plainly that **the capture approval path itself has no automated test** — `allows_capture()` needs `cfg!(windows)` and the resolver enumerates the real desktop, so CI cannot drive it. Claiming parity with the browser path, which is tested end to end, would have been the more expensive kind of wrong.
   - **The op-type contract test repeated the bug the same file had already fixed**: it matched only string literals, twelve lines below the `eventConstants` helper written because event names moved into constants. Generalized to `stringConstants` and used by both. Known caveat, recorded rather than fixed: it scans `#[cfg(test)]` blocks too, so a test fixture inventing a plausible op type would fail the suite for a stall that cannot happen.
   - Rust 381 (one test replaced, not added), frontend 213.

94. **Structured code review of 90–93, and what it found that three self-audits had not (2026-09-16)**
   Five parallel reviewers (reuse / style / reliability / correctness / custom-rules) plus a meta-review over the whole approval feature (`a7ecd8e..HEAD`, 18 files, +1590). 24 findings after de-duplication. The shape worth recording: **the three previous self-audits all looked at "did the mechanism work", and none of them looked at "what happens when the parts around it fail"** — four of the six real defects were in failure paths that no audit had asked about.
   - **Stop could still land in a gap where nobody refuses the request.** Stop is two steps (pull the switch, then `refuse_all`); a request registering *between* them had no one to refuse it, so the UI went idle while the tool call waited out its full 120 s and left no `*_cancelled` record. Checking the switch before asking does **not** close this — the gap is between "checked" and "registered" — so `ask` now re-reads the switch immediately after registering. The fix is inside the gate, not at the call sites, so it cannot be forgotten by the next consumer.
   - **A registered request could leak its slot.** `resolve` / `refuse_all` / timeout were the three clean-up paths; a dropped future (task abandoned, runtime shutting down) was a fourth that nothing covered, and the registry is app-scoped and never rebuilt per run. Now a `Drop` guard, so all four end the same way. Both of these got tests that fail without the fix.
   - **The same-app handle recycle that 93 left open is closed.** 93 fixed "identify by handle, not by title" but verified only the app name, so a handle recycled by *another window of the same app* — Chrome closing one window and opening another, the most likely case by far — still passed. `ApprovedWindow` now also carries the pid, and `verify` compares it. Stated degradation: if the pid could not be read at approval time, the check falls back to the app name.
   - **Approving a dead request looked exactly like approving a live one.** `resolve_agent_approval` returns whether anyone was still waiting, and 90's write-up claimed that mattered — but the UI discarded the boolean. The store now raises the error banner, and a dropped-and-unreadable request is *refused immediately* using the id from the payload instead of being left to time out.
   - **Poisoned-lock handling was fail-closed but silent** — every later approval would be refused with no explanation. Now recovers with `into_inner()`.
   - **Two documentation counts were wrong in the direction of the defect they described.** `SECURITY.md` said "four outcomes" after `Cancelled` made it five — i.e. it dropped exactly the outcome 6075aa2 existed to add — and "five different sentences" counted `Approved`, which deliberately has none. Both fixed.
   - **The review's own severity ranking needed correction.** Seven findings arrived as blocking style violations; four of them (double quotes, `{ a: 1 }` spacing, `(s) =>`, 2-space JSX) are the repo's existing uniform convention with no lint config present, so "fixing" only the changed files would have manufactured the inconsistency the rules exist to prevent. Left alone deliberately, with the real fix named: add a Prettier/ESLint config and format the whole repo in its own commit. AGENTS.md already records what happens when a repo-wide reformat rides along with a behaviour change (`9918d0f`).
   - Also done: the refusal-record shape (`record_external` + `Err`, ~14 hand-written copies) is now one `refuse_external` helper, so "returned an error but forgot to record it" stops being possible; `spawn_blocking` panics on both capture legs now leave a `computer_capture_failed` record; `WindowCapture` holds a `CaptureTarget` instead of copying its four fields; `describe_window` and the enumeration callback share one `window_from_handle`; the op-type contract scanner reuses the constant-resolving helper written twelve lines above it for event names.
   - Rust 381 → 385, frontend 213 → 216.

95. **Reading a page, and what "align with the reference implementation" turned out to mean (2026-09-21)**
   The ask was to align browser/computer use with an external reference implementation (read out-of-tree, nothing from it vendored here). Reading it first changed the plan, so both halves are recorded.
   - **Its Computer Use was not there to copy.** That package ships as a published *placeholder* — every runtime surface reports unavailable and fails closed. What survives in it is only the contract its removed native producer had to satisfy: an accessibility element model, a screenshot/coordinate-frame pair, a signed out-of-process helper, an OS permission preflight. So on the desktop side this repo is *ahead* of what was there, and there was nothing to port. Deliberately not adopted: its capability list is input injection, which is ROADMAP 86 and still unbuilt.
   - **Its Browser Use is real and much larger**: an injected automation script running inside an Electron web view, dozens of wire commands, several backends, all reached by the model through a *single* `js` tool plus REPL-global bridges. That shape does not transfer — it depends on owning the browser. What does transfer is the one gap it made obvious: agent-ide could open a page and list tabs but had no way to **read** one, which `services/browser.rs` itself already named as the next step ("DOM 快照…要走 WebSocket 上的 CDP 会话，那需要新增一个 ws 依赖，属于下一步").
   - **So: `workspace_browser_read_page`.** New dep `tokio-tungstenite` (default features only — no TLS, this socket goes to 127.0.0.1), and the HTTP/WebSocket split is now stated at the top of `services/browser.rs`. It reads a page the user *already has open*; it does not navigate.
   - **Its own pair of gates, deliberately not `allow_browser`.** The precedent is capture-vs-enumeration: a tab list says "you have this site open", the text says what is *on* it, including whatever is only visible because the user is signed in. Reusing the navigation grant would silently upgrade one into the other. Rejected the other direction too — requiring `allow_browser` as well would force a user who only wants "read my local preview" to also hand over navigation authority.
   - **The model cannot supply the expression.** `page_text_expression(max_chars)` takes a number and nothing else. A model-chosen `Runtime.evaluate` argument runs in that site's origin with the user's session — it could read cookies or act as the user — which is not what "read this page" was approved for. This is the whole reason the tool is a fixed extractor rather than a general `evaluate` tool, which is the shape a single scriptable-bridge design would push you toward.
   - **Three smaller decisions that each had a worse alternative.** Truncation happens *in the page* (a multi-MB `innerText` otherwise crosses the socket to be discarded here) and reports the full length, so the model knows what it did not see. Ambiguous filters refuse and list the candidates instead of taking Chrome's first result. A page whose DevTools window is open keeps its place in the list with `ws_url: None`, so "no such page" and "that page's debugger is taken" are different sentences — filtering it out made them identical.
   - **`select_read_target` returns a `ReadTarget`, not the `PageSession`.** Same reasoning as the `Drop` guard in 94: the "cannot happen" branch is better deleted than commented. The allowlist filters *candidates*: pages outside it never match and are never named. (This bullet originally claimed the refusal names only a count — 96 found that true of the no-match branch only, and fixed the hole the ambiguity branch left.)
   - **`agent_tool_permissions` had reached ten positional parameters**, of which eight were `bool, Vec<String>` pairs; the call sites were walls of `false, Vec::new()` and the tests existed partly to catch a mis-ordered one. The four external grants now travel in a named-field `ExternalGrants`, so the eleventh and twelfth arguments did not have to be guessed by position.
   - Rust 385 → 396, frontend 216 → 217. Still no e2e: every new test stops at a refusal or a parser, and the WebSocket exchange itself has no automated coverage — the fake-CDP server proposed after 94 would now need a WebSocket leg too.

96. **Audit of 95: the authority decision was made on a URL nobody re-read (2026-09-21)**
   Adversarial audit of `46a94b3`. Two real holes, both in the same place: the gap between *deciding* and *doing*, which is the gap 93/94 spent two cycles on for window capture and which I did not carry across to the browser.
   - **The origin was checked against a snapshot, and the read was performed against a target.** `/json/list` gives the URL; the debugger socket is bound to the *target id* and survives navigation, including cross-origin navigation. Nothing re-read the URL, and the approval wait alone is up to 120 s — an SSO bounce or a delayed redirect was enough to hand the model a page on an origin nobody allowed, recorded under the *stale* URL. Fixed by making the extraction expression return `location.href` **in the same evaluation as the text**, and refusing if that origin is not the approved one (`verify_read_origin`). Rejected the obvious alternative — a second `/json/list` before reading — because it only narrows the window instead of closing it: the page can navigate between that list and the evaluate. The text does reach this process before the check; it does not reach the model, and the refusal does not name where the page went.
   - **A filterless read was `workspace_browser_tabs` without its grant.** Both arguments are optional, `string_arg` turns `""` into `None`, and the ambiguity refusal lists the matching pages — so a bare call returned the title and URL of every allowed page, before any prompt. With `*` in the allowlist, which the UI actively offers, that is the whole tab list. The precedent I claimed to be following already had the answer: `select_capture_target` refuses a filterless call. Now so does this one. What is *not* claimed any more: that the refusal never names pages. Within the allowlist it does, exactly as capture does, and SECURITY.md now says so instead of the stronger sentence 95 shipped.
   - **A comment asserted a disclosure invariant the line under it broke.** "这一步的失败原因里含候选页面，而一次被拒的调用不该顺带把'你开着这些页面'写进记录" — but `refuse_external` stores the detail verbatim, and only `target` had been sanitized. The information itself is benign (allowlisted pages, shown to the user); a false claim in a security comment is not, because the next audit trusts it. Reworded to say what the code does.
   - **A slow close turned a successful read into a timeout.** `socket.close(None).await` ran before parsing, inside the timeout, so a peer that did not flush its close frame produced "the page did not answer within 10 seconds" about a page that had answered — the exact class of misreport this feature was built to avoid. The handshake is no longer awaited; dropping the socket closes the connection.
   - **Truncation could split a surrogate pair.** JS `slice`/`length` count UTF-16 units, so a cut landing inside an astral character yielded a lone surrogate — not valid JSON, so the whole read failed with "unexpected response". Now `Array.from(...).slice(...)`, which also makes the reported length a code-point count matching Rust's `chars()`.
   - **`validate_page_ws_url` was not bypassable, and the audit proved it rather than assuming it**: the trailing `/` in the expected prefix is what stops `ws://127.0.0.1:9222@evil.example/x`, because userinfo's `@` needs the position that `/` occupies. That case is now in the hostile-input list — without it, deleting the slash left every assertion green.
   - **The fake CDP server finally exists**, because the one defect that had no test (the post-approval Stop re-check) could not be reached without one: a `TcpListener` answering `/json/list`, plus `tokio_tungstenite::accept_async` for the WebSocket leg. Three tests now travel the real path — a full read over a real socket (including that the answer is matched by request id, with an unrelated CDP event sent first), a socket that closes without answering, and Stop landing between the approval and the read. The env-var isolation forced `#[test]` + a hand-built runtime: `env_test_guard()` is a synchronous lock, and clippy is right that holding it across an `await` is wrong.
   - Rust 396 → 401.

97. **The irreversible-action record now survives closing the app (2026-09-21)**
   Sequencing, stated because it was a judgment call: the next capability on the list is input injection (86), and its only compensating control is this record — which SECURITY.md itself called "the open item" because it evaporated on restart. Building the most irreversible capability in the product on a log that a restart erases is the wrong order, so this went first.
   - **What was broken.** `AgentOrchestrator.external_actions` was memory-only. Close the app and "what did it open yesterday" had no answer anywhere — while `ExternalActionRecord.run_id`'s own doc comment promised the frontend would "reconcile it with the restored session after a restart", something no code could do. `publish_external_actions` now appends the newly recorded entries to `~/.agent-ide/external-actions.json`, and `AgentGlobalState::new()` reads the current workspace's entries back before any command can run.
   - **Disk, not localStorage — and this is the part worth arguing.** `diffs` are mirrored into localStorage because the backend holds the authoritative copy and the mirror is a convenience. External actions have no second copy. Putting the only copy of an irreversible-action log inside the webview means page script, or a "clear site data", can erase it. The config directory is additionally on the Agent write deny-list (`.agent-ide`), so the Agent's own write tools cannot reach the file that records what it did.
   - **Refusing to overwrite an unparseable log** is the one thing the file IO does beyond the repo's existing `create_dir_all` + `to_string_pretty` + `fs::write` shape. A corrupt log is read as empty *and left alone*: a file a human might still recover is worth more than a clean start, and "start fresh" here means deleting the evidence. Not done: atomic temp-file + rename. No writer in this repo does it, and doing it for one file would be a claim about durability the other four do not make; the non-atomic write is written down in SECURITY.md instead.
   - **One key function, used by both sides.** `current_workspace()` is read by the load path and the append path. Two call sites deriving the key separately is the shape where records get written under one key and read under another — and that failure looks exactly like "persistence never worked".
   - **`restored: bool` rather than letting the UI do date arithmetic.** `run_id` cannot express "previous session": after a restart every run id is unfamiliar, so `isFromOtherRun` would have tagged restored records with nothing at all (it returns false when either id is missing). The frontend normalizer only accepts a literal `true`, because the wrong default is the dangerous one — labelling something that just happened as history reads as "it didn't happen".
   - **Restored entries go in front, and lose first when the bound bites.** They are older, and the list is read in order; appending them would have shown last week's navigation as the newest thing on screen. When more than 200 come back, the oldest restored ones drop rather than this session's — the in-memory list exists to show the session, the file is the long-term record. Both properties have tests.
   - Shared budget stated rather than hidden: 500 entries on disk across *all* workspaces, so a busy project can evict another's history. Per-workspace files were the alternative and would need a path→filename hash; one file with a `workspace` field on each entry costs one filter and no hashing.
   - Rust 401 → 409, frontend 217 → 219.

98. **Audit of 97: the durable log had three ways to quietly stop being durable (2026-09-21)**
   Adversarial audit of `1a8a6f6`. Duplication and the workspace filter came out clean; everything found was in failure handling, scoping, and coverage — the same shape as 96.
   - **One truncated write ended persistence permanently, and silently.** `read_all` returning `Err` made `append` return early, so a half-written file (SECURITY.md itself said a crash can truncate it) meant *every* later append skipped — compensating control off, UI normal. The two facts were documented separately and their product was not. Now an unreadable log is **moved aside** (`external-actions.unreadable-<timestamp>.json`, timestamped so a second failure does not overwrite the first) and a new one started. Refusing to write and overwriting in place are both wrong for the same reason in opposite directions: one deletes the future, the other deletes the past.
   - **Every IO failure was silent, including write failures.** A read-only config dir, a full disk, or no saved workspace all produced "nothing persisted, nothing said", which on screen is indistinguishable from "the Agent did nothing outside the workspace" — and only one of those has a next step. `append_for_current_workspace` now returns a `PersistOutcome` whose `warning()` the command layer emits as a `warn` action-log entry. The module's own justification for staying silent ("both cases give the user the same next step") was simply wrong, and the repo already has the counter-precedent in `usage_is_unknown()`.
   - **Switching workspace mid-session showed the previous project's record.** `restore_external_actions` ran only at construction and the list was never re-scoped, so after a switch project A's actions stayed on screen and in the Changes badge while new records were filed on disk under B. `save_workspace_path` now re-scopes (and had to become `async` + take the state). A record that names the wrong project is worse than a missing one — same reasoning as the `run_id` decision in 65.
   - **The wiring had no test: deleting either the append call or the restore call left all 409 tests green.** Every new test exercised the module in isolation. `a_stopped_browser_call_is_recorded_but_not_counted_as_performed` now runs with its own config directory and asserts the record reached the file — which also fixes a hazard the same finding surfaced: that test had started doing config-dir IO *without* `env_test_guard()`, so it could write into whichever temp config dir a guarded test was holding and break it.
   - **The 500 bound was asserted nowhere** — the only bound test passed `limit: 2` directly, so the number SECURITY.md states as fact was free to drift. It now runs at `MAX_PERSISTED_ACTIONS`.
   - **The Changes badge became permanently non-zero.** Restored records counted as "performed", so a fresh launch badged Changes with historical count and no action could clear it. The badge now counts this session only; the history stays in the list, and each restored row shows its date — which also answers the audit's point that "previous session" alone cannot distinguish yesterday from three months ago.
   - **Accepted with reasoning, not fixed**: the append runs inside the orchestrator critical section (the drain-and-record invariant is stronger than the cost of a sub-millisecond whole-file write); two app instances against one home directory can lose each other's records (no single-instance guard today); and the durable log is now itself a disclosure surface — full URLs with query strings and window titles, in plaintext, unbounded by age, with no in-app prune. That last one is written into SECURITY.md as a stated trade with "a clear-the-log control" named as the open item, because an audit record that forgets is not an audit record but one that never forgets is a second copy of everything sensitive the Agent saw.
   - Rust 409 (two tests replaced, not added), frontend 219.

99. **A prune control for the durable log, and why it can only reach backwards (2026-09-21)**
   98 left "a clear-the-log control" as the open item, because an audit record that never forgets is a second copy of every URL and window title the Agent saw. This is that control, and its scope is the whole design.
   - **It only removes *earlier sessions*.** `forget_earlier_external_actions` passes the ids of this session's records as `keep_ids`. A control that can erase what just happened makes the record deniable, and a deniable record is not a compensating control for an irreversible action — it is a formality. What the user actually wants is "stop showing me last month", which is exactly the restored set.
   - **It leaves a tombstone.** `external_log_cleared` records how many were dropped and when. A log that silently got shorter and a log somebody trimmed are indistinguishable afterwards; the tombstone is the difference. It survives later clears (it is not an action, it is the note that something is missing), and because it is not an action it is excluded from the "N external action(s)" count via `isBookkeepingAction` — counting it would have been exactly the class of counting defect the last four audits kept finding.
   - **An unreadable log is not cleared.** `forget_earlier_sessions` returns an error rather than writing a fresh file: the quarantine path in 98 exists to keep a file a human might still read, and "clear" must not be the one operation that deletes it for certain.
   - **The frontend re-reads instead of trimming its own array.** What is on screen is whatever the one durable record says; deleting locally first would show "cleared" even when the backend refused. A failure raises the error banner rather than silently leaving the old rows.
   - **The button only appears when there is something to forget**, and its label carries the count ("Forget 12 earlier"). The external-actions block had *no* buttons by design — that rule is about Undo, which cannot exist for a navigation; this control does not claim to reverse anything, and the tooltip says what it does to the durable log.
   - Rust 409 → 412, frontend 219 → 222.

100. **Input injection (86), bound to a frame the model has already seen (2026-09-21)**
   The last big unbuilt capability, and the one `services/computer.rs` had refused to build for a reason it wrote down: "撤不回，而且比导航更狠：它能点掉任何一个确认框" — including this app's own approval dialog. What made it buildable was not new nerve, it was the three cycles that came before: a per-action approval gate, an identity check that survives handle recycling, and a record that outlives the process.
   - **Authority is the fifth independent pair** (`allowComputerInput` + `inputApps`, Windows only). Not folded into capture: reading what is in a window and acting on it are different in kind. Rejected making the switch *depend* on capture — that would read as "capture is part of clicking"; the dependency is enforced at runtime by the frame instead, which is the honest place for it.
   - **Coordinates only exist against a frame.** `computer_capture` now mints a frame id carrying the window handle, pid and size; `computer_click` takes `frame`, `x`, `y` and **has no window selector at all**. This is the whole design. ROADMAP 93 established that re-finding a window by title is wrong for *reading*; for clicking, the same mistake lands a click on someone else's dialog. Pairing an image with the coordinate space it defines is the one idea worth keeping from the reference contract read in 95 — which is otherwise a fail-closed placeholder.
   - **The frame's app is re-checked against the click list**, because the frame was minted under the capture grant and the two lists can differ. Without it, "may screenshot Signal" silently became "may click Signal".
   - **A resized window is refused, and the window must come to the front.** `SendInput` delivers to screen coordinates: a covered window receives nothing while whatever is on top receives the click, so a failed `SetForegroundWindow` is a refusal, not a warning. Rejected `PostMessage(WM_LBUTTONDOWN)`, which needs neither: modern UI frameworks rely on real input-queue state, so messages often do nothing at all — and "clicked but nothing happened" is the worst outcome here, because the model retries.
   - **Out-of-bounds is a refusal, not a clamp** — for the frame and for the desktop. Clamping converts a miscalculated coordinate into a click in a corner, and corners have things in them. Normalization uses `width - 1` so the last pixel column is reachable; dividing by `width` makes the rightmost column unclickable, and that column usually holds the close button.
   - **Move, down and up travel in one `SendInput` call.** Three separate calls let a user's real mouse movement land between them, turning the click into a drag.
   - **`ApprovedWindow::verify` is reused rather than re-implemented**, with the refusal tail ("nothing was captured" / "nothing was clicked") as a parameter. A second copy of those three checks would eventually be missing one, and the one it would be missing is the same-app handle recycle that 94 found.
   - Deliberately absent: typing, key presses, right/double click, drag, scroll, and any reading of the result. After a click the model must capture again — a tool that reported "what happened" would be guessing.
   - Rust 412 → 422, frontend 222. The Win32 call itself still has no automated coverage: the tests cover the gates, the frame binding, the bounds and the coordinate maths, and stop where `SendInput` begins.

101. **Audit of 100: "must come to the front" did not prove the window was in front (2026-09-21)**
   Self-audit of `4e422c1`. The Explore subagent could not be dispatched (DNS), so this was read by hand; the findings are narrower than a full audit would produce and that is worth saying.
   - **The real defect: `SetForegroundWindow` is asynchronous.** A non-zero return means the request was *accepted*, not that the switch has happened. The click was sent immediately after, so it could still land on the window that was on top — the exact failure the check exists to prevent, with the check appearing to prevent it. SECURITY.md's "the window must come to the front" was therefore a claim the code could not produce, which is the same class of defect as 94's and 96's. Now `GetForegroundWindow()` must confirm the window, polled up to 500 ms, and the click is refused otherwise.
   - **The rect is read after activation, not before.** Activating a minimized window restores it at that moment, so measuring first compared the frame against a size that was about to change. Reordering also makes the size check mean what it says.
   - **What the audit confirmed rather than found**, and it was worth checking: the frame's `width`/`height` come from `DesktopWindow.bounds`, which is `GetWindowRect` (`services/computer.rs:249`), and the PNG is encoded at exactly those dimensions (`capture.rs:326`) — so a pixel the model reads off the image maps to the same window-rect offset the click uses. If those two had disagreed (client rect vs window rect), every coordinate would have been quietly wrong.
   - **A test for the click path's identity re-verification.** The click tests used `handle: 0` and never reached `ApprovedWindow::verify`, so the reuse claimed in 100 was untested for clicking. `a_remembered_window_is_verified_for_clicking_too` now pins both the same-app handle recycle and that the refusal says "nothing was clicked" rather than "nothing was captured" — the parameter that made the reuse possible.
   - **The size comparison became a pure `check_same_size`**, so the message that tells the model to capture again is testable without Win32. Previously it lived inside the `unsafe` block where no test could see it.
   - **Written down instead of fixed**: mixed-DPI multi-monitor is unverified. `GetWindowRect` and the `SM_*VIRTUALSCREEN` metrics share whatever DPI view the process has, so they should agree, but this has not been exercised on a scaled secondary monitor; the frame size check is what would catch a mismatch rather than a silent mis-click. Also unchanged: the foreground wait adds up to 500 ms before a click, which Stop cannot interrupt.
   - Rust 422 → 424.

102. **The click is now tested by actually clicking something (2026-09-21)**
   Both 100 and 101 listed the same gap: every click test was a pure function, so the suite could prove the coordinates were computed correctly and prove nothing about whether anything was ever sent. `a_click_reaches_the_window_it_was_aimed_at` closes it — it registers a window class whose `WndProc` records `WM_LBUTTONDOWN`, creates a real visible top-level window on a pump thread, and calls the production `click_in_window` (now `send_gesture`, see 110) from another thread (which is also the production shape: the clicked window belongs to someone else).
   - **Both branches assert**, which is the point. If the session allows the foreground switch, it asserts the window received exactly one click and that the recorded client point is inside the client rect, with the horizontal offset within 32 px of the requested window-rect coordinate — that is the coordinate mapping, end to end, through `SendInput`. If the session refuses to let the window come forward, it asserts that `click_in_window` (now `send_gesture`) **refused** and that nothing was sent. A test that quietly passes when the environment is missing is the failure mode this repo keeps finding; this one has no such branch.
   - **The assertion is "it landed in that window", not an exact pixel.** Border and caption thickness come from the system theme; pinning them would pin this machine. The 32 px horizontal tolerance is about the widest a left border gets.
   - **It prints which path it took** (`injection path exercised` / `not exercised here`), so a future reader can tell from CI output whether the injection was really covered on that machine. On this machine it was: the window was clicked for real.
   - Cost worth stating: the test briefly steals focus, and it needs `Win32_System_LibraryLoader` for `GetModuleHandleW`. It is the only test in the repo that touches the real desktop.
   - Rust 424 → 425.

103. **Audit of the click capability: it would have clicked the wrong monitor (2026-09-21)**
   The subagent audit that 101 could not dispatch finally ran, over `4e422c1` + `04986ac` + `e254cec` together. It found one outright bug that every test and every hand-read had missed, and it falsified two of my own claims.
   - **`MOUSEEVENTF_VIRTUALDESK` was missing.** Absolute `SendInput` coordinates map to the **primary monitor** unless that flag is set; we normalized against the *virtual desktop* metrics. On a single-monitor machine the two are identical, so the bug had no symptom here at all. On any multi-monitor layout the click would land at a compressed position on the primary screen while the target window was confirmed foreground on another — and during an Agent run the middle of the primary screen is very plausibly this application's own approval dialog. Every guard passed: bounds, size check, foreground wait. Fixed, and `absolute_events_are_mapped_to_the_whole_virtual_desktop` now pins the flag so a single-monitor developer cannot lose it again. This is the strongest argument yet for dispatching the audit rather than reading one's own code.
   - **My "no such branch" claim in 102 was false.** The audit ran the test and it printed `injection path not exercised here` — the refusal branch. Both branches do assert, but only one reaches `SendInput`, so whether the suite covers the injection depends on whether the session grants the foreground switch (a `cargo test` from a background shell does not). Reproduced it myself immediately afterwards. SECURITY.md and the test's own doc comment now say "conditionally, and it prints which".
   - **Foreground is not unobscured.** A topmost overlay or a toast can cover a window that *is* foreground. `WindowFromPoint` is now checked immediately before the send and its root must be the target — which also shrinks the window between the foreground confirmation and the send from "three Win32 calls" to almost nothing. What is left is a race, not a hole, and it is written down as such.
   - **Non-success records carried no coordinates.** `refuse_external` stores only kind, target (app name) and the error, so a denied or stopped click recorded "The user denied this action." against `chrome.exe` — unreconstructable, which defeats the purpose of keeping it. Every refusal path now appends where it was aimed. The audit also caught that the prompt shows the capture-time title while the record shows the click-time one, with nothing tying them together; the click path now appends `(approved as "…")` exactly as the capture path does.
   - **Two smaller ones fixed**: a partial `SendInput` left the left button held (the comment named the hazard and did nothing about it — now a compensating mouse-up is sent), and the cursor was left parked wherever the Agent aimed (now saved and restored).
   - **Accepted, written down, not fixed**: a handle recycled *within the same process* passes `verify` (same app, same pid) and only the frame's size check stands between the model and a sibling window — Win32 has no stable per-window identity. The 32 px tolerance in the injection test is too loose to prove the window-rect/client-rect distinction it was justified with (a ~8 px confusion would pass); it is a sanity bound, and SECURITY.md no longer implies more. Normalize→denormalize is off by up to one pixel toward the origin.
   - **The DPI question 101 left open is answered**: the shipped app is Per-Monitor-V2 aware because tao calls `SetProcessDpiAwarenessContext`, while `cargo test` has no event loop and runs DPI-unaware — so the test exercises a DPI mode production never uses, which is the other reason its coordinate assertion stays loose. Mixed-scaling hardware is still unverified.
   - Rust 425 → 426.

104. **The browser HTTP path finally gets tested, and it immediately found a proxy leak (2026-09-21)**
   `open_url` and `list_tabs` had **zero** coverage — an audit noted that years ago in repo time and every cycle since then tested the parsers instead. The fake CDP server built for 98's Stop test made this cheap, so it moved into `services::browser::test_support` (one fake, two users: a second copy would have drifted on "which endpoint answers what") and now three tests travel the real HTTP path.
   - **The finding: `cdp_client()` went through the machine's proxy.** reqwest honours `HTTP_PROXY` / `ALL_PROXY` by default, and this machine has one set. The symptom was absurd enough to notice: a request to a port with nothing listening came back with an *empty response body* instead of connection-refused. Two consequences in production, and the second one is a disclosure — the browser tools fail with unrecognisable errors instead of the actionable "start Chrome with `--remote-debugging-port`", and `open_url` puts the target URL in the request line, so a proxy learns what the Agent is opening. `no_proxy()` now, with a test that sets both env vars at a dead port and asserts the list still comes back.
   - **The tests also corrected me about the endpoint shape.** I asserted `PUT /json/new?url=…`; Chrome's format is `/json/new?<encoded url>` with no parameter name. The assertion was wrong, not the code — but that is exactly the kind of thing a parser test can never tell you, and the method matters: Chrome 111+ rejects `GET` on that endpoint, so getting it wrong means the whole feature is dead on current Chrome.
   - **One of my new tests was itself racy and I fixed it rather than keeping it green by luck.** "Bind an ephemeral port then drop it" gives a port number that a *parallel* test's fake server can immediately claim — the connection then succeeds. Port 1 needs administrator rights to bind, so nothing takes it.
   - Rust 426 → 430.

105. **Sweeping the rest of the HTTP surface after 104 (2026-09-21)**
   The proxy bug in 104 was a *class* of defect (a client built without saying what it should not do), so I checked every other HTTP client in the backend. There are exactly two, and the other one is `LlmClient`.
   - **No proxy change there, deliberately.** The LLM endpoint is remote; a user behind a corporate proxy *needs* it. The CDP client is the opposite case — 127.0.0.1 must never leave the machine. Same builder, opposite requirement, which is why this is a per-client decision rather than a project-wide default.
   - **What it did lack was a connect timeout.** `Client::new()` and the DeepSeek `http1_only()` branch had none, so a dead route or broken DNS left the OS retrying for as long as it felt like — with nothing on screen distinguishing "connecting" from "thinking". Now 15 s, shared by both branches (which differ only in `http1_only`, so the duplication was also removable).
   - **Total and read timeouts stay absent, and that is the interesting half.** A reasoning model can go minutes before the first token, so any "no data for N seconds" rule kills legitimate long generations. A hung response is covered by Stop instead — the `tokio::select!` in `send_with_retry` — which is a button the user holds rather than a number I guessed. The CDP client's 10 s total timeout is right for the same reason reversed: `/json/list` either answers immediately or is not there.
   - Rust 430 (no new tests: the change is a builder option, and a test that asserts "we set a 15 s connect timeout" would only restate the line).

106. **The GDI capture path gets the same treatment as the click (2026-09-21)**
   102 built a real window to test `SendInput`. The same machinery makes the *other* Win32 path testable, and that one had never been exercised either: `PrintWindow` + `GetDIBits` in `copy_window_pixels`. A wrong stride, an upside-down bitmap or a leaked GDI object is invisible to every parser test.
   - **The window helper moved to `services::computer::test_support`** rather than being copied. `computer` is the module that owns window enumeration, so both `capture` and `input` depend on it already; two copies of "create a window and pump it" is the shape where one copy rots while the other keeps working.
   - **Clean-up is now a `Drop` impl**, which also fixes something the 103 audit flagged: the old test joined its pump thread on the last line, so a failing assertion left the window alive — and the next test may be looking for a window by title.
   - **`a_real_window_is_captured_at_the_size_it_reports`** goes from "find it by app name and title" to "decode the PNG and check it against the size the capture reports". Those two numbers come from different paths (`GetWindowRect` via the enumeration, and the bitmap the encoder was handed), so agreement is worth something.
   - **The assertion is deliberately not about pixels.** The test window has no background brush, so what it renders is up to the system and the compositor; pinning colours would pin this machine. What is pinned is that the image describes the window it claims to, at 4 bytes per pixel — a dropped channel shifts the whole image and would look plausible in a thumbnail.
   - Rust 430 → 431.

107. **Narrowing the in-process handle recycle with the window class (2026-09-21)**
   103 found that a handle recycled *inside the same process* passes `verify`: same app, same pid, and only the frame's size check left between the model and a window it never captured. I wrote it off as "Win32 has no stable per-window identity", which is true and was also lazy — there is one more cheap discriminator.
   - **The window class.** `GetClassNameW` is recorded at capture time and compared before the click (and before a re-capture). It catches the most common shape of in-process recycling by a wide margin: a main window closes and a tooltip, popup or menu inherits the handle, which differs by class (`Chrome_WidgetWin_1` versus `tooltips_class32`). It does **not** catch two sibling windows of the same class — that case stays open, and SECURITY.md now says exactly where the line is instead of the vaguer "a window of the same app can still inherit the handle".
   - **Compared only when both sides are known**, like pid: an unavailable class degrades to the previous behaviour rather than refusing everything. The alternative — treating "cannot read the class" as a mismatch — would have made the whole capability fail on any window Win32 declines to describe.
   - **Rejected: comparing the window's position.** A user moving a window between capture and click is normal and the coordinates stay correct (they are window-relative and recomputed from the current rect), so a position check would refuse legitimate clicks. Size is different: it invalidates the coordinates, which is why that one *is* a check.
   - Rust 431 (the new case is an arm of an existing test — `a_remembered_window_is_verified_for_clicking_too` now covers same-app-same-pid-different-class, which was previously a silent pass).

108. **The injected project memory had no guard against its own budget (2026-09-21)**
   `AGENTS.md` is injected into every Agent run and bounded at 8 000 bytes; the file's own header says "keep it under ~7 500 — measure, do not guess". The truncation *mechanism* was tested three ways. **This repo's own copy was not**, and the part that gets cut is the tail — "Where decisions go" — so crossing the line produces no error, just a run that quietly stops seeing the last rules.
   - Measured before touching anything: 6 658 bytes. Then added three traps this session earned (the sync `env_test_guard` in an async test, `no_proxy()` for loopback clients, Win32 identity being four checks and still inexact), which took it to 7 460 — inside the guidance but with ~40 bytes of headroom, i.e. the next line crosses it. Trimmed two over-long bullets back to **7 375**.
   - `this_repos_project_memory_fits_with_headroom` now reads the real file and asserts both the hard limit and a 500-byte margin. A test that only asserted the hard limit would go red exactly when the damage is already invisible; the margin makes it go red while there is still room to think.
   - Worth naming the pattern: the mechanism was well tested and the *instance* was not. Same shape as 98's "deleting the append call left all tests green" and 103's "the e2e never reached `SendInput` here" — a guard that works, protecting something nobody checked.
   - Rust 431 → 432.

109. **Turning "documentation claims must be verified against the code" into a test (2026-09-21)**
   108 named the pattern — a well-tested mechanism guarding an unchecked instance — so this applies it one level up, to the convention AGENTS.md states and nothing enforces. I broke that convention repeatedly this session: two wrong counts in SECURITY.md (96), a claim the code could not produce (103), a "no such branch" assertion the test itself contradicted (103).
   - **`SECURITY.md names every built-in tool the backend can advertise.`** The scanner already existed for command and event names in `tests/ipc-contract.test.ts`; built-in tool names are the fourth string nobody type-checks. Adding a tool and forgetting the document is the same drift as adding an event with no listener, and it is the most expensive kind here: SECURITY.md is the document that answers "what is it allowed to do".
   - **One direction only, deliberately.** Checking the reverse (a document naming a tool that no longer exists) needs an allowlist of non-tool `workspace_*` identifiers — `workspace_tool_call`, `<workspace_root>` — and that allowlist would rot in exactly the way the test exists to prevent. A rename is still caught: the new name is not in the document.
   - **Two premise assertions** (more than ten tools found, and `workspace_computer_click` among them), because a regex that silently matches nothing makes this test pass forever. Same guard the op-type test already carries.
   - It passes on the current tree, which is the point of writing it now rather than after the next omission.
   - Frontend 222 → 223.

110. **From "can see and can click once" to "can operate" — gestures, and one path for all of them (2026-09-21)**
   The desktop side could look at a window and send exactly one left click. That is enough to prove the authority model and not enough to use: context menus, expanding a tree, scrolling a list are all ordinary steps in any real task, and a model that cannot take them will substitute something worse (clicking whatever is visible, or asking the user). This adds double click, right click and the wheel. I chose it over keyboard input deliberately — see below.
   - **No new authority.** All four gestures are irreversible pointer input into an approved window, so they stay behind the existing fifth pair (`allowComputerInput` + `inputApps`). Inventing a sixth pair for "scroll" would suggest scrolling is a lesser power; it is not — moving a page under a button the user is about to press is exactly the kind of thing that pair exists to authorize.
   - **One code path, not four copies.** `computer_pointer_tool` takes a `Gesture` and runs the same checks in the same order: authority, the scroll bound, the frame, the frame's app against `inputApps`, the coordinate bounds, the approval, the post-approval Stop re-check, then the identity re-verify. The alternative — a `computer_scroll_tool` beside the click one — is how one of those ends up missing on one branch, and this file already records that failure mode twice (the desktop tools missing from the cancelled-record list in 99; the append call in 108).

   - **A `Gesture` enum, not `right: bool, double: bool`.** Of those four combinations "right double click" means nothing in any UI, and excluding it with a type is more reliable than checking for it at runtime. `describe()` and `record_kind()` hang off the same enum so the sentence in the approval dialog, the sentence in the record, and the op type are all produced once: "the prompt said one thing and the log says another" is not a bug anyone reports, it is a bug that quietly makes the log useless.
   - **An unknown `action` is refused, never downgraded.** Falling back to a left click when the model asks for something unrecognised performs an action nobody approved — the worst possible guess on a path that cannot be undone. The error lists the accepted names so the model's next attempt is informed rather than another guess.
   - **A scroll is bounded at 10 notches and 0 is refused.** Approval is *per call*, so an unbounded `notches` turns one approval into unlimited authority — "scroll 500" is one prompt. 0 is refused rather than reported as success, because "nothing moved" read as success tells the model the page has ended.
   - **`unsigned_abs`, not `abs`.** `i32::MIN.abs()` panics in debug, and `notches` arrives from model-authored JSON. A parameter out of range must not take the process down.
   - **The partial-send compensation now releases only what the gesture pressed.** The old code always sent a left mouse-up; for a scroll that is a fabricated input event which could complete a button press the *user* was making. `buttons_to_release` returns nothing for a scroll.
   - **Found while writing this:** refusals after Stop recorded the target as `chrome` for clicks, because the default branch in the cancelled-record helper was the browser one. The log would have shown "a click on chrome" for a click aimed at another app entirely. Fixed, and the test now asserts the target as well as the kind — a wording defect in a record is invisible until the moment someone needs the record.
   - **The event sequences are pure functions with their own tests.** `gesture_events` and `buttons_to_release` are testable; `SendInput` is not (the injection e2e only sometimes gets the foreground). "The right button, the right number of times, the right wheel delta" is otherwise observable only by watching the screen, which is to say: not in CI, and not before the action has happened.
   - **`WHEEL_DELTA` lives in `WindowsAndMessaging`, not `KeyboardAndMouse`** in windows-sys, despite every other mouse constant here being in the latter. Noted because the compile error is the only way to find that out.
   - **The op-type scanner needed a third rule.** `tests/ipc-contract.test.ts` matched `ApprovalRequest::new("literal")` and `ApprovalRequest::new(CONST,)`; the new call site passes a variable, so both click and scroll would have silently dropped out of the scanned set — the test would still have passed. That is the 108 pattern again, so: a rule that follows `record_kind`, *plus* premise assertions naming both op types, which is what actually fails if the rule stops matching.
   - **Deliberately still absent:** typing and key presses (a new authority pair, and no frame-like binding for "which control has focus" — a keystroke goes wherever focus is, which is not something a screenshot can pin), drag (two points, and a drop can move or delete), and the middle button (no use case stated).
   - Rust 432 → 441. Frontend stays 223: the new assertions sit in existing tests, because "the dialog understands every op type" is one property, not one per op type.

111. **Audit of 110: the wheel does not land where the prompt said, and three claims the code did not support (2026-09-21)**
   Six findings, all in what the feature *tells the user* rather than in whether it works. That is the pattern this file has been recording since 103: the mechanism is fine and the sentence about it is wrong, and on an irreversible action the sentence is the product.
   - **`WM_MOUSEWHEEL` is not delivered by point.** Windows sends it to the focused control — or, with "scroll inactive windows when I hover over them", to the hovered one. Every guarantee in `send_gesture` is about the *point* (foreground, `WindowFromPoint` ownership, frame bounds) and the prompt said "at (413, 220)", so the user was being asked to approve a precision the backend cannot deliver. It stays within the approved top-level window, so this is not an authority escape — it is the prompt being untrue. Fixed by saying so in three places that already share one source: the approval detail, the tool description, and the reply to the model; plus the module doc and SECURITY.md. Rejected: dropping the coordinates for scroll (the pointer still has to be moved there, and hover routing makes the point load-bearing on the common configuration).
   - **The partial-send compensation released a button the batch never pressed.** `buttons_to_release(gesture)` keyed on the gesture's *definition*, so a batch that stopped at the leading move still got a `LEFTUP` — exactly the fabricated event the same commit removed for scrolls, left in place for clicks, where it can complete a press the *user* was making. Now `buttons_still_held(gesture, sent)` walks the events the batch actually accepted. The old test asserted the wrong property confidently ("only the buttons a gesture pressed"), so it was replaced rather than extended.
   - **The cursor was not restored on the failure path.** Same function, one early return above the restore. Both paths now go through `restore_cursor`; SECURITY.md claimed this unconditionally and was wrong for the partial send.
   - **Seven of eleven non-success exits could not say what was attempted.** One record kind covers left, double and right click, and the string carrying the gesture was only built *after* the approval — so a refused right click and a refused left click were byte-identical in the log, and two exits (unknown `action`, unparseable `notches`) wrote no record at all. Now an `attempted()` wrapper appends the gesture to every refusal, coordinates and title are added as soon as they are known, and both parse-time refusals record. The refused "scroll 500 notches" was the one the bound exists to surface and was precisely the one that left no trace.
   - **The scroll bound lived in one entry point, not on the path.** `check_scroll_notches` was applied in the dispatch arm while `Gesture::Scroll`'s field is public — a second call site would reach `SendInput` unbounded, and `notches * WHEEL_DELTA` overflows past ±17 895 697. Moved into `computer_pointer_tool` with the other checks. The counter-argument (validate at the boundary) is right for shape and wrong for consequence: the boundary is where the value *enters*, the path is where it *acts*.
   - **A model-supplied `url` could label a refused desktop action.** 110 fixed the `chrome` default for clicks but left `string_arg(&args, "url")` ahead of it, so `{"url": "..."}` on a click refused after Stop filed the record under that address. Desktop tools now never consult `url`; the test passes one to prove it.
   - Also corrected: "the same five checks" listing six (it is eight), two stale `click_in_window` references, "1..10" for a range that is ±10, the double-click caveat naming the double-click interval when the real condition is the window class having `CS_DBLCLKS` (the batch has no delay, so timing is never the risk), and a "not implemented: input injection" paragraph 70 lines above the section documenting the shipped feature.
   - **Not fixed, recorded instead:** the op-type scanner reads quoted literals inside `record_kind` and does not consult `stringConstants()`, so a future `Gesture::Middle => MIDDLE_CLICK_KIND` would drop out of the scanned set while the premise assertions still pass. Building constant resolution into that scanner is more machinery than the risk warrants today; the next person to extract those strings into constants needs to extend it.
   - Rust 441 (one test replaced by one). Frontend stays 223.

112. **The three new gestures had no evidence they arrive as themselves (2026-09-21)**
   110 shipped right click, double click and scroll with pure-function tests over the event batches and one real-window test that only ever sent a left click. So "we filled in the right flag bits" was tested and "the system delivers it as a right click" was not — and that second half is the one a person can only check by watching the screen, after the irreversible thing has happened.
   - **A real-window test per gesture**, all four through one `sending` helper. The assertions are specific on purpose: the right click asserts *no left click* arrived, the double click asserts one `WM_LBUTTONDBLCLK` rather than two singles, the scroll asserts the delta is `-2 × 120` so a reversed sign fails. "Some input arrived" would pass for three of the four defects worth catching.
   - **The test window now carries `CS_DBLCLKS`** — the condition SECURITY.md names for a double click working at all. Without it the double-click test would be asserting a version of the claim that has been relaxed to make the test pass.
   - **`TestWindow` is now one-at-a-time.** Its counters are process-wide statics and `cargo test` is parallel; four tests each opening a window would have read each other's clicks. That is an intermittent failure, and intermittent failures end up ignored. A `Mutex` guard held for the window's lifetime, poison-tolerant so one panicking assertion does not turn every later test into "the window would not open".
   - **The counters are tested without `SendInput`.** This is the part that matters: the arrival branch runs *only* when the session grants the foreground switch, and on a background-shell `cargo test` all four take the refusal branch — verified by the printed lines in this session's run. So a wheel delta decoded with the wrong shift, or a double click counted as a left click, would leave all four tests green forever. `the_test_window_counts_each_kind_of_mouse_input_separately` posts the four messages directly and checks each counter plus the signed delta; it runs in every session. Without it the three new tests would be the 108 pattern again — a mechanism tested, the instance not.
   - **Not attempted: forcing the foreground branch.** The tricks that would do it (`AttachThreadInput`, injecting an input event first to break the foreground lock) change the very behaviour under test — the test would pass through a route production does not take. The honest position is that this half of the coverage is conditional, it prints which branch it took, and the evidence it rests on is independently verified.
   - Rust 441 → 445.

113. **Audit of 112: the tests that were supposed to be specific were specific in three places out of four (2026-09-21)**
   The point of 112 was "assert the window got *that* gesture, not that some input arrived". Each test then hand-picked which counters to check, and the picking was uneven.
   - **The click test never checked `double_clicks`.** A `Gesture::Click` that regressed into emitting the four-event double-click batch gives `clicks == 1`, `right_clicks == 0`, `wheel_events == 0` — and the test passes. That is the one gesture pair a user cannot tell apart afterwards: both land in the log as `computer_click`. Fixed structurally rather than by adding the missing line: `sending` now takes a whole `Seen { clicks, right_clicks, double_clicks, wheel_events }` and compares all four at once, so no future gesture test can pick a convenient subset.
   - **The refusal branch summed three of the four counters.** A leaked double click was still caught, but only *by accident* — Windows sends `WM_LBUTTONDOWN` before `WM_LBUTTONDBLCLK`, so the left-click counter moved. Accidental coverage stops being coverage the moment the accident changes. The refusal branch now compares the same four-field struct against zero.
   - **Nothing verified `CS_DBLCLKS` was still set.** The unconditional counter test posts `WM_LBUTTONDBLCLK` directly, which does not need the style, so `style: 0` would have kept it green and only the foreground-only double-click test would have noticed. SECURITY.md claimed the test "verifies the claim as stated" — it did not verify the precondition of that claim. Now asserted with `GetClassLongPtrW(hwnd, GCL_STYLE)`, in the test that runs everywhere. This is the same defect shape as the one 112 was written to fix, one level down.
   - **The one-at-a-time mutex could turn a hang into a permanent hang.** `Drop` joins the pump thread while holding the lock, and there is no per-test timeout in `cargo test`; if the pump ever entered a modal `DefWindowProc` loop (a click on the title bar starts a window-drag loop) nothing would ever release it and every later `TestWindow` test would block until CI's job timeout. The aim point was a literal next to a literal window size, so shrinking either could reach it. `sending` now converts the aim point through `ScreenToClient` and asserts it lands in the client area *before* sending — the hang becomes a red assertion with an explanation.
