Rejected: releasing the lock before the join (a window could then outlive the lock and be found by the next test's title search) and a timeout-bounded join (`std` has none, and a detached pump leaves a live window, which is worse).
   - Confirmed clean by the audit and worth recording: the guard is released after `DestroyWindow` because `Drop::drop` precedes field drops, not because of field order; poison tolerance lets a panicking assertion be followed by a clean run; `CS_DBLCLKS` on the shared class has no ordering dependency (every `open()` re-registers the same literal) and does not affect the capture test or `ApprovedWindow::verify`'s class check; the wheel `wParam` layout is the same for a posted message and a `SendInput` one, so the unconditional test does validate the decode the injection test relies on.
   - `ScreenToClient` lives in `Win32::Graphics::Gdi` in windows-sys, not `WindowsAndMessaging` — the second constant this week that is not where the rest of its family is.
   - Rust stays 445.

114. **Seven commands were registered and unreachable, and only one direction of the contract test was checked (2026-09-22)**
   `lib.rs` registered 97 Tauri commands; seven had no caller anywhere in `src/`. The contract test only asserted *invoked ⊆ registered*, so this was invisible — while the neighbouring event test checks **both** directions and says in its own comment that both kinds of drift are real. That asymmetry was an oversight, not a decision, and it hid a security item SECURITY.md had already written down.
   - **`call_mcp_tool` deleted.** It carried `McpToolPolicy::AllowAll` for a settings-panel "try this tool" button that was never built, so the only way to reach the permissive path was raw IPC. The alternative was to build that button — i.e. ship an arbitrary-MCP-invocation surface nobody asked for, to justify a command that exists. Deleting removes a privilege surface; the policy type stays, because runs still use it.
   - **`update_llm_config` and `file_exists` deleted** — with `llm_profiles::update_default_profile` and `infer_provider`, which had no other callers. `update_default_profile` was worth reading before deleting: it rebuilt the default profile from three fields and **silently discarded** every per-profile setting (token caps, pricing, tool-call mode). `save_llm_profile` supersedes it.
   - **`disconnect_mcp_servers` deleted — and that exposed a real gap, which is now fixed.** My first justification for deleting it ("the registry already shuts servers down on reconfigure") was **false**, and the audit caught it: `shutdown_all` has exactly one caller, `discover`. Disabling or deleting a server in the panel only wrote the config file, so its child process kept running with its tools still registered; and after removing the *last* server the Discover Tools button is disabled, leaving no in-app way to stop anything. `save_mcp_config` now calls a new `McpRegistry::retain_configured`, which stops and forgets exactly the servers that should no longer be running — not `shutdown_all`, because saving a config should not silently restart every other server and swap out the tool list mid-session. The decision of *which* servers to drop is a pure function (`servers_to_drop`) with a test, since the real path needs child processes. Deleting the command was right; the reasoning for it was wrong, and the wrong reasoning was hiding a broken toggle.
   - **`clear_agent_conversation` wired to the control that already existed.** TaskView has a **Clear** button; it reset the frontend session and left the backend's conversation intact, so the next prompt still carried the previous task's `conversation_digest()` into the model context. The UI said "fresh start" and the model kept talking about the old task, with nothing visible to explain it. `clearAgentSession` is now async, clears `conversationTurns`, and reports failure of the backend half — because that failure has no other symptom. This is why "wire it" beat "delete it" here: the affordance was already on screen and half-done.
   - **That error had nowhere to appear, which was a second defect.** Only ChatView and DiffView render `error`, and they are *mutually exclusive tabs* with the Plan tab that holds the button — so the failure message was written to state nobody could see. TaskView now renders `error` too. Worth stating generally: an error written to a store field is only surfaced if some mounted component renders it, and "which tab is open" is part of that.
   - **`get_agent_steps` and `get_agent_sdd_artifacts` wired into `reconcileBackendRun`**, the same reconciliation `restoreDiffs` already does and for the same reason: both live only in memory on the backend while the frontend restores them from `localStorage`. Without it, Run/Skip lands on a step the backend has never heard of — a row of buttons that error with `Step not found`. The overwrite condition is `matched || length > 0`: my first version was `length > 0` alone, which leaves the old task's steps on screen when the run *did* match but replanned to nothing — and the banner in that state says "Backend run matched", so the user has no reason to doubt the rows.
   - **The scanner made me undo my own abstraction.** I first routed both reads through a `reconcile(command, apply)` helper; the new test immediately flagged both commands as unreachable, because the name had become a variable. That is not a false positive — every name-based tool in this repo, including a human with grep, loses the same trail. Inlined both with literal command names, and the helper is gone. Same lesson as the op-type scanner in 111, arriving from the other direction.
   - **The scanner's own premise floor was too low**, and test files counted as callers. `> 20` on a set that should hold 93 means a parse that finds a third of the list still passes the reverse check, since the unparsed names are simply absent; and a command invoked only from a `*.test.ts` is still dead in the product. Both tightened.
   - **The exemption list is empty and should stay empty.** A command either has a UI entry point or it does not exist. If an IPC-only command is ever genuinely needed, it goes in that list with a reason — the friction is the point.
   - Also removed: the store's `reset` action, which had no caller (`clearAgentSession` is the one the UI uses).
   - Frontend 223 → 226; Rust 445 → 446. Registered commands 97 → 93.

115. **The silent plaintext key fallback, removed (2026-09-22)**
   The credential write-up above lists three fixes; its third — "Remove the silent plaintext fallback" — had been sitting unimplemented. `LlmProfile::api_key()` preferred the plaintext `api_key` field over the keyring entry, so one failed migration left `config.json` holding a key *and* winning over the keyring from then on — the worst of both storage models, which is exactly what the keyring was added to avoid. (Item 1, storing the reference explicitly, shipped. Item 2 shipped **half**: the panel probes whether the read succeeded, but still never shows *which* reference was read.)
   - **Order reversed: keyring first, plaintext never by default.** A plaintext-only profile now fails with a message naming the profile, where to re-enter the key, and the opt-in variable — and, because re-entering will also fail on a machine whose keyring is broken, it says that too rather than letting the user discover it by trying.
   - **`AGENT_IDE_ALLOW_PLAINTEXT_KEY` is the escape hatch**, the shape the same write-up proposed. An environment variable rather than a config field, because the config file *is* the plaintext's hiding place — putting the switch in there lets the thing under suspicion sign its own permission slip. `0`, `false`, `no` and `off` all count as off; a variable named ALLOW set to `no` meaning "allowed" would be its own defect.
   - **Both halves of a failed keyring read are reported.** When the entry cannot be read *and* a plaintext key exists, the error carries the keyring error and the reason the plaintext was skipped. With only one half, the user fixes the wrong side.
   - **Provenance is visible, and the panel now uses a decision rather than a string.** Settings shows `sk-1****7890 (plaintext in config.json)`. The first version of this fix relied on `has_readable_api_key`, which has exactly one caller in the backend and is never sent to the frontend — so the panel, which decides "saved" by comparing the mask against the literal `"not configured"`, still rendered a green *(saved)* badge for a key that would fail every run. That is the trap recorded earlier in this file, half-reintroduced by the fix meant to close it. `LlmProfileResponse` now carries `api_key_usable` and the panel reads it.
   - **The eye toggle deliberately bypasses the gate.** Routing `reveal_api_key` through `api_key()` produced "Cannot read stored key" directly under a mask proving it had been read. The gate governs whether we *send* a key to a provider; showing the user their own file is a different question.
   - **The file is still not rewritten when the store fails.** Deleting the user's only copy of a key to satisfy a policy would be worse than the policy violation. The fix is about what we *use*, not about destroying what they have.
   - **Test note:** "keyring first" needed its own test and did not have one — the two tests written first both used a deliberately-missing entry, so reverting the order would have kept them green. `the_keyring_value_wins_over_a_plaintext_one` stores a real secret under the test-only service name and asserts the keyring value wins with the opt-in both off and on.
   - Rust 446 → 450.

116. **The token meter and the model disagreed about what history exists (2026-09-22)**
   `AgentContext.conversation` was filled in exactly one place — `send_agent_prompt`'s claim section — while `build_agent_context` hardcoded `conversation: None` for every caller. Two consequences, both user-visible:
   - **The context panel under-reported.** `estimate_agent_context` measured a context with no history while the prompt that followed carried up to 6 turns. The gap grows with the conversation, so the number is most wrong exactly when the user is consulting it to decide whether to cut context. (Scope, corrected in 117: what now matches is the *context section set*. The system prompt, the accumulated stage transcript and the tool schemas are still unmeasured.)
   - **Single steps ran cold.** "Generate a plan, then press Run on each step" never saw any history — neither reading it nor writing it — so `Run` on step 3 could not know what step 2 did, and the `(original task not recorded)` fallback in `verify_workspace` / `repair_workspace` had nothing to fall back to for anyone who worked that way.
   - **The fix is a signature, not three assignments.** `build_agent_context` now takes `conversation` as a parameter. The field default was the defect: a struct literal with a plausible `None` in it reads as complete, and two of three callers forgot. As a parameter, forgetting does not compile. (Rejected: a `with_history()` builder — it moves the same forgettable step one call later.)
   - **Reading the digest before claiming the run is *not* safe — see 117.** This entry originally justified moving the read out of the claim section with "only a claim holder writes history". That is false, and 117 puts the read back inside it.
   - **Continuing is not a new question.** Pausing already recorded that ask; continuing it should update that turn rather than push a second copy, or the 6-turn window fills with duplicates. `record_continued_turn` does that, comparing on the *truncated* form since that is what history stores — a 400+ char prompt would otherwise fail to match itself. (117 fixes which turn it updates, and corrects the claim that the paused outcome is always the "no file changes produced" placeholder: a pause after an implementing stage records real files.)
   - **A step enters history as a sentence, not as the template.** `format_single_step_prompt` output is a full prompt with step type, scope, execution mode and output rules; stored verbatim it would spend the 400-char budget on boilerplate. `single_step_history_prompt` records `Ran step: <title>` plus the extra instruction when there is one. (The title is the *first* line of the template, so it would not have been truncated away — 117 corrects that overstatement, and bounds the extra instruction, which was the real leak.)
   - Corrected in this file: the claim at Known Issues 36 that "cancel, error, `run_agent_step` and `continue_agent_pipeline` add nothing" — only the first two are still true, and deliberately so: a cancelled or failed run has no outcome worth feeding forward.
   - Rust 451 → 455, four new tests. (This entry first said "five new tests; 450 → 455", copying a stale baseline instead of measuring. `git grep -E "^\s*#\[(tokio::)?test\]" -- src-tauri/src` counts 451 at the parent commit and 455 here; `cargo test --lib -- --list` agrees on the endpoint.)

117. **Audit of 116: the safety argument was false, and the duplicate it prevented could still happen (2026-09-22)**
   An Explore audit of `232d112` found six defects. All are fixed here, plus the wording corrections folded into 116 above.
   - **"Only a claim holder writes history" is false.** `truncate_agent_conversation` and `clear_agent_conversation` mutate `conversation` with no lease, and 116 had moved the digest read *out* of the claim section — across two `.await`s in `send_agent_prompt`, and in `run_agent_step` the digest was already baked into `ctx_str` sixteen lines earlier. So pressing Send and then *Cut from here* could leave the strip showing the turns as gone while the run that started a moment later still sent them. Both reads are back inside the claim critical section; the context (and its git-diff/project-tree enrichment) is now built *after* the claim, which also stops that work from being done for a request that gets rejected.
   - **`record_continued_turn` only looked at `last`.** A pause does not invalidate `paused_run`, so the user can ask something else and then press Continue — and the turn to update is no longer last. It now searches backwards for the matching ask, so the update lands on the turn the UI is showing (the strip cuts by id) instead of pushing a near-duplicate whose sibling's outcome stays frozen at the pause.
   - **`repair_workspace` could launder the "no task recorded" placeholder into real history.** With an empty conversation and no supplied description, `resolve_original_prompt` returns `(original task not recorded)`; recording that as a turn makes it `conversation.last()`, and the *next* verify/repair inherits it as the original task — a diagnostic marker promoted to a real instruction. It is now a named constant (`ORIGINAL_PROMPT_UNKNOWN`) and is never recorded. A repair that ran **zero** iterations (checks passed immediately, no model call, no change) is not recorded either.
   - **The unbounded half was the one nobody looked at.** `single_step_history_prompt` bounded the template and passed `extra` straight through — and `regenerateDiff` builds `extraPrompt` from hunk JSON plus the entire file body, so a *Regenerate* click wrote 400 characters of source into the digest and resent it on every later prompt. The extra is now its first non-empty line, capped at 100 chars.
   - **A step's result now says what that step did.** `turn_outcome()` describes the whole review area, so three consecutive steps produced three identical `result:` lines. `record_step_turn` appends the step's own `new_diffs` count, which `record_step_success` already returns and the action log already uses.
   - **A step turn can never become the "original task".** Six one-at-a-time steps evict the user's actual prompt from the 6-turn window, after which the repair fallback would have handed the model `Ran step: <last title>` as the task description — worse than the placeholder, because it looks real. `ConversationTurn` gained `derived`, and the three fallback call sites now go through `last_task_prompt()`, which skips derived turns. Derived turns still reach the model: continuity for the step-by-step workflow was the point of recording them.
   - **The test that let the `extra` leak through could not fail on it.** Its assertions were two `contains` calls plus `template.len() > recorded.len() * 2` — a 5.7× margin against a 2× threshold, i.e. incidental shape. It now asserts the exact string, the bound on a realistic `regenerateDiff` payload, and the `Ran step: ` prefix the write-up advertises.
   - **The panel never re-estimated when the conversation changed.** With the conversation now a measured section, `Clear` and *Cut from here* — the two controls whose only purpose is to change it — left the token row frozen. `ChatView` subscribes to `conversationTurns` and the estimate effect depends on it.
   - Still open, recorded rather than smuggled in: the estimate omits the system prompt, the growing stage transcript and the tool schemas, so the panel's number remains a floor, not the total.
   - Rust 455 → 457.

118. **A measured context meter, and a task title with a real source (2026-09-22)**
   Pushed back on: why is this slow when there is a reference implementation available to study? The honest part of the answer is that the two gaps recorded in 116/117 were both solved shapes elsewhere and nobody had looked. So this cycle settled the contract first and did two items in one batch instead of one item per audit. (Everything below is our own code; the reference was read out-of-tree and nothing from it is vendored.)
   - **Context usage: what the design has to be, and why.** The number a user reads must come from the *provider's reported usage*, not a local estimate — the two have different units (an estimate is derived from character counts) and putting them in one place invites treating them as cross-checks. "Used" is input **+** output, because the window is shared. An unknown window means the row is **hidden**, not rendered as 0%. A local estimator that covers the system prompt, tool schemas and the growing transcript is worth having, but as an input to *proportions* and to a compaction decision — not as the headline figure.
   - **Taken: the split, not the estimator.** `RunUsageMeter` now also records the **last** reported request (`last_prompt_tokens` / `last_total_tokens`) next to the accumulated totals, because the accumulated number is *spend*, not *occupancy* — a six-stage run sums past any window and would render as a lie. `RunUsageSnapshot::context_meter()` returns `None` when nothing was reported, so local runtimes and mock endpoints produce no row at all instead of a permanent 0%. `emit_usage_action_log` emits `agent-context-usage`; `ChatView` renders `Measured last request: 12,500 / 128,000 tokens · 10%` on its own line, and only when the profile's window is known. The estimate line stays exactly as it was, one line above and clearly labelled — two numbers, two units, never added together.
   - **Not taken: a character-ratio tokenizer, and auto-compaction.** A ratio estimator with CJK weighting is better than nothing, but replacing this repo's estimator would rewrite every existing budget test for a number that is still an estimate; the measured line is the honest improvement. Auto-compaction needs a summarisation call and a place to store the summary — a separate feature, and 36 already records why "compress" here means "cut".
   - **The task title now has a defined source.** The ranking worth having is: first user input truncated (≤60 chars, else 57 + `...`), stored when the task starts; then optionally an async model-generated title, gated to the first turn and to inputs long enough to be worth summarising; then a user rename that sticks. Tier 1 is what this repo needed and now has (`deriveTaskTitle`, first non-empty line, whitespace collapsed, 60/57 rule). Tiers 2 and 3 are deliberately skipped: a sidecar title call costs a request per session, and a "renamed by hand" flag with no rename UI would be an inert field.
   - **`Task` was trimmed, not deleted.** The earlier proposal to delete it was wrong and is withdrawn: `TaskView` and `AgentRunSummary` both *read* `currentTask.title`, so deleting it would have frozen the Plan header at the literal `"Agent Task"` forever. What was actually dead is now gone — `tasks`, `addTask`, `setCurrentTask`, and `Task.steps` / `Task.affectedFiles` / `Task.status`, none of which any code ever wrote. A task *list* is a sessions index, a much larger feature than a field.
   - **Restore is normalized.** `normalizeRestoredTask` accepts only `{id, title}` as strings, and the "is this session worth restoring" check now uses the *normalized* value — otherwise a stale payload from an older build could revive an empty session whose task then arrives as `null` anyway.
   - Rust 457 → 458; frontend 226 → 233 across 27 files.

119. **Audit of 118: the percentage divided by the wrong number, and "last request" was the wrong request (2026-09-22)**
   Eight defects, all fixed here. Two of them make 118's own wording wrong, corrected below rather than left standing.
   - **The denominator was an input budget, not the window.** `effective_input_tokens()` is `max_context − reserved_output − 512`; the numerator is input **+** output. So the row divided a whole request by a shrunken input allowance, and `Math.min(100, …)` was quietly capping the result — a clamp hiding a unit mismatch. Now it divides by `maxContextTokens`, the real window, and the clamp is gone: a number above 100% would mean something is wrong and should be visible. **Correction to 118 and to its commit message**, both of which claimed the row already used the window and showed `12,500 / 128,000` — a figure `effectiveInputTokens` cannot produce for a 128k model.
   - **"Last reported request" is routinely the *smallest* one.** Inside a stage the prompt grows, but the transcript carried *between* stages is capped (`MAX_CARRIED_THREAD_CHARS = 24_000`), so a Coder stage that burned twelve tool rounds is followed by a bounded Reviewer request — and that one won. Single-step runs and repair loops are worse: a few hundred tokens overwriting a full pipeline. The meter now keeps the **peak** (`fetch_max`), which is what "how full did the window get" actually means. **Correction to 118**, which justified "last" as occupancy.
   - **The row never reset between runs.** A run that reports no usage (local runtime, mock, or a failure before the first reported call) left the previous run's number on screen under a label claiming it was this one's. `sendPrompt`, `runAgentStep` and `continueAgentPipeline` now clear `contextUsage` when they start.
   - **The emit had no test in either language, and nothing pinned the key casing.** Dropping `#[serde(rename_all = "camelCase")]` would make `normalizeContextUsage` reject every payload and the row would silently never appear — with all five commands green. `the_context_meter_reaches_the_frontend_with_the_keys_it_expects` uses `RecordingEvents` to assert all three cases (no calls, calls but no usage, usage) and reads `payload["peakTotalTokens"]` by name; the frontend test adds a snake_case payload as a negative.
   - **`lastPromptTokens` was plumbed end to end and never rendered.** Deleted rather than displayed: `ContextMeter` now carries one number.
   - **The two rows were two lines apart in the same style, sharing a denominator.** Relabelled `Provider-measured peak request`, separated by a rule, and it no longer borrows `effectiveInputTokens` — so the estimate line and the measured line no longer share a single number that means different things.
   - **`deriveTaskTitle` cut UTF-16 code units.** `"x".repeat(56) + "😀"` produced a lone surrogate, i.e. a `U+FFFD` in the header. It now slices code points; a test asserts no lone surrogate survives.
   - **`"Agent Task"` was still reachable, and an empty derived title erased a good one.** Only `sendPrompt` sets a title, which is correct — a single step, a continue and a repair all continue an existing task — so the fallback is now `"No task yet"` instead of a literal that reads like a real task name, and a whitespace-only prompt keeps the previous title instead of nulling it. `docs/agent_ide_ui_design.md` still documented the deleted `status` / `steps` / `affectedFiles`; fixed.
   - **Two design claims in 118 were overstated**: a provider-anchored meter *prefers* reported usage and still needs a fallback when a response omits it, rather than "never" touching an estimate; and the tier-1 title in the shape 118 described is stored once when the task starts, whereas this port re-derives it per prompt. The per-turn behaviour is deliberate — there is one conversation here and the header should name the current ask — but it is a deviation and is recorded as one.
   - Still open: a per-*request* occupancy number would need the meter to know which call is the main turn and which are sidecars; the peak is the honest approximation without that. And the estimate still omits the system prompt, the transcript and the tool schemas.
   - Rust 458 → 459; frontend 233 → 234.

120. **Sessions: "new session" and "session history" had no entry point because sessions did not exist (2026-09-22)**
   Reported as a UI problem — 新建会话和历史会话不知道在哪里操作. It was not a discoverability problem. There was exactly one conversation, it lived only in `AgentOrchestrator.conversation` (in memory), and the only control that touched it was a `Clear` button inside the restored-session banner, which appears only when there are restored steps. Both things the user was looking for were genuinely absent.
   - **What a session is here, and what it deliberately is not.** A session is the model *context*: the bounded turns plus a title and timestamps (`agent/session_store.rs`, `~/.agent-ide/sessions.json`, workspace-scoped, newest first, capped at 50). It carries no steps and no diffs. A diff describes what was on disk at one moment; restoring one a day later mostly means showing a plan and a review queue that no longer match reality, which is the failure this product exists to prevent. `resumeSession` therefore clears steps/pipeline/SDD instead of leaving the previous session's, and both the command doc and the UI copy say file changes are not restored.
   - **Clear and New are one action, not two.** A "wipe the context but keep the session id" mode looks identical on screen (the chat empties) but silently swaps the contents of a row the user can click back into. `start_new_agent_session` clears the turns *and* mints a new id; the old session is already on disk, so nothing is lost. `clear_agent_conversation` was deleted rather than kept as an alias.
   - **Lazy persistence.** Nothing is written until the first turn, and a session whose turns are all cut is removed from disk. A row you click into that has nothing in it is pure noise.
   - **Switching sessions mid-run is refused.** A run records its turn into *the current session* when it finishes, so switching during a run files today's ask at the end of a three-day-old conversation. `run_in_flight()` reuses `try_begin_run`'s liveness rule (the claim's `Weak` still upgrades) rather than `current_run_id`, which is `None` whenever the caller omitted a run id.
   - **`next_turn_id` is persisted with the session.** Restarting it at 1 on resume would let a new turn collide with a restored one — the user clicks "cut from turn 5" and turn 1 disappears instead. Turn ids are the only way to express "cut the context here".
   - **A failed write is reported where history is read.** `persist_session` cannot propagate an error (the turn already happened), so it records it on `session_persist_error` and `list_agent_sessions` hands it to the panel. Otherwise the failure has no symptom at all until the next launch shows an empty list.
   - **Entry points, since that was the actual complaint**: two icon buttons in the Agent panel's utility row (`New session`, `Session history` → the new `sessions` view), the renamed button in the restore banner, and two palette commands (`Start a New Agent Session`, `Open Agent Session History`) — the palette is the only textual surface for 8px icon buttons, and `CommandPalette.test.tsx` now pins both titles.
   - Reference contract adopted: a provider-style session list (updated-desc, capped, one row per session with something recognisable on it), clear≡new, lazy save, resume replays the stored history into context, and the recorded session's own metadata wins on resume. Not adopted: SQLite, fork/`parentID`, compaction boundaries, and a background-generated title with a provenance field — the title is the first user line, kept sticky so head-eviction cannot rename a session later.
   - Still open: a session carries no model/branch/cwd, so one recorded in another workspace is filtered out of the list rather than reconciled; there is no rename; the transcript rebuilt on resume is derived from the bounded turns, so it is a summary of the conversation rather than the original message stream.
   - Rust 459 → 468; frontend 234 → 242 across 28 files.

121. **Audit of 120: the frontend cleared before the backend agreed, and a failed delete took the context with it (2026-09-22)**
   Nineteen defects, the load-bearing ones fixed here. Two of them make 120's own wording wrong and are corrected below rather than left standing.
   - **`delete_agent_session` destroyed the live context before the delete could fail.** It called `start_new_session()` first, then `session_store::remove(&id)?`. A failed remove (corrupt file, read-only) returned `Err` with the conversation already cleared and the id already swapped: the UI reported a failure and kept showing turns the model could no longer see, and the only recovery path — resuming that session — now returned older content than what had been live. Order reversed: remove first, then switch.
   - **"New session" wiped the view before the backend agreed, and the refusal was invisible.** `startNewSession` cleared state and *then* invoked; when the backend refused mid-run the user got an empty, idle session while the run kept going with its old context — and `isStreaming: false` also hid the Stop control. It now invokes first, mutates only on success, and rethrows so the history panel can show the refusal; the three fire-and-forget call sites swallow the rejection deliberately because the store has already put it in `error`. **Correction to 120**, whose commit message presented the refusal as a guarantee the UI honoured.
   - **A turn recorded with no workspace open was silently never written.** `persist_session` treated "no workspace key" as success, so the history stayed empty forever with no warning — indistinguishable from "saved". It is now an error, matching `external_log`, and `AgentSessionList` carries `sessionsAreSaved` so the panel says *why* the list is empty instead of showing the same emptiness for three different causes.
   - **An unreadable `sessions.json` was an empty list, a wrong error message, and a permanent write failure.** `list`/`find` swallowed the read error (the panel said "No saved sessions yet" while the file was right there), `resume` reported "no longer on disk" for a session that was on disk, and every later `upsert` failed forever because nothing moved the bad file aside — the exact defect `external_log` was changed to fix, reintroduced. Now: quarantine on write, read errors surfaced through `warning`, and resume distinguishes "could not be read" from "not there".
   - **The rebuilt transcript attributed the Agent's own turns to the user.** `derived` exists to separate "what the user asked" from `Ran step: …`, the backend serializes it, and the normalizer dropped it — so resuming a session that ran plan steps showed those lines as things the user typed. `derived` now survives normalization and those turns render as agent messages.
   - **Writes were not atomic and the title rule was not the rule it claimed to be.** `write_all` now goes temp file + rename (a crash mid-write used to truncate all 50 rows), and `session_title_from` collapses internal whitespace and cuts at >60→57+"..." exactly like `deriveTaskTitle`, which it claimed to match but did not (58–60 characters and repeated spaces produced two different titles for one session).
   - **Smaller ones**: `record_continued_turn` wrote the file twice on the new-turn path; `session_persist_error` outlived the session it belonged to (a new session inherited the old one's warning); the panel only refreshed on a streaming edge, so opening it during a run showed nothing — it now loads on mount and has a refresh button; `clearMessages` and the initial state seeded two different welcome texts, so "fresh session" had two looks — one `welcomeMessage()` now, and the unused action is deleted; the stale comment naming `clear_agent_conversation` now names the three commands that actually mutate history without a lease.
   - **Correction to 120 on diffs**: the store comment said steps *and diffs* are cleared on resume; only steps/plan/SDD are. Keeping the diffs is the right behaviour — they are real pending changes on disk and must stay reviewable and undoable — so the code was left alone and the comment, the resume banner and this log now say so.
   - **Tests the audit demanded**: a Rust test serializing `AgentSessionList`/`AgentSessionDetail` and reading `activeId` / `updatedAt` / `turnCount` / `lastOutcome` / `derived` by name (without it, dropping `rename_all` would make every row read "unknown time · 0 turns" with all five commands green); a `run_in_flight` test, since 120 presented the mid-run refusal as a core guarantee while nothing exercised it; the title-parity cases; and a store test asserting the view survives a refused switch.
   - Still open: `session_store` does unlocked read-modify-write on one file, so two instances sharing `~/.agent-ide` still lose each other's sessions (documented in the module header and `SECURITY.md`, which also now records that `sessions.json` holds prompt text in plaintext with no age bound); the list read happens under the orchestrator lock; orchestrator tests that record a turn now touch disk through a process-global config dir, mitigated by making the store's own tests filter by an id prefix rather than by making 20 tests take the guard.
   - Rust 468 → 472; frontend 242 → 244.

122. **An empty answer from a reasoning model: the streaming path said nothing, and the error pointed the wrong way (2026-09-22)**
   Reported from a real run: `finish_reason=length, content_chars=0, reasoning_chars=14710`, followed by the question "can Max output just be left unset by default?".
   - **It already is unset by default.** `LlmProfile.max_output_tokens` is `Option<u32>` defaulting to `None`, and `build_chat_request` only inserts the key when it is `Some` — so a fresh profile sends no output cap at all. Unsetting it therefore cannot be the fix: the cap that truncated that run was the **provider's own default**, which for reasoning models is often a few thousand tokens and can be consumed entirely by the thinking block.
   - **The old message pushed the user in the wrong direction.** It explained `finish_reason=length` but never said which limit applied, so the natural reading was "turn Max output off" — which was already the state that produced the failure. `empty_response_error` now names the two cases separately: with a cap it prints the field actually sent (`max_tokens=1024` / `max_completion_tokens=2048`, per `output_token_key`) and says raise it; without one it says leaving it empty does not remove the limit, it hands it to the provider, and a reasoning model usually needs 8k or more.
   - **The streaming path had no such message at all.** `StreamChoice` never deserialized `finish_reason`, reasoning deltas were discarded without being counted, and a stream that ended with no content and no tool calls returned `Ok("")` — the Agent "finished" having said nothing, with nothing in the log to explain it. Only the non-streaming path (`prefers_non_streaming`, currently just `deepseek/deepseek-v4-flash`) could produce the error the user saw. Both paths now share one function, so the same failure reads the same way.
   - **Rejected: inventing a default output budget.** The reference implementation always sends an explicit budget, but it can only do that because it carries a per-model registry of window and max-output limits (bundled JSON, per-provider catch-all, a model with neither is refused rather than defaulted). Guessing a number here would send a value some models reject outright with a 400, so omission stays the default until there is a registry — and the remedy is stated at the point of failure instead.
   - **Also rejected: retry-on-length.** The reference recovers by continuing a truncated response up to three times; ours has no continuation mechanism, so a retry would re-roll the same prompt and pay twice for the same doomed request. Recorded here rather than half-built.
   - The settings placeholder read "4096", which looked like the default that would be applied; it now reads "provider default", and the help text says Max output is the only one of those four numbers that reaches the provider.
   - Still open: `LlmUsage` drops `completion_tokens_details.reasoning_tokens`, so "the thinking cost this much" is only visible as a character count inside an error string; there is no per-model registry, no `reasoning_effort` / thinking-budget parameter, and no truncation recovery.
   - Rust 472 → 473; frontend unchanged.

123. **Audit of 122: "unset by default" was only true of the Rust struct, and one empty round threw away a whole tool loop (2026-09-22)**
   Twelve defects. The two that change conclusions are corrections to 122 itself.
   - **Correction to 122: a profile built through the UI is *not* unset.** Every provider preset in `SettingsPanel` carries `defaultMaxOutputTokens` (OpenAI/Azure/DeepSeek 4096, Anthropic 8192, local 512) and selecting a preset writes it into the form, which is then saved. `Option<u32> = None` is the struct default, not the default of a profile anyone actually creates. So 122's diagnosis of the reported run — "the cap was the provider's own" — is unproven: a preset-built DeepSeek profile would have sent `max_tokens=4096`, which a 14 710-character thinking block fills just as easily. The settings copy now says the presets fill it in, and what clearing the box actually means.
   - **A single empty round was aborting the entire tool loop.** `execute_with_tools` used `?` on the model call, so after the streaming path started returning `Err` for an empty response, one empty final round discarded `merged` **and** the transcript — every `role: "tool"` result already paid for, possibly after side-effecting MCP calls — and reported the stage as failed, with the earlier tokens still on screen. It now treats that as the loop's end when earlier rounds produced something, appending the reason; cancellation still propagates untouched, because the caller classifies it by that exact string.
   - **The advice was unconditional, so it pointed the wrong way whenever nothing was truncated.** The new streaming branch fires on *any* empty stream — `stop`, `content_filter`, a dropped connection — and the message still said "raise Max output". `finish_reason_is_truncation` now gates it: `length` / `max_tokens` / unknown get the budget advice (unknown counts, since truncation cannot be ruled out), while an explicit normal stop says the output limit is *not* the cause and points at the prompt, the tool protocol or a filter.
   - **The terminal chunk was being thrown away.** `StreamChoice.delta` was required, so a chunk shaped `{"choices":[{"index":0,"finish_reason":"length"}]}` — what several OpenAI-compatible gateways send, and the only chunk carrying the reason — failed to deserialize and was silently dropped by `if let Ok(parsed)`. That is the same trap `choices: #[serde(default)]` was added for. `delta` is now optional, and `index` is read so the error reports the number of choices actually seen instead of a hard-coded `1` (a stream that carried none now honestly says `0 choice(s) … [no choices]`).
   - **`reasoning_chars` only understood one spelling.** DeepSeek's `reasoning_content` was handled; OpenRouter-style `reasoning` was not, so on those gateways the message printed `reasoning_chars=0` while explaining that a large `reasoning_chars` was the cause. Both structs now take `alias = "reasoning"`.
   - **Discarded tool-call fragments were reported as "no tool calls".** `ToolCallAccumulator::finish` drops any call whose name never arrived; the diagnostics now carry `incomplete_tool_call_fragments=N`, because "the stream was cut mid tool call" and "the model produced nothing" need different fixes.
   - **`test_llm_connection` called a working endpoint a connection failure.** A reasoning model with a small cap made the probe return the empty-response error, and the panel said the connection failed when endpoint, key and model were all fine. That case now reads "Reached the provider, but it returned no answer".
   - **The CLI could only ever hit the "no cap" branch, whose advice named a setting it does not have.** `LLM_MAX_OUTPUT` is now read there, and both branches of the message name it alongside Settings → Max output — an instruction the reader cannot follow is worse than no instruction.
   - **A Stop landing between the last chunk and the empty check was reported as a content failure.** The flag is re-checked immediately before the check, so a cancellation stays a cancellation. (Still open: this file writes the `"Agent task cancelled"` literal in ten places instead of `orchestrator::CANCELLED_ERROR`; importing it would point `services` at `agent`, so the drift is recorded rather than papered over.)
   - **Test quality**: the previous test asserted the copy's phrasing. It now asserts facts — the field name and value that were sent, that no value is quoted when none was sent, that `LLM_MAX_OUTPUT` is named, and that a `stop` finish never mentions the output limit — plus a pure `stream_empty_diagnostics` covering the fragment case, since the real SSE path needs an HTTP server to drive.
   - Still open: `finish_reason` and `reasoning_chars` are accumulated across choices rather than per `index` (latent — nothing in the repo sends `n>1`); `LlmUsage` still drops `completion_tokens_details.reasoning_tokens`; no per-model registry, no thinking-budget parameter, no truncation recovery.
   - Rust 473 → 475; frontend unchanged.

124. **Reasoning tokens were invisible, so an empty answer looked like a billing error (2026-09-22)**
   Follow-on from 122/123, which left this as the open item: the truncated-empty failure charges completion tokens like any other answer, and nothing in the product could say how many of them went into thinking.
   - **`LlmUsage` silently dropped the field.** OpenAI/DeepSeek report `completion_tokens_details.reasoning_tokens`; a few gateways put `reasoning_tokens` at the top level. Serde discarded both, so the only trace of a 14 710-character thinking block was a character count inside an error string. `reasoning_tokens()` now reads the nested field first and falls back to the flat one — recognising only one spelling would show 0 at exactly the moment the number matters.
   - **It is a breakdown, not an addition.** Providers already count reasoning inside `completion_tokens`, so the meter accumulates it separately and `total_tokens` stays `prompt + completion`. Adding it would trip the per-run token cap early and inflate the spend estimate — a cap that fires on a doubled count is worse than no cap, because the user cannot tell which number is wrong. The test asserts both halves: the number is visible *and* the total did not move.
   - **Where it surfaces, and where it deliberately does not.** The `run_token_usage` action-log detail gains "(of which N reasoning, already counted in completion)" and the status-bar hover gains one sentence; the 24px status-bar label is untouched, because a third number there would crowd out the two that need watching live. The line only appears when N > 0 — on a non-reasoning model a permanent `reasoning: 0` is noise. Both wordings say "already counted", since the obvious misreading is "I was charged twice".
   - **The estimate row now admits what it leaves out.** `estimate_agent_context` measures the assembled *context block* only: the system prompt, the tool schemas and, inside a pipeline, the messages accumulated by earlier stages are all absent, so the real request is always larger. The row is labelled "selected context", which was accurate but easy to read as "the request"; a tooltip now names the three missing parts and points at the measured row as the number that covers everything. Counting them for real is still open (119) and needs a different path — the role prompt, `output_rules` and the tool definitions are reachable, but MCP tools depend on which servers are connected, so a number that changes with server state would need its own explanation.
   - Still open: `reasoning_tokens` is not priced separately (some providers bill thinking at the completion rate, others differently — pretending to know would be a fabricated cost); the estimate still does not include the request overhead; `finish_reason` / `reasoning_chars` remain accumulated across choices rather than per `index`.
   - Rust 475 → 476; frontend 244 → 246.

125. **Audit of 124: the tooltip I added to prevent false confidence created some (2026-09-22)**
   Ten defects. The first one contradicts 124's own copy, so it is corrected here rather than left to read as true.
   - **Correction to 124: the measured row is *not* "the provider's count for the whole request".** It renders `peak_total_tokens`, a `fetch_max(prompt + completion)` over the run — so it includes the model's **output**, and it belongs to the largest single call of the *last* run, not to the request being estimated. The file's own comment fourteen lines below said exactly that. The tooltip now says the two numbers are not comparable and why; 124's claim that it "points at the measured row as the number that covers everything" was wrong in the same way.
   - **The tooltip also under-listed the gap.** It named the system prompt, the tool schemas and earlier stages' messages; the stage user message also carries the user's own prompt and the pending diffs sent for review, which can be tens of KB. Both are named now — a list that reads as exhaustive but is not is worse than an explicit estimate, because it invites arithmetic.
   - **A flat `reasoning_tokens` larger than `completion_tokens` is no longer reported as being inside it.** The nested `completion_tokens_details` shape is a breakdown by protocol; the top-level spelling some gateways use has no such guarantee (some count thinking in the total only). Feeding `{"completion_tokens":80,"reasoning_tokens":3900}` used to print `Completion tokens: 80 (of which 3900 reasoning, already counted in completion)` — an impossible sum on its face, which sends the reader to doubt the accounting itself. That value is now dropped, with a test; 124's unqualified "providers already count reasoning inside completion_tokens" is true of the nested shape only.
   - **A payload carrying only reasoning tokens was discarded whole.** `is_empty()` was not extended, so such a call counted as "provider reported nothing": the number was lost *and* `reported_calls` stayed put, making the run say "Token usage not reported by provider". Exactly the silent-drop class 124 exists to close.
   - **The number was still missing from the surface that needs it most.** The empty-response error printed `reasoning_chars` only, while the usage — already parsed and already recorded — sat in scope. Both paths now append `reasoning_tokens=N` when the provider reported it: characters say "the thinking was long", tokens are in the same unit as the output cap and can be compared to it directly.
   - **The new IPC key was unpinned in a hand-written `json!`** — the third time this repo has been bitten there (context meter, session list). A typo would make the hover sentence vanish with all five commands green. `state_payload_carries_run_usage_without_faking_a_price` now records reasoning and asserts `usage["reasoningTokens"]`.
   - **The cost half of the claim was untested.** 124 said adding reasoning into the total "would trip the per-run token cap early and inflate the spend estimate", but the test built a meter with no pricing and no cap, so neither consequence was exercised. It now has both and asserts the spend equals the prompt+completion figure with the cap still passing.
   - **`pub reasoning_tokens` next to `fn reasoning_tokens()`** meant the field could be read by accident and would be `None` for every mainstream provider. Renamed to `reasoning_tokens_flat` with `#[serde(rename)]`, so the accessor is the only reachable reading.
   - Left as noted: the caveat is a `title` attribute on a non-focusable `<div>`, consistent with the rest of this file but unreachable by keyboard — the row is display-only, and a focusable annotation pattern would be a separate change across every row that has one.
   - Rust 476 → 478; frontend unchanged.

126. **The budget inputs never said "tokens", and the box would not accept how people write large numbers (2026-09-22)**
   Reported as *配置 Context Budget Estimate 单位是什么，用户输入不够友好*. Both halves were true: the section was titled "Context Budget Estimate" and the four fields were labelled `Max context / Reserved output / Max output / Per-run cap` with the unit stated **nowhere** — the only clue was a placeholder like `128000`, and the sibling section next to it is in dollars, so guessing was not safe.
   - **The unit is now in the section headers**, where it covers every field at once: "Context Budget — all four are token counts" and "Per-Run Spend Cap — in US dollars". Per-field labels stay short because the grid is three 10px columns; each one gained a `title` saying what it actually controls, which the long prose paragraph below could not do per-field.
   - **`type="number"` was the unfriendly part, not the labels.** A number input refuses letters and commas, so `128k` and a pasted `128,000` are both untypeable — the only way to enter a window was to count zeros, and `128000` vs `1280000` are nearly identical at 11px while differing by an order of magnitude in every budget line that reads them. The token fields are text + `inputMode="numeric"` now, parsed by `parseTokenInput` (`k`/`m` suffixes, grouping characters ignored).
   - **Each token box echoes what was understood**, in monospace under the field, turning red when the text parses to nothing. That is the point of allowing shorthand: the user has to be able to confirm that `128k` became 128,000 without trusting the parser blindly. Empty input shows no echo — four permanent "not set" lines would be noise.
   - **`0` reads as "not set" now, because that is what the backend does.** `inputToNumber` already discarded it; the echo makes the behaviour visible instead of leaving the user believing they had set a cap of zero.
   - **The effective-input line states its own arithmetic** ("max context − reserved output − 512") and the unit, so the number is checkable rather than magic.
   - Money fields stay `type="number"` with decimal steps: `$/M` prices need `0.000001` granularity and nobody writes dollars as `1.5k`. `inputToNumber` is deleted rather than kept for them — `usdToMicros` already owns that parsing.
   - Not done: no per-model window lookup that could fill Max context in for you (same missing registry as 122), and no validation that Reserved output < Max context — the estimate already clamps at 0, and an inline error for an impossible pair is a bigger design question than this round.
   - Rust unchanged; frontend 246 → 252 across 29 files (`src/utils/tokenInput.test.ts`).

127. **One word for one thing: the panel called the same object a task, a session and a chat (2026-09-22)**
   Reported as *task plan 之间还不知道怎么用…怎么新开 new task 和查看历史 task 都不清楚*. This was not a missing feature — 120 shipped both controls — it was a vocabulary failure, and a sharp one:
   - The first tab was labelled **Task** but rendered `ChatView`; the file named `TaskView.tsx` is the body of the **Plan** tab; the tab test ids were crossed (`task` → `agent-tab-chat`, `plan` → `agent-tab-tasks`). Meanwhile the container you start and revisit was labelled **session**. So "task" pointed at the chat, the thing the user calls a task had another name, and the Plan view's own empty state said "Start a conversation in **Chat**" — a tab that did not exist by that name.
   - **Settled on one word: task.** Tabs are `Chat | Plan | Changes`; the utility buttons are `New task` and `Task history`. This matches how the reference implementation's desktop UI names the identical object (`titleBar.menu.file.newTask`, `workspaceSidebar.workspaces: "Tasks"`, `commandCenter.section.recentTasks`) while its CLI calls the same row a session — one concept, named for the surface. Ours now does the same, and `AgentPanel` carries the mapping note so the next reader does not "fix" it back.
   - **The IPC commands stay `*_agent_session`.** Renaming them buys the user nothing and costs a sweep across the command registry, the contract test and four store actions. What the identity needed was a written-down mapping, not a migration.
   - **The empty chat is now the only explanation of how the pieces fit**, because nothing else explained it: describe a change → steps appear under Plan where you can run/retry/skip one → file writes queue under Changes to review or undo → New task starts fresh, Task history brings one back. The previous text ("Welcome to Agent IDE. I'm your AI coding assistant…") told a first-time user nothing about the four tabs it sits next to.
   - Also aligned: the palette entries (`Start a New Agent Task`, `Open Agent Task History`, with `tasks` as a keyword), the restore banner's button, the resume system message, every tooltip in the history list, and the three empty states.
   - Rejected: merging Plan into Chat. They answer different questions — "what is the model going to do, and can I change it" versus "what did we say" — and the plan rows carry per-step controls (scope, execution mode, Run/Retry/Skip) that would be unusable inside a transcript. The reference keeps them separate too (an inline read-only todo block plus a pinned progress panel), and its plan list is *read-only*, which ours deliberately is not.
   - Still open: `AgentRunSummary` shows `currentTask.title` while the history row shows the session title, and after Stop the header keeps the stopped task's title next to an empty plan (`stopAgent` clears steps but not `currentTask`); single-step runs and repair never set a title at all. There is still no "past tasks" list distinct from the conversation list, and no rename.
   - Rust unchanged; frontend 252 tests across 29 files (labels only, no new tests — the palette test's two titles were updated).

128. **Stop was throwing away what the run had already produced (2026-09-22)**
   Went looking at 127's open item "after Stop the header keeps the title next to an empty plan" and found the cause was much worse than a stale label: `stop_agent` ran `orch.steps.clear(); orch.diffs.clear(); orch.sdd_artifacts.clear();` — no comment, no test — and `stopAgent` in the store mirrored the wipe.
   - **This made the product's core failure reproducible with one click.** Tool writes are published back as `applied` diffs carrying their pre-write content and an undo checkpoint, precisely so a user can see and undo them. Press Stop after the Agent has written a file and the review area empties while the file on disk stays changed — "disk changing while the Diff view stays empty is the failure this product exists to prevent" (AGENTS.md). Proposed-but-unreviewed diffs and a half-written SDD draft went the same way: Stop means "do not do more", not "discard what is done".
   - **It was also non-deterministic.** A draining run publishes its tool writes on the cancellation path; whether they survived depended on whether that publish landed before or after Stop's `clear()`.
   - **Now**: `note_run_stopped` marks any `doing` step as `error` with a `Stopped by you.` log line, leaves every other step and all diffs/artifacts alone, and re-emits `agent-plan-ready` — without that the Plan panel keeps spinning on a step whose run is gone, since that event is the only thing it listens to. The command layer then writes an action-log entry naming how many steps were stopped and how many pending changes are still waiting, because "Stop kept your changes" is not visible from an empty status line.
   - **Why `doing` → `error` and not a new status**: `TaskStep.status` is an unvalidated `String` shared over IPC, and the frontend only styles the five values it knows. Inventing `stopped` would render as an unstyled row on the old renderer; the log line carries the distinction that matters.
   - The store now keeps `steps` and `diffs` too, and only clears `agentRunId` / `restoredSession` — that run really did end, and reconciliation should not re-adopt it.
   - Still open from 127: `AgentRunSummary` shows `currentTask.title` while the history row shows the session title, so the two can disagree; single-step runs and repair still set no title; no rename.
   - Rust 478 → 479; frontend 252 → 253.

129. **The three budget fields already had defaults; the panel just never said so (2026-09-22)**
   Asked directly: *这三个数值能不能给一个默认，我看很多 agent IDE 都不用设置*. Checking before answering: **two of the three already had one.** `reserved_output_tokens.or(max_output_tokens).unwrap_or(4096)` has been the rule in both budget formulas all along, and Max output is filled in by whichever provider preset you pick (125). Only the window had none — and because `effective_input_tokens` returned `None` without it, the row read "not set", which made the whole group look mandatory.
   - **`Max context` now has an assumed value for the estimate**: `ASSUMED_MAX_CONTEXT_TOKENS = 128_000`, labelled "128k assumed" in the placeholder and named in the help text. Assuming **low** is the safe direction — the budget looks tighter and warns earlier, never the reverse.
   - **It is deliberately not a per-model table.** I went looking for real numbers first and the public sources contradict each other for the same model names (one DeepSeek family documented as 128K/8K in one place and 1M/384K in another; Claude and GPT tables disagree across aggregators and shift release to release). Hardcoding that would pin a snapshot that is wrong on arrival, and **a wrong window is worse than an assumed one** because the percentage next to it looks trustworthy. The honest version of this feature is asking the endpoint (some model-list APIs expose `context_length`), which is a separate piece of work.
   - **The assumption is confined to the displayed estimate.** `estimated_input_tokens_from_budget` still returns `None` when the window is unset, so the context packer trims against nothing rather than against a guess — cutting a user's context on an invented window would be worse than not cutting. The outgoing request is untouched: `max_tokens` is still only sent when Max output has a value.
   - **The magic numbers are named and shared**: `DEFAULT_RESERVED_OUTPUT_TOKENS`, `CONTEXT_ASSEMBLY_HEADROOM_TOKENS`, `ASSUMED_MAX_CONTEXT_TOKENS` in `services/context.rs`, mirrored in `src/utils/contextBudget.ts` with a test pinning both sides to the same 123,392 for an unconfigured profile — the settings panel has to compute this before anything is saved, so the formula genuinely needs two implementations, and the previous pair had drifted into three copies of `4096` and `512`.
   - `LlmProfileResponse.effectiveInputTokens` went from `Option<u32>` to `u32`: an Option that is never `None` is the kind of shape the audits keep catching.
   - Section header now says all four are optional, and each field's tooltip says what happens when it is empty.
   - Rust 479 → 480; frontend 253 → 258 (`src/utils/contextBudget.test.ts`).

130. **Correction to 129: a global "assumed 128k" was the wrong shape (2026-09-22)**
   Challenged in one line — *现在都是百万 token 了，你确定 128k* — and the challenge is right. 129's own justification ("128k is the common floor, and assuming low is safe") does not survive contact with the current field: flagship windows are 200k to 1M, so a fixed 128k is wrong most of the time, and wrong *low*. That does not fail safe: it makes the estimate cry wolf, claiming the budget is ~123k when the model actually has a million. A false alarm costs the same trust as false confidence — the user starts ignoring the row, or trims context that never needed trimming.
   - **`ASSUMED_MAX_CONTEXT_TOKENS` is gone.** `effective_input_tokens` returns `Option<u32>` again and the row reads "unknown" with a one-line instruction, because "we do not know your model's window" is the true statement. (129 turned that Option into a `u32` to avoid an Option that is never `None`; now it legitimately can be, so it is back.)
   - **The fallback is the preset the user already picked, not a constant.** `defaultMaxContextTokens` already exists per provider preset (openai/azure 128k, anthropic 200k, deepseek, local) and is already written into the visible box when you select a preset — so for preset users nothing was ever missing, which is also why the global assumption was solving a problem that barely existed. When Max context is empty the estimate now borrows that preset's number **and says so in the sentence**, naming the preset and the figure, so an inherited number can never be mistaken for a measured one.
   - **The preset numbers are labelled as conservative floors, not lookups**, in the copy and in the field tooltip. DeepSeek's 64,000 was not a floor of anything — no source I found lists it — so it moved to 128,000, the smallest window that family's public docs actually mention. Where the vendor now ships 1M, the user sets it; the box is right there.
   - **Still no per-model table, for the reason 129 gave and this round re-confirmed**: the sources contradict each other for the same model names, so a table would be wrong on arrival. The difference is that a *preset floor the user can see and edit* does not pretend otherwise, while a hidden global constant did.
   - Rust 480 (test rewritten, count unchanged); frontend 258 → 259.

131. **The limits belong to the model, not the provider (2026-09-22)**
   Asked whether `defaultMaxOutputTokens` needed the same treatment as the window. It did, and the question exposed that the *shape* was wrong in both cases.
   - **Per-provider defaults cannot be right.** Inside one vendor, output caps differ by two orders of magnitude — a small vision/flash variant may allow ~1k output while the flagship allows 128k — and the same model id is served by the official endpoint, Azure, and a dozen gateways. Any single number attached to "OpenAI" or "DeepSeek" is therefore wrong for some of that vendor's own models.
   - **`utils/modelLimits.ts` is a model-keyed table**, ordered most-specific-first (`gpt-5.4-mini` before `gpt-5`), matched case-insensitively against the id with gateway prefixes tolerated (`openai/gpt-4o`, `deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4-Flash`) — gateway users are exactly the ones with no preset to lean on. An unrecognised model returns `undefined` and the estimate says "unknown"; that stays the rule from 130.
   - **Picking a model now fills both boxes**, and changing the model updates them *only* where the field is empty or still holds the previous model's value. A hand-typed number survives a model switch; a number that plainly belonged to the last model does not, because leaving it there makes the estimate disagree with the real ceiling invisibly.
   - **The three `default*Tokens` fields are gone from the provider presets** (they carried 128k/200k/64k windows and 4096/8192 outputs from an older model generation — the `deepseek` window of 64,000 matched no published figure at all). Presets now carry only endpoint, model list and tool-call mode. The one case where a preset value is still right is **Local GGUF**, where the ceiling comes from the `n_ctx` you loaded with and has nothing to do with the model name, so `handleProviderChange` prefers the table and falls back to the preset.
   - **Both directions of a wrong Max output are now visible**, which is what makes shipping a table acceptable: too small truncates and the empty-response diagnostic says so (122/123), too large is a plain provider 400. Neither fails silently, and the value sits in an editable box.
   - The numbers were cross-checked against two independent sources per family and the conflicting ones were left out rather than averaged; o-series and Gemini are absent for that reason.
   - Still open: nothing refreshes this table — the honest fix remains asking endpoints that publish `context_length`. And `Max context`/`Reserved output` are still display-only; only `Max output` reaches the provider.
   - Rust unchanged; frontend 259 → 264 (`src/utils/modelLimits.test.ts`).
132. **The output limit is now per request, not per model (2026-09-22)**
   Adopted from the reference implementation's `resolveNormalRequestMaxOutputTokens`: it caps each request's output budget at `min(model's declared max, window − estimated usage − margin)`. Ours had only the first half, which makes one class of failure unavoidable.
   - **`prompt + max_tokens > window` is a 400, and the limit itself is legal.** A million-token model may allow 384k output, but once the prompt fills 900k of the window, asking for 384k is rejected outright. Max output answers "how much can this model write"; the provider checks "does this request fit". The two disagree in exactly the long-context case where the run matters most.
   - `window_limited_output_tokens` (pure, tested) clamps to `window − estimated prompt − 1024`, where the margin exists because the prompt size is a **character-based** estimate and images are not counted at all — an under-estimate would turn into the very 400 this is meant to avoid.
   - **Two refusals to clamp**, both deliberate: no window configured (clamping against a guessed window would shorten answers for no reason), and a remainder under 512 tokens (the local estimate is not authoritative; squeezing an answer into 200 tokens on its word is harder to diagnose than a provider saying "does not fit"). The second is the reference's rule too — a local estimate never overrides the provider's own verdict.
   - **A clamp is never silent.** `OutputClamp { requested, sent }` accumulates on the client like image drops do, and `output_clamp_report` turns it into one action-log line naming both numbers, how many times it happened, and what to do (shorten the context, or raise Max context if the real window is larger). Without it the user sees a short answer while Settings still says 384000, and suspects the model.
   - **`Max context` finally does something.** It was display-only ("budgeting only, never sent"), which is still true — it is not sent, it now *protects*. `LlmConfig` carries it, `to_config` passes it through; the CLI leaves it unset.
   - Deliberately not done: continuation after a truncated answer. The reference auto-continues up to three times; we have no continuation mechanism, so a retry would re-roll the same prompt and pay twice (122/123 already rejected retry-on-length for that reason).
   - Rust 480 → 483; frontend unchanged.
133. **Audit of 128-132: Stop stopped detecting cancellation (2026-09-22)**
   Nine defects. The first is a regression 128 introduced and the audit caught before it shipped further.
   - **Cancellation had been detected by accident, and 128 removed the accident.** `record_stage_outcome` decided "this run was cancelled" by *failing to find the step id* — which worked only because `stop_agent` cleared `steps`. Once Stop started keeping the plan, the lookup always succeeds, so a stage whose response had fully arrived would take the orchestrator lock after Stop and run the whole success path: status → `done`, diffs pushed into the review area, pipeline stage → `completed`. The user would see the step as stopped while the backend called it done, and the cancelled stage's diffs would surface later as if the *next* run had produced them. Now the cancel flag (already a parameter) is checked before any mutation, and the step-missing branch is documented as what it actually is: the context was truncated.
   - **The test for it was passing against a premise that no longer existed.** `a_stage_finishing_after_stop_lands_nothing` simulated Stop with `steps.clear()`. It now drives the real path — `note_run_stopped()` plus a raised cancel flag — and additionally asserts the step stays `error` rather than being flipped back to `done`.
   - **Re-running a stopped stage inherited the old step's logs.** `ensure_stage_step` matches by title, so with the plan surviving Stop the next run appended to a step that still held the previous response excerpt and the `Stopped by you.` line. It now resets `status` and `logs` when it adopts an existing step.
   - **Stop's own log over-counted.** It reported `diffs.len()`, which includes applied, rejected and reverted cards, so a run whose changes were already applied announced "3 pending change(s) … still waiting". Now `reviewable_diff_count()` — the same `is_reviewable_diff_status` filter the rest of the product uses — and the log only fires when something is genuinely waiting.
   - **The Chat view showed `0` where Settings said "unknown".** `?? 0` on the estimate row turned "we do not know the window" into a confident zero, contradicting 130's whole point and `contextBudget.ts`'s own comment. It now prints "unknown", matching the settings panel for the same profile.
   - **Five shipped preset model ids had no table entry**, so selecting them *cleared* both budget boxes (Azure's default `gpt-4`, plus `gpt-4-turbo`, `gpt-4-32k`-class ids and `gpt-3.5`/`gpt-35-turbo`). 131's claim that picking a model fills both boxes was false for them. Rules added for the legacy generation; the ordering puts them after `gpt-4o`/`gpt-4.1` and before the bare `gpt-4` catch.
   - **`claude-opus-4` was sharing sonnet-4's output cap** (64k vs its real 32k) — the exact "one number for two families" error the table was built to remove, and Max output is the field that reaches the provider, so it would have earned a 400. Split, with a test.
   - **A hand-typed value could still be replaced silently.** `parseTokenInput("abc")` is `undefined`, and for an unrecognised previous model `before?.contextWindow` is also `undefined`, so `undefined === undefined` let the overwrite through. `shouldAdoptModelLimit` now requires the current text to parse *and* to equal the previous model's number.
   - **`ASSUMED_MAX_CONTEXT_TOKENS` was still in the tree**, dead but `pub` (so no dead-code warning), carrying a doc comment arguing against the per-model table that 131 shipped. 130's message said it was gone; only its use was. Deleted, and the three comments that still described the removed preset fallback now point at `utils/modelLimits.ts`.
   - Process note: two of this round's edits were made with PowerShell string replacement because the file tools were failing on the large Rust files. One of them silently broke a comment (a backtick-n inside a here-string became a newline) and produced code that did not parse; `cargo clippy` caught it. Shell text editing on source files is a last resort, and it needs a compile immediately after.
   - Rust 480 → 482 passing (the stop test was rewritten, not added); frontend 264 → 265.
134. **Gap analysis against the reference, and the first thing it turned up: a patch tool (2026-09-22)**
   Compared the reference implementation's whole user-facing surface against ours (its 19 slash commands, ~40 model tools, subagents/skills/workflows, session features, permission modes) and picked the item with the worst cost-to-effort ratio: the Agent had **no way to change part of a file**.
   - **`workspace_write_file` was the only editing path, and it replaces the whole file.** Changing one line meant regenerating the entire file, which costs output tokens proportional to file size, requires the model to hold the whole file in context first, and puts every untouched line at risk of drift. The original reasoning for whole-file writes is still in the code (patch location failures were the dominant old failure mode), and it was right at the time — the answer is not to remove it but to give the model the narrow tool as well, ordered first, with the descriptions steering: edit for part of an existing file, write for new files and near-total rewrites.
   - **`workspace_edit_file` is exact-match only.** The reference cascades through eight strategies (quote normalisation, line-number-prefix stripping, escape normalisation, trimmed lines, flexible indentation, block anchors with 0.8 similarity). Adopted: CRLF/LF normalisation for the comparison. **Rejected: every fuzzy one.** They exist to rescue a model that copied the snippet imprecisely, and the rescue can land the edit somewhere the model did not mean — a wrong edit that reports success is strictly worse than a refusal, in a product whose premise is that you can see what happened. The line-number-prefix strategy is also unnecessary here: our `workspace_read_file` returns raw text, so there are no `123: ` prefixes to strip.
   - **Ambiguity is refused, not resolved.** Zero matches asks the model to re-read; more than one without `replace_all` reports the count and the two ways out. Silently taking the first occurrence is the most dangerous default available: the model believes it changed the place it was looking at, and both sides call it a success.
   - **Line endings survive.** The file is written back with the ending it already had. Writing LF into a CRLF file would make one edit show up as "every line changed" in the review area, which is the same class of failure as not showing the change at all.
   - **Undo records the bytes from disk**, not the normalised copy, so an undo restores the file exactly — including its original line endings.
   - Same gate (`allow_write`, i.e. Auto mode), same `resolve_for_agent_write` deny list, same `record_write` trail, and added to the post-Stop side-effecting refusal list. Editing a file that does not exist is refused with a pointer to `write_file` rather than silently creating it, so `allowFileCreate` cannot be routed around.
   - Recorded gaps not taken this round, in the order I would take them: reasoning-effort control (`reasoning_effort` / dialect equivalents per model), `AskUserQuestion` mid-run, conversation compaction (ours is a fixed 6-turn window), `Glob`/regex `Grep`, WebFetch/WebSearch, four-level permission modes, workspace checkpoints with rewind, subagent delegation, model switching without saving a profile.
   - Rust 483 → 487 (486 passing, 1 ignored); frontend unchanged.
135. **Reasoning effort: a tier the user can set, and a degradation they can see (2026-09-22)**
   Second item from the gap list (134). Reasoning models were reachable but not *steerable*: nothing in the product could ask for more or less thinking, which is also why an empty answer at `finish_reason=length` (122) had no remedy other than raising Max output.
   - **A tier, never a token budget.** The reference implementation is unambiguous here: `reasoningLevel` is a closed enum per model, completely decoupled from `maxOutputTokens`, and nothing anywhere reserves tokens for thinking. Copied exactly — `reasoning_effort: Option<String>` on the profile, sent as `reasoning_effort` on the request. "How many tokens may it think for" is not a sendable quantity; thinking and the answer share one output budget, which is the whole reason 122 happened.
   - **`off` is sent twice on purpose.** Some gateways only understand `enable_thinking`, and "do not think" is the one intent plain `reasoning_effort` cannot express on them. Non-off tiers send a single key; adding a boolean there would only create ambiguity.
   - **A refusing endpoint degrades instead of failing.** The existing capability negotiation now recognises both spellings, drops them together (leaving either one gets the same 400 again) and retries — the same mechanism that already handles `stream_options` and `tools`.
   - **And the degradation is reported.** `reasoning_rejected` on the client, `reasoning_degradation_report` for the wording (pure, tested), one action-log line naming the tier you asked for, that the run used the provider default, and how to stop sending it. Without it the setting says `high` while the model runs at default depth and nothing on screen differs — the same class as a silently-dropped image or a silently-dropped tool schema.
   - The stored value is a free string rather than a Rust enum: tier vocabularies belong to the vendors (`disabled/enabled`, `low/high/max`, `none/low/medium/high/xhigh`), and a closed enum here would mean "cannot be set" the first time a vendor adds one. The select offers the four that are portable.
   - Process note: this file's working copy is **CRLF** (git converts on checkout), and several shell-based edits anchored on `\n` silently failed or landed in the wrong scope — one of them inserted a block into the retry loop referencing variables that do not exist there. `cargo check` caught each one. Shell text editing must be CRLF-aware, and a compile has to follow immediately.
   - Rust 487 → 490 (489 passing, 1 ignored); frontend unchanged.
136. **Two read-only search tools: find by name, find by shape (2026-09-22)**
   Next from the gap list (134), and chosen partly because it needs **no signature changes** — the previous attempt at `AskUserQuestion` was abandoned mid-flight after a changed function signature spread across six files and the shell edits went wrong.
   - **What was missing.** `workspace_search_text` is a literal substring search with an optional extension filter. "Find every `fn handle_*`" or "which files are `*.test.ts`" could not be expressed, so the model searched for a shorter substring and picked through unrelated hits, or walked directories one `workspace_list_files` call at a time.
   - **`workspace_glob`** matches file names, **`workspace_grep`** matches contents with a regex and returns `path:line:`. Both read-only, both always advertised — they add no authority, only reach.
   - **One new dependency, `regex`.** The glob is implemented *through* it (the pattern is translated to an anchored regex) rather than pulling a second crate: `*` / `?` / `**` boundary handling then has exactly one definition, and that boundary is the only part of glob semantics that is actually error-prone. The regex crate is also linear-time by construction, so a pathological user pattern cannot hang a run the way a backtracking engine would.
   - **A pattern without `/` matches in any directory** (`*.ts` finds `src/a.ts`). Strict anchoring is defensible but wrong in practice: the model would ask for `*.ts`, get nothing, and conclude the repo has no TypeScript. `**/` matches zero directories too, so `src/**/*.ts` includes `src/a.ts`.
   - **One filter, three tools.** Both new tools walk through the same `SKIPPED_DIRS` and the same `is_credential_path` refusal as the existing search, via a shared `collect_workspace_files`. Three copies of "skip `.env`" means one of them eventually forgets, and that one is the leak.
   - **A broken regex is an error, not an empty result.** "No matches" for a pattern that failed to compile reads as "this code does not exist", which is the worst possible answer to give a model that is about to write a replacement.
