   - Caps: 20 000 files walked, 60 results, 200 characters per matched line — a single minified line would otherwise spend the context budget on one match.
   - **Test-writing note worth keeping**: two of the new tests failed first on their own assertions, because the "no matches" message echoes the pattern (`No files match "*.env"`, `No matches for /super-secret-value/`) and a `!contains(...)` check was satisfied by the echo rather than by the absence. Assertions about absence need an anchor that cannot appear in a diagnostic — here, `contains("No matches")` plus `!contains(".env:")`.
   - Rust 490 → 493 (492 passing, 1 ignored); frontend unchanged. `SECURITY.md` names both tools — a contract test (`tests/ipc-contract.test.ts`) fails when a shipped built-in tool is undocumented, and it caught exactly that here.
137. **The audit of 136: a search tool's silences are its defects (2026-09-22)**
   Adversarial audit of `69057ba` found 16 problems. Almost all of them share one shape: the tool returns "no matches", the model believes the code does not exist, and nothing distinguishes that from the truth. Fixed here.
   - **Windows patterns matched nothing.** `src\**\*.rs` contains no `/`, so the backslashes were escaped as literals against `/`-normalized paths — guaranteed zero results, with a hint pointing at `**`. The pattern is now normalized (`\` → `/`, a leading `/` or `./` dropped) before translation. This was the single most likely failure in daily use: the model writes the separator it just saw in a path.
   - **Brace expansion is refused, not taken literally.** `**/*.{ts,tsx}` was compiled as a file literally named `x.{ts,tsx}`. `[abc]` is deliberately *kept* literal — `page[id].tsx` is a real filename in Next.js projects, and matching it is the useful behaviour.
   - **The 20 000-file walk cap was invisible.** Past it, both tools said "no matches" for content that exists. The walk now reports truncation, and directory entries are sorted before recursing so the kept subset is at least the same one twice.
   - **A symlink was read through.** `file_type()` does not follow links, so `notes.txt -> ~/.ssh/id_rsa` was a regular file to the walker, the credential check only saw the link's name, and `read_to_string` followed it — returning out-of-workspace content under an in-workspace-looking path. Both walkers now skip symlinks. Legitimate directory symlinks were never traversed anyway, so nothing is lost that used to work. `SECURITY.md`'s "every path goes through `resolve_existing`" was false for the three search tools, which is what let this stay invisible; that sentence is now split by tool class.
   - **`artifacts/` is now skipped.** It holds frozen whole-repo E2E copies: every file appeared twice and the second one was stale code the Agent could quote or edit.
   - **A regex must not be trimmed.** `string_arg` trims, so the pattern `" $"` (find trailing whitespace) became `"$"` and matched every line. `grep` now reads the raw string, like `content` and `new_string` already did.
   - **"More matches omitted" claimed at exactly 60.** `len() >= 60` cannot tell "full" from "there is more"; collecting one extra and comparing `>` can. Sending the model to narrow a pattern that already returned everything is a loop it cannot escape.
   - **No size cap on a searched file.** `read_to_string` on a committed 500 MB log is resident in full; reads now skip files over 2 MB (both search paths — `walk_and_match` had the same hole).
   - **Case-insensitive matching on Windows.** `**/README.MD` returning nothing while `workspace_read_file README.MD` succeeds is two tools disagreeing about whether a file exists.
   - **A parity test that tests parity.** The original "both tools skip what the old one skips" never called the old tool — it compared against hand-written expectations, so a filter drifting in one walker only would still pass. It now searches the same fixture through `search_text_tool` and `grep_text_tool` and compares the visible file sets.
   - Also corrected: entry 136 recorded the test baseline as 487 → 493; 135 ended at 490. Rust 493 → 496 (495 passing, 1 ignored); frontend unchanged.
138. **A tool loop that outgrows the window trims itself instead of failing (2026-09-22)**
   Next from the gap list (134), reached by reading the reference implementation's compaction contract and then asking where *our* context pressure actually is. The answer was not the conversation.
   - **Where the pressure is.** Our history never reaches the model as chat messages: the last 6 turns are flattened into a ≤700-char-per-turn digest inside the context bundle, so "conversation compaction" would compact about 4 KB. The unbounded thing is the **within-run tool loop**: `stream_with_tool_loop` re-sends every earlier message on each of up to 13 requests, and tool results are the bulk (one `workspace_read_file` returns up to 64 KB). Six large reads overflow a 128k window, and the failure mode is the provider rejecting the *whole* request — the rounds already paid for, and any writes already on disk, come back as "context length exceeded".
   - **What was added.** Before every request, if the window is known, `trim_tool_loop_history` measures the prompt and drops the oldest exchanges from the part the loop itself produced. It reuses `bound_transcript_with_limits`, which already keeps an assistant tool call and its results together (splitting a pair makes the provider reject the request outright), always keeps the most recent group whole, and states the omission in a system line.
   - **The assembled prompt is never touched.** Everything before `prompt_len` — system prompt, project context, carried transcript — has its own budget in the context assembler. Trimming it here would be two sets of rules fighting over the same bytes.
   - **Window unknown ⇒ do nothing.** Same rule as `window_limited_output_tokens`: dropping what the model just read, based on a guessed window, is worse than an explicit provider refusal. `prompt_token_budget()` is `Max context − reserved output − 1 024`, and it returns `None` rather than 0 when the window is smaller than the reserve — a 0 budget would re-trim on every single round.
   - **token → char conversion is proportional, not a fixed ratio.** The estimator counts ASCII at 4:1 and non-ASCII at 1:1, so treating the token overflow as a character overflow over-cuts Chinese history and under-cuts ASCII. The target is scaled by the measured ratio of that specific history instead.
   - **Reported as `history_trimmed`.** Through the same seam as `image_input_degraded` / `output_clamp` (a `Vec` on the `Arc`-shared client, drained by `finish_agent_run`, printed to stderr by the CLI). The system line in the transcript is only visible to the model; on its own it leaves the user with a run that inexplicably re-reads files it already read. The wording lives in `history_trim_report` so it is testable.
   - The omission note dropped the words "from previous stages": the same function now serves mid-loop trimming, where that clause would be false.
   - **Not taken: LLM summarization of the dropped rounds.** The reference implementation summarizes with a second model call against a nine-section prompt. That is the right design when history *is* the conversation; here the dropped content is tool output whose source is still on disk, so "re-read the file" is both cheaper and more accurate than a summary of it. Revisit if we ever put real turns into the message list.
   - Rust 496 → 501 (500 passing, 1 ignored); frontend unchanged.
139. **`ask_user_question`: the Agent can ask, and a non-answer stays a non-answer (2026-09-22)**
   First item on the gap list (134), abandoned once before: the earlier attempt added a parameter to `agent_tool_permissions` and reached 27 compile errors across nine call sites plus ~40 test constructions. This time the plumbing question was answered first — `WorkspaceToolPermissions` already carries the approval gate, so the tool needed **no signature change anywhere**.
   - **The gap.** Faced with a fork only the user can settle (two valid designs, which of three files they meant), the model had two options: guess and build half the work on the wrong branch, or ask in its final answer — by which time the run is over and the user must re-prompt.
   - **One channel, two question types.** `ApprovalGate::ask_question` reuses the registry, the id space and the 120 s timeout, with `Decision::Answered(String)` added. So Stop refuses a pending question exactly like a pending approval, and the "stop waiting ⇒ close the dialog" invariant holds for free. Separate event (`agent-question-requested`) because the interactions differ: guessing which kind a payload is would eventually render a choice as an authorization.
   - **A decision sent to the wrong kind of wait is never a yes.** The registry is keyed by id alone, so a stale frontend can send an approval to a question or an answer to an approval. Both map to refusal, pinned by a test — the asymmetric failure (an answer counting as an approval) would authorize an irreversible action nobody approved.
   - **No answer must never become an answer.** Timeout and unattended return `Ok` with "nobody answered, say which option you assumed"; dismissal returns `Err`. An invented answer is worse than none: it gets carried through the rest of the run as the user's stated preference. This is also why timeout is *not* an error — a failure pushes the model back to guessing silently.
   - **Free text always.** The model may not add an "Other" option (rejected); the dialog always has an input. Its options are what it thought of, not the set of possible answers.
   - **Malformed arguments are refused before the dialog opens** — <2 or >4 options, case-insensitive duplicates, an empty question. The cost of this tool is the user's attention, and a dialog they cannot parse spends it for nothing.
   - **Advertised only when a channel is attached** (`can_ask_user()`), so the CLI never sees it. A tool that always answers "nobody to ask" gets called repeatedly.
   - **The doc guard had a hole**: `tests/ipc-contract.test.ts` scanned for `pub const … "workspace_*"`, and this tool is deliberately not `workspace_`-prefixed (it asks a person, not the workspace, and shares the reference implementation's name). The scanner now reads every `pub const … &str` in `workspace_tools.rs`, and asserts `ask_user_question` is in range — a guard that covers half the tools would have gone unnoticed exactly once.
   - **Not taken: 1–4 questions per call** (the reference allows up to four, each with its own multi-select). One question, one dialog, one answer is the smallest thing that removes the fork; batching four would need a form, and the failure mode of a partially-filled form (some answered, some not) is precisely the "invented preference" hazard above.
   - Rust 501 → 509 (508 passing, 1 ignored); frontend 265 → 274 across 32 files.
140. **The audit of 138 and 139: a measurement that misses the biggest thing it measures (2026-09-22)**
   Adversarial audit of `d94e8bf` + `b273166` found 14 problems. The two that mattered would each have made a shipped feature useless or unsafe.
   - **The trim measured `content` and nothing else** — so `workspace_write_file`, whose entire payload sits in `tool_calls[].arguments` on a message whose `content` is `""`, was estimated at ~0 tokens. "Write three big files" — the most likely way to overflow a window — would never have triggered a single trim, which is exactly the failure 138 claims to remove. `message_cost_chars` / `message_cost_tokens` now count tool-call names and arguments, and `bound_transcript_report` uses the same accounting for its group costs (without that second half, the budget it enforces was also blind to write payloads). Images stay excluded, same reason as `estimated_prompt_tokens`: a guessed vision-token count is another fake number.
   - **Both modals owned Escape at once.** `QuestionDialog` and `ConfirmDialog` are both `fixed inset-0 z-50` with a `window` keydown listener, so with a question and an approval pending, one Escape dismissed the question *and denied the approval* — a keypress silently refusing an irreversible action the user had not read. The question now yields while an approval is pending (the one with a side effect goes first) and its handler is gated on being visible.
   - **An assistant message replayed tool calls that would never be answered.** Pre-existing: a round that emits `emit_agent_changes` *and* calls a workspace tool replayed both `tool_calls`, but only the workspace call got a `tool` result — a provider 400 for the whole next request, taking that round's work with it. Only answered calls are replayed now, and the output-protocol call is synthesized into the text immediately instead of waiting for the final round (where it was being dropped entirely).
   - **Counting and wording.** `dropped_messages` counted the inserted system note as a survivor, so every trim under-reported by one while the summary said "exchanges"; it is now `dropped_exchanges`, taken from the group count `bound_transcript_report` returns. A trim that only *truncated* long results reported nothing at all (`dropped == 0`) — `removed_chars` now covers that case with its own sentence. And the details said "the biggest request estimated N tokens" about a request that was never sent: it now says "would have been N before trimming".
   - **Two budgets that could not help.** If the assembled prompt alone exceeds the budget, trimming cannot make the request fit, yet it destroyed the history and logged a warning every round; that case now returns `None` and lets the provider refuse. Same for a budget that is technically positive but useless (128k window with 126k Max output leaves ~1k): `MIN_USEFUL_PROMPT_BUDGET` replaces the `> 0` check, and the real problem there is already reported by the output clamp.
   - **`ask_user_question` hardening.** The "empty question is refused" rule lived three layers away in `string_arg`; it is now in the tool with a test. The user's free-text answer no longer goes verbatim into the action log (the model can ask for anything, and that log is kept as evidence of what the run touched) — only its length. A replaced question now says so in the UI instead of only the console. Focus moved off the first option onto the free-text input, matching `ConfirmDialog`'s reason for focusing Deny: a stray Enter must not become a preference the user never stated.
   - **The doc guard was half-blind in both directions.** Scanning only `workspace_*` missed `ask_user_question`; scanning only `workspace_tools.rs` would have missed a prefixed tool added in a new file. It now unions both, and names the two output-protocol tools (`emit_agent_changes`, `emit_sdd_draft`) explicitly — they are advertised on every native-tools request and neither version of the scanner ever saw them. SECURITY.md now describes them.
   - Rust 509 → 511 (510 passing, 1 ignored); frontend 274 → 276.
141. **Rename and fork a task: finishing the session feature (2026-09-22)**
   Next from the gap list (134), and the two operations the session feature shipped without: a task's name was fixed by its first prompt forever, and the only way to reuse a task's context was to resume it — which then overwrites that task's record with everything you do next.
   - **A name the user owns.** `rename_session` + `session_store::rename`, with `title_is_custom` persisted on the row. The flag exists because the automatic title is derived when the title is *absent*, and those two conditions only happen to coincide today: the thing that must hold is "a name a person chose is never replaced by a machine", so it is recorded explicitly rather than inferred.
   - **Renaming does not reorder the list.** The list is sorted by activity time, which is how a user recognises "the one from this morning"; bumping `updated_at` on a rename would make that ordering lie. The reference implementation stamps a separate `time_title_updated` and keeps activity time monotonic for the same reason.
   - **Empty is an error, not "go back to the automatic title".** Both readings are defensible and they look completely different on screen, so guessing would leave the user unsure whether the rename took effect at all. The dialog's input is `maxLength={60}`, matching the backend's truncation point, so the one boundary the user can hit is visible before they hit it.
   - **Two paths on purpose.** Renaming the *current* task must change the in-memory title too, or the next turn writes the old one straight back — "it changed and then changed back". Renaming any other task only touches the row. Both normalize through `normalized_session_title`, so there is one rule, not two.
   - **Fork is the honest version of "try another approach".** `fork_session` mints a new id, copies the turns, titles it `Fork of X`, and persists immediately — the row must be visible in the list before the first new turn, or the user cannot tell it worked. What makes it different from resume is only *where the next turns go*; the original row is left byte-identical, and a test asserts exactly that.
   - **No `parent_id` field.** The reference implementation records a parent session id; here the only question the UI needs answered is "where did this come from", and the title answers it. A stored field nobody reads is dead weight that blocks the next schema change.
   - **`serde(default)` on the new field**, so rows written before it read back as "derived title" — which is exactly what they are.
   - Rust 511 → 514 (513 passing, 1 ignored); frontend 276 → 279.
142. **Project memory: say whether it is working, and offer to write it (2026-09-22)**
   Next from the gap list (134). `AGENTS.md` is injected into every Agent run, and both of its failure modes were invisible: the project has no such file (the Agent guesses the build commands and the layout, while the user assumes it knows the conventions), or the file exceeds the 8 000-byte injection bound and everything past it is dropped — the tail, which is where the last rules written down live.
   - **The status is the feature.** `project_memory_info()` reports exists / bytes / limit / truncated plus the path, and a card in Settings turns that into one of three sentences. The reference implementation appends a `[File truncated]` marker for the **model** and tells the user nothing; that is exactly the silent degradation this product is not allowed to have.
   - **The draft action is a prompt, not a code path.** The backend returns `draft_prompt`; the frontend sends it as an ordinary prompt. The Agent writes the file with its normal tools, so the change lands in the review area with an undo checkpoint and no new authority is introduced anywhere. Same shape as the reference `/init`, which is also pure prompt expansion.
   - **The prompt's three constraints are the whole design**: only facts verified in this repo (an invented build command is worse than no file — it is treated as truth by every later run, and the person who follows it is the one who finds out); no secrets or machine-local absolute paths; stay under the byte budget, with the reason stated so the model has a motive to comply. It also says *update in place, do not rewrite wholesale*, because such a file usually carries conventions that exist nowhere in the code and losing them is invisible in a diff.
   - **The frontend recomputes `truncated` from the two numbers it displays** rather than trusting the backend's boolean: if they ever disagree, the sentence on screen must agree with the numbers next to it.
   - An unreadable payload says so instead of rendering as "no project memory" — that reading would send the user to create a file that already exists.
   - **Not taken: an action-log warning on every truncated injection.** It belongs there (that is where degradations live) but the injection happens inside context assembly, which has no `RunEvents`; doing it properly means threading the flag out of the context builder. Recorded here rather than half-done.
   - Rust 514 → 516 (515 passing, 1 ignored); frontend 279 → 286 across 33 files.
143. **The audit of 141 and 142: `persist_session` is not "save" (2026-09-22)**
   Adversarial audit of `c454c28` + `1df14f7` found 10 problems. Three of them share one root cause: `persist_session()` means *sync*, and for a session with no turns that means **delete**.
   - **Renaming an unsaved task reported success while the row was removed.** `remove` is idempotent, so the error channel stayed empty; on screen the task vanished from the list, the name did not change, and nothing was reported. Reachable: truncate the context of a resumed task, then rename the stale row that the panel has not reloaded. Both `rename_session` and `fork_session` now refuse a turn-less session by name.
   - **`fork_session` mutated before it persisted**, which is exactly the ordering `delete_agent_session` documents against. A failed write left the user in a session with no row on disk, the previous conversation gone from memory, and the UI still pointing at the old task — the next prompt would have been recorded into the ghost. It now builds the `StoredSession`, `upsert`s it, and only then adopts it.
   - **`title_is_custom` was a field nobody read** — the guard that protects a user's name was still `session_title.is_none()`, so the flag proved nothing, and the same commit had rejected `parent_id` for precisely that reason. `push_turn` now consults it, and cap eviction protects custom-titled rows: renaming is the strongest signal a user can give that a row matters, and it was the one operation that did not refresh activity time, so the named row was the *first* to be evicted.
   - **"all of it is sent with every run" was false.** Fitting the 8 000-byte bound is not the same as reaching the model: the context assembler gives project memory a 15 % quota of the input budget and drops the section entirely below `MIN_USEFUL_SECTION_CHARS`, and Chat can switch project memory off for a single run. The card now says it "fits the injection bound" and names both things that can still trim it. Claiming otherwise in the very commit that is about making a silent degradation visible would have been the same defect one layer up.
   - **With no workspace open, the card described someone else's file.** `workspace_root()` falls back to the process's current directory, so in `tauri dev` it read *this repo's* `AGENTS.md` and reported it as "this workspace". `workspace_open` now comes from `current_workspace_key()`, and the card says "Open a workspace folder first" with no action offered.
   - **"Sent" was set before sending** and never cleared: with no model configured it stated a falsehood, and it outlived the run it described. It is now set on the promise's success and cleared on the next idle edge.
   - **The draft prompt named the task after itself.** The task title is derived from the prompt's first line, so a task would be called "This workspace has no AGENTS.md yet, so every Agent run here start...". The prompt now opens with `Draft this project's AGENTS.md`.
   - Rust 516 → 517 (516 passing, 1 ignored); frontend 286 → 287.
144. **The truncated project memory now reaches the user, not just the model (2026-09-22)**
   Finishing what 142 recorded as deliberately not done. `bound_project_memory` appends a `project memory truncated` marker **into the injected text**, so the only party told was the model; the user saw an Agent that ignored the rules they had just written.
   - **The fact is carried out of assembly, not recovered from the text.** `load_project_memory` now returns `LoadedProjectMemory { text, bytes, truncated }`, `AgentContext` carries `project_memory_truncated: Option<usize>` (the size on disk), and the command layer emits `project_memory_truncated` through the same action-log channel as the other degradations. Recovering it by substring-matching the prompt would have made the report depend on the exact wording of a marker meant for the model.
   - **It is reported before the run, not at finish.** Every other degradation is a fact about a request that already went out, so `finish_agent_run` is the right place for them. This one is different: it is known at assembly, and it explains the run that is *about to* happen. If the Agent then ignores the last rules in the file, this warning is the explanation — emitting it after the fact puts it a full turn too late.
   - Taking the orchestrator lock a second time for the emit is fine and deliberate: `emit_run_action_log` is synchronous, so the lock never spans an `await`.
   - The CLI prints the same two sentences to stderr in its agent-run path; stdout stays parseable. (Not adjacent to the image/history warnings, which are printed after the run completes.)
   - The wording lives in `truncation_report`, tested like `history_trim_report`: it names the byte size, the bound, and that the tail *was not seen in this run* — "it was truncated" alone does not tell the user which of their rules went missing.
   - Rust 517 → 519 (518 passing, 1 ignored); frontend unchanged.
145. **Switch model without saving a profile — and stop two paths from silently switching it back (2026-09-22)**
   Next from the gap list (134). Trying another model meant saving a profile, and `save_llm_profile` sets it active by default: a one-off experiment rewrote the global configuration.
   - **The override changes the model name and nothing else.** `get_llm_client(profile_id, model_override)` replaces `config.model`, which is what `output_token_key` reads to decide between `max_tokens` and `max_completion_tokens`. Endpoint, key, token cap, spend cap, context budget and per-token prices deliberately stay with the profile: switching a model must not silently widen a spend limit the user set.
   - **Which means the reported cost can be wrong, so it is said out loud.** `model_override_report` produces a `model_override` action-log warning naming both models and listing exactly what did not change with it; the chat also shows the same caveat under the input. A cost figure the user cannot trace to a rate is worse than no figure.
   - **Two paths were switching the model behind the user's back.** `continue_agent_pipeline` and `repair_workspace` passed `None` for the profile, i.e. "whatever is active now" — pick model B in the chat, continue the pipeline, and the second half ran on model A with A's prices and caps, invisibly. Both now take the chat's profile and override. This was pre-existing and is the kind of defect that only shows up in a bill.
   - **Blank is not a model.** An empty or whitespace-only override is treated as "no override" in the store, in the backend, and in the report — an empty model name would otherwise become an unreadable provider 400.
   - Not taken: adopting the overridden model's context window / prices from `modelLimits`. That table lives in the frontend and covers a subset of ids; guessing a window for the backend's budget decisions would trade a visible caveat for an invisible wrong number. Saving a profile remains the way to get those right, and the warning says so.
   - Rust 519 → 520 (519 passing, 1 ignored); frontend 287 → 288.
146. **The audit of 144 and 145: the gate was red and I did not see it (2026-09-22)**
   Adversarial audit of `e3fa459` + `213b8cf` found 12 problems, including one that should never have shipped.
   - **`cargo clippy --all-targets -- -D warnings` was failing at `213b8cf`.** `EstimateContextRequest.model_override` had no reader, so `dead_code` fired. I had run clippy *without* `-D warnings` and grepped for `^error`, which turns the mandated gate into a weaker one — exactly the class of mistake AGENTS.md's first bullet describes. The field is deleted (the estimate is computed with the profile's window, because the override carries no window), and the store stopped sending it. **Run the five commands as written, no filters that can hide a warning.**
   - **The commit claimed an invariant the code did not have.** It said recomputing `model_type` is what makes `o3` receive `max_completion_tokens`. False: `output_token_key` reads `config.provider` and `config.model`, `LlmConfig.model_type` has no reader at all, and reasoning comes from the profile's `reasoning_effort`. The write-only assignment is gone and the claim is corrected.
   - **And the thing it claimed to prevent could still happen.** `output_token_key` also required `provider == "openai"`, so a custom/Azure-style profile with an `o3` override sent `max_tokens` and got a 400 with no strip-and-retry (`unsupported_parameter` does not know these keys). It now keys on the model id alone, tolerating a `vendor/model` prefix.
   - **The override warning covered 2 of 4 run paths** — missing from exactly the two the commit had just fixed (continue, repair), so the half of a pipeline that ran on another model said nothing. Both now emit it.
   - **`project_memory_truncated` coverage is narrower than the other degradations**, and SECURITY.md implied parity. A continued pipeline re-uses the paused run's context and does not repeat the warning; `repair_workspace` never assembles a context at all; the two estimate surfaces report sizes without warning. Documented as it is rather than claimed away.
   - **"only the first 8 000 bytes reached the model" was an over-claim**: `safe_prefix` retreats to a char boundary, and the context assembler can trim or drop the section afterwards. Now "at most the first 8 000", with the budget caveat spelled out.
   - **A model override left the connection check vouching for a target that was never tested.** `llmTargetFingerprint` is `profile|endpoint|model|key`; the override changed the model without changing the fingerprint, so the status bar kept saying "connected" for a model the run had never reached. The override is now part of the fingerprint, and blank still means "unchanged".
   - Also: both commits had spliced a new function into the middle of `finish_agent_run`'s doc comment (cutting a sentence in half and re-attaching the rest to the wrong function); the override input is now disabled when no provider is configured, like the profile select next to it; and a stray whitespace regression in SECURITY.md is repaired.
   - Still open, recorded rather than half-done: an override is not validated against the selected profile the way `chatProfileId` is (`resolveChatProfileId`), and it survives "New task". Both are visible in the input, so nothing is hidden.
   - Rust 520 unchanged (519 passing, 1 ignored); frontend 288 → 289.
147. **The context estimate now names what it leaves out, on screen (2026-09-22)**
   Closes the omission recorded in 119 and deferred again in this session's earlier round. The estimate covers the context bundle only; the request that goes out also carries the system prompt and output rules, the tool schemas, the pending-diff summary and the user's own prompt. The consequence is the failure this product exists to prevent in miniature: a number that looks comfortably inside the budget, next to a run that was truncated for being too long.
   - **The list comes from the backend** (`estimate_omissions`, surfaced as `notCounted` on the estimate response), because what was left out is only knowable on the side that assembles the request. It had been a hand-written sentence in a `title` tooltip in `ChatView` — two copies of one fact, free to drift.
   - **And it is rendered on screen, not only on hover.** A tooltip is invisible to anyone not hovering, which is the same defect as a marker only the model can see. The tooltip stays for the longer explanation (pipeline-accumulated messages, why the measured row is not comparable).
   - **Not taken: guessing the missing sizes.** The system prompt differs per path and role, the tool schemas depend on the run's permissions, and the prompt is not typed yet. A guessed total would replace an honest gap with a false number — the same trade rejected for the model-override window.
   - `AGENTS.md` gains the lesson from 146, and loses a sentence to pay for it: run each gate exactly as written, no pipe that can swallow a warning. Measured at 7 496 bytes, inside its own 7 500 target.
   - Rust 520 → 521 (520 passing, 1 ignored); frontend unchanged.
148. **Undo a whole turn: linking checkpoints to conversation turns (2026-09-22)**
   Next from the gap list (134) — the feasible half of "checkpoints + rewind". The reference implementation ties every checkpoint to a message id, which is what makes a per-turn rewind expressible at all; we had **no linkage whatsoever**: `ApplyCheckpoint` was `{label, snapshots, diff_ids}`, `ConversationTurn` was `{id, prompt, outcome, derived}`, and neither carried a run id. "Undo what that turn did" could only be approximated by popping the stack blindly — which also undoes everything in between that the user wanted to keep.
   - **The linkage is the run id.** `push_undo_checkpoint` stamps `current_run_id` plus the session id; `push_turn` stamps the run that produced the turn. One funnel on each side, so there is exactly one place per side that can be wrong.
   - **A manual Apply between runs gets no run id**, deliberately: that change is the user's, not a turn's. Attributing it to the most recent turn would make "undo this turn" quietly revert a file the user chose to apply himself.
   - **Only when that turn's apply steps sit contiguously on top of the stack.** The undo stack is strictly LIFO — restoring a middle layer builds later changes on a state that never existed, and nothing in a diff shows that damage. A blocked attempt returns the reason *and* the way forward ("undo those first, then revert this turn"), because a refusal that only says "failed" leaves the user with nothing to do. A test asserts the workspace is byte-identical after a refusal.
   - **The undo label now says when the top checkpoint came from an earlier task.** The stack deliberately survives "New task" — the writes are still on disk — which means Undo in a fresh task can restore files from a conversation no longer on screen. Clearing the stack would be worse (it drops the only way back), so the fix is disclosure.
   - UI: a per-turn **Revert files** beside "Cut from here" in the context strip, shown only for turns that carry a run id. Cutting context changes what the model sees; this changes what is on disk. Two separate actions on purpose — doing both from one click is a third thing the user did not ask for.
   - Not done: reverting a turn does not also cut the context, and a turn whose checkpoints were already evicted by the 20-deep cap has nothing to revert. Both are honest limits of the data, recorded here rather than hidden behind a disabled button.
   - Rust 521 → 524 (523 passing, 1 ignored); frontend 289 → 291.
149. **The audit of 147 and 148: all five gates green, and the feature did nothing (2026-09-22)**
   Adversarial audit of `1929508` + `39dfcc8`. Every gate passed — `fmt`, `clippy -D warnings`, 523 Rust tests, `tsc`, 291 frontend tests — and the turn-revert feature was **inert plus wrong** in four independent ways. Worth recording as a pattern: a green suite says nothing about a field name crossing IPC or an ordering between two functions.
   - **`ConversationTurn.run_id` crossed IPC as snake_case.** The struct has no `rename_all`; the other fields survived only because they are single words. The frontend read `runId`, got `undefined`, and the "Revert files" button never rendered for any turn. Fixed with an explicit `rename = "runId"`. The estimate commit in the same round got this right (`notCounted`), which is what made the omission invisible.
   - **The normalizer dropped it too**, and `loadConversationTurns` bypassed the normalizer entirely with `invoke<ConversationTurn[]>` — a generic parameter is the same unchecked assertion as `as`, on the one payload that decides whether a destructive control appears. Both fixed; the normalizer is now exported and used on that path.
   - **Checkpoints were stamped from ambient state at publish time.** `finish_agent_run` clears `current_run_id` *before* `publish_tool_writes` drains the writes, so every tool-write checkpoint — the dominant write path in Auto mode — got `run_id: None` and its turn could never find it. Worse, a **stopped** run drains after the *next* run has claimed, so its checkpoint would be stamped with the next run's id and "revert this turn" would have restored another turn's files. `record_external_actions` already documents exactly this hazard and takes the id as a parameter; `record_tool_writes` / `push_undo_checkpoint` now do the same, fed from `permissions.run_id`. Manual applies pass `None` explicitly.
   - **Partial failure destroyed the remaining checkpoints.** The loop popped every layer of the turn even after a restore failed, applying older layers on top of a state the failed layer was supposed to have restored — with the snapshots gone (memory-only, no second copy). It now stops at the first failure, keeps the rest of the stack undoable, and says so in the label.
   - **The action log claimed success regardless.** `undo_last_apply` uses `apply_log_level(failed)`; the new command hardcoded `"success"` and counted only restores. Now the same helper, the failed count in the summary, and the review surface/ordering matched to undo.
   - Rust 524 unchanged (523 passing, 1 ignored); frontend 291 unchanged — the fixes are in code the existing tests already cover, and two turn tests now pass the run id explicitly, which is what the command layer does.
150. **Pin the wire names, because nothing else does (2026-09-22)**
   Closing the root cause of 149's first defect rather than only its instance. `ConversationTurn` now has a test that serializes it and asserts the exact key set (`derived, id, outcome, prompt, runId`) plus a round-trip, because a field name crossing IPC **is** the interface and no gate checks it: the compiler, clippy, 523 Rust tests and 291 frontend tests were all green while the frontend read `undefined` and a destructive control never rendered.
   - **Not taken (yet): a scanner over every `Serialize` struct.** The obvious generalization — fail any snake_case field without a `rename`, across `orchestrator.rs` and `commands/agent.rs` — would false-positive on the types that are snake_case **on purpose**: `StoredSession` is the on-disk session format and never reaches the frontend. A guard that cries wolf gets suppressed, so the honest version needs a marker for "this type crosses IPC" first. Recorded here instead of shipped half-right.
   - Rust 524 → 525 (524 passing, 1 ignored); frontend unchanged.
151. **`web_fetch`, part one: the gates, before the request (2026-09-22)**
   Next from the gap list (134). Fetching a URL for the Agent is dangerous in one direction only: not "it failed to fetch", but "it fetched something it should never have reached". `services/web_fetch.rs` lands that judgment as pure, tested functions first; the tool surface (authority flag, approval, external-action record, Settings toggle) is the next round, so nothing in the UI promises a capability that is not wired.
   - **Adopted from the reference contract**: http upgraded to https (plaintext hands the whole URL, query string included, to every hop), credentials in the URL **refused** rather than stripped, a 2 000-char URL cap, 10 MiB wire cap, 100 000-char post-conversion cap with a visible truncation marker, text-like content types only, and — the good part — **cross-host redirects are not followed**. The target is handed back so the model asks again, which keeps "the user approved *this* host" true instead of letting one approval travel.
   - **Rejected from the reference contract**: it answers the user's `prompt` with a *second model call* over the page and shows the main model only that answer. That spends money the user never sees and hides the source. We return the extracted text.
   - **Added beyond it**: the fetched text is wrapped as untrusted third-party content with an explicit "data, not instructions" line. The reference has no such wrapper. It will not stop a determined injection, but the default reading of a web page must be *material*, not *orders*.
   - **Private-address refusal is the point of the module**: loopback, `*.localhost`, `*.local`, single-label hostnames, RFC1918, link-local (`169.254.169.254` is one GET away from cloud instance credentials), CGNAT, and IPv6 forms that decode to a private v4 (`::ffff:10.0.0.1`, `64:ff9b::192.168.0.1` — both unwrapped before the check).
   - **Known limit, stated rather than papered over**: no DNS pre-resolution, so a public domain that resolves to a private address (DNS rebinding) is not blocked here. Blocking it correctly means judging the resolved address at connect time, which belongs in the fetch layer next round.
   - Rust 525 → 532 (531 passing, 1 ignored); frontend unchanged.
152. **`web_fetch` ships on by default, and the priority changes with it (2026-09-22)**
   The user pushed back on the direction of the last several rounds: too many gates, too much cost and degradation reporting, not enough capability. He is right, and the correction is recorded in memory as well as here.
   - **The evidence he had.** Before the Agent could do anything real, a user had to: switch to Auto for writes, hand-type a browser origin allow-list, hand-type a second one for page reads — and I was about to add a third for `web_fetch`. A tool that needs its owner to become a configuration engineer reads as *broken*, not as *careful*. Six distinct `warn` kinds in the action log had the same effect from the other side: the seventh warning would have been ignored, and so would the six.
   - **The distinction I had collapsed.** `AGENTS.md` says the user must be able to **see and undo** everything — *undo*, not *ask*. The undo stack and the review area were already the answer; I kept adding approval prompts on top. Gate what is irreversible **and** invisible (writes outside the workspace, pushing a remote, deleting files, desktop input injection). Everything else: let it act, leave a trail.
   - **So `web_fetch` is attached by default and asks for nothing.** Reading a public page cannot damage this machine and needs no undo. The safety lives where the harm is real: private/loopback/metadata addresses refused outright, cross-host redirects not followed, content marked untrusted, and every fetch recorded as an external action so "what did it look at" is answerable afterwards.
   - The one gate kept on the request path is Stop: after Stop the tool refuses to open a new request, and that refusal is deliberately *not* recorded as a fetch.
   - `tool_names_do_not_collide_with_mcp_routing` asserted every tool starts with `workspace_`. That was a stand-in for the real invariant (not colliding with MCP, and being claimed by `handles()`); two tools now deliberately lack the prefix, so the test asserts the property instead of the proxy.
   - Next, from the same correction: collapse the six degradation warnings into one line with details on demand, report spend once at the end, and prioritise the remaining gaps by "can it finish more tasks" — subagent delegation, a build mode that stops interrupting, WebSearch.
   - Rust 532 → 535 (534 passing, 1 ignored); frontend unchanged.
153. **Seven warnings became one (2026-09-22)**
   Second item from the same correction. The action log had seven `warn` kinds for "this run did less than you think": `tool_capability_degraded`, `image_input_degraded`, `history_trimmed`, `output_limit_clamped`, `reasoning_effort_rejected`, plus two emitted before the run. Each was individually justified and collectively self-defeating — the seventh would have been ignored, and so would the six.
   - **`emit_degradation_log` emits one `run_degraded` entry.** Summary is a scannable list ("degraded in 3 way(s): 1 image(s) were not sent…; …"), details carry every full reason. The five wording functions stay exactly as they were — they are pure and tested; only the delivery changed.
   - The CLI prints the same thing as one stderr block instead of four.
   - `finish_agent_run` no longer has five emit helpers, so the two that became unreachable were deleted rather than left behind a `#[allow]`.
   - The test that pinned `image_input_degraded` now pins `run_degraded` **and** the count in the summary, so a silently-dropped report is still caught. Its negative half — a clean run writes nothing — is what keeps the entry meaningful.
   - Still two before the run (`model_override`, `project_memory_truncated`), and deliberately: they explain the run that is *about to* happen, and merging them into the finish entry would put the explanation a turn late. Merging those two with each other is the next small step.
   - Rust 535 unchanged (534 passing, 1 ignored); frontend unchanged.
154. **Subagent delegation, part one: the contract (2026-09-22)**
   The largest remaining capability item, and the one with the most leverage: "find every place X is used" currently drags dozens of files through the main conversation and crowds out the work itself. Handing it to a read-only subagent means the main context gains one paragraph instead. `agent/subagent.rs` lands the prompt, the bounds and the returned shape as pure functions; the run loop needs `LlmClient` reachable from the tool layer, which is a signature change and gets its own round.
   - **Adopted from the reference contract**: the child starts **fresh** (the prompt must carry the whole question — it cannot see the parent conversation), recursion depth is exactly **1**, only the child's final text comes back (not its transcript), and the system prompt explicitly forbids writing `findings.md` — the caller reads the message, not files left behind. That last rule exists because leaving report files is the single most common subagent misbehaviour.
   - **Not adopted**: their `maxTurns` is threaded through four layers and **read by nothing** — the default `4` looks like a bound and is not one; the only real limit is an inactivity watchdog. `MAX_SUBAGENT_ROUNDS = 8` here is enforced, and when it stops a child the caller is *told*, because a half-finished answer that looks finished is worse than no answer.
   - **The returned value is bounded at 20 000 chars** with the same reasoning: the whole point is that the parent's context gains a conclusion, not a transcript. Truncation and the round cap both appear in the text handed to the caller.
   - **Cost is one line, at the end** (`delegation_log_line`): a delegation is a real model loop and the user has a right to know it happened, but not a running commentary.
   - Rust 535 → 540 (539 passing, 1 ignored); frontend unchanged.
155. **Subagent delegation, part two: the loop, and the question part three has to answer (2026-09-22)**
   `executor::run_subagent` and the `delegate_task` tool now exist and compile; the channel that would switch the tool on is **not attached yet**, so `can_delegate()` is false, the tool is not advertised, and nothing in the UI promises a capability that is not wired.
   - **The loop reuses `stream_with_tool_loop`** with a new `max_iterations` parameter, so the subagent's 8-round cap is the same enforced mechanism as the main run's 12 — not a second implementation that can drift. The child's stream goes into its own channel that is drained in the background: it must not interleave with the parent's answer in the chat, and an undrained channel would deadlock the child on `send`.
   - **Depth 1 is structural.** The child's permissions are built from `read_only()` and never get a subagent channel, so it cannot delegate — the prompt says so too, but the prompt is not what enforces it. Building the child from `read_only()` rather than by subtracting from the parent means a future capability added to the parent is *not* inherited by default; subtracting would eventually leak "the subagent can suddenly write files".
   - **Cancellation is the parent's switch**, shared: Stop means stop the whole thing, not just the outermost layer.
   - **Part three's real question**: the tool schema the child advertises. `stream_chat_with_tools` sends the *client's* configured tool list, so cloning the parent's client would advertise write and delegate tools that the child's invoker then refuses — the model would spend rounds calling tools that cannot work. Attaching the channel before knowing the answer would have shipped exactly that, so the channel stays off until the client can be given a child-scoped tool list.
   - Rust 540 unchanged (539 passing, 1 ignored); frontend unchanged.
156. **Subagent delegation, part three: the child's tool list, and the switch goes on (2026-09-23)**
   `delegate_task` is now live on the four desktop run entry points. The blocker 155 recorded is closed by giving the child **its own client**: `SubagentChannel::child_client` clones the parent's and calls `with_extra_tools(tool_definitions(child_permissions))`, which **replaces** the list rather than appending to it.
   - **One source for two mechanisms.** A request body's tools come from the *client*; the calls are served by the *invoker*. Those are two different objects and nothing made them agree, so the same `child_permissions` value now computes both, and a test asserts the property that matters: every name the child advertises satisfies `child_invoker.handles(name)`, and the parent's `workspace_write_file` / `workspace_edit_file` / `workspace_move_file` / `workspace_run_command`, its MCP tools and `delegate_task` itself are all absent. Without that, the child would spend rounds out of 8 calling tools its invoker refuses and come back with "the tools are unavailable" — five green gates and a useless feature, the failure shape 149 already cost a cycle to.
   - **Replacement, not subtraction, again.** `with_extra_tools` overwrites, so the parent's MCP surface cannot leak by omission — the same reasoning that builds the child's permissions from `read_only()` rather than by removing bits from the parent.
   - **The clone shares what should be shared.** `LlmClient`'s usage meter, image/output/history degradation records and `tools_rejected` all sit behind `Arc`, so the child's spend lands on this run's budget and its degradations reach the same end-of-run report. A delegation that billed nowhere would be an invisible cost — the thing the capability-over-gates decision explicitly still requires to be visible.
   - **Pipeline continue and repair must re-attach, not inherit.** Both clone `orch.tool_permissions` from the previous run, which now carries a channel holding *that* run's client — the previous usage meter, possibly a different model override. So the channel is replaced with this run's client, joining `adopt_cancel` and `reset_image_budget` in the list of fields those two paths must reset. Missed, the child's cost would be charged to a run that already ended.
   - Headless entries attach no channel, so there the tool is absent rather than advertised-and-failing.
   - **A profile that cannot send tools gets no channel at all.** `build_chat_request` only inserts `tools` when `tool_call_mode == "native_tools"`, and a subagent has *nothing but* tools — no parent conversation, no packaged context. On a text-protocol profile the child would therefore be a guaranteed-empty loop that still answers confidently, which is worse than an error. `SubagentChannel::for_run` returns `Option` so that judgment lives in one place instead of four call sites, and `LlmClient::can_send_tools` also covers the mid-run case: the provider can reject `tools` *after* the list was advertised, so `delegate_task` re-checks before spending anything.
   - Rust 540 → 543 (542 passing, 1 ignored); frontend 291 unchanged.
157. **The audit of 151-156: an SSRF hole, a meter nobody read, and a subagent nobody logged (2026-09-23)**
   Adversarial audit of `81bb38e`, `546c3de`, `c23fd4f`, `5b057ef`, `16c2867`, `c74dd9f`. Five gates green throughout; six defects, four of them in the parts the write-ups called guarantees.
   - **`web_fetch` checked a host the request would not use (fixed).** The host came from `split("://")` + `split(['/','?','#'])` and was judged by `IpAddr::from_str`, which only accepts canonical dotted-quad. reqwest then re-parses with the WHATWG parser, which accepts `127.1`, `0x7f.0.0.1`, `0177.0.0.1`, `2130706433`, `192.168.1` and percent-decodes `%31%32%37.0.0.1` — every one of those walked straight past the loopback/RFC1918/metadata deny-list and connected. The one guarantee the module exists for did not hold, on a tool that is on by default, needs no approval, and is also in the **child** subagent's list, one page-injected URL away from a metadata read. Now `normalize_fetch_url` parses with `reqwest::Url`, judges `host_str()`, and returns `upgraded.to_string()` — what was checked is what gets sent. Two tests: the six spellings, and "the returned URL is the one that will be sent".
   - **A delegation in a continued pipeline escaped the run's budget (fixed).** `SubagentChannel::for_run(&llm)` ran *before* `llm.with_usage_meter(resumed_meter)`, so the child's client kept `fresh_meter` — which `resumed_usage_meter()` then discarded. The child's tokens went to a meter no one reads: absent from the usage log and **unconstrained by the per-run token and spend caps**, which is the opposite of what 156 claimed. The meter is now resolved inside the same critical section that claims the run, and attached before the channel is built. `repair_workspace` was already correct for an accidental reason (its meter is the one `get_llm_client` attached), and that is now true by construction on both paths.
   - **Nothing the child did reached the audit trail (fixed).** `child_permissions()` starts from `read_only()`, and `Default` mints *fresh* `external` / `writes` / `image_bytes` `Arc`s — while `publish_external_actions` drains the **parent's**. Every page a subagent fetched was recorded into a log nobody empties, so SECURITY.md's "every fetch is recorded … answerable afterwards" was false for exactly the delegated ones. Authority still starts from zero; the **records** now join the parent's (`external`, `writes`, `image_bytes`, `run_id`). `images` deliberately stays separate: that queue is "attachments for this tool result", and the child's pictures belong to the child's conversation, not to a parent message nobody asked to have images in. The write log is shared for the same reason even though the child cannot write — if that ever changes, the failure mode would be disk changing with an empty Diff view.
   - **A failed or stopped delegation recorded nothing (fixed).** `run_subagent(...).await?` returned before `record_external`, so the two cases where the user most needs the line — seven rounds then a provider error, or a Stop mid-loop — spent money and left no trace. Now a `delegate_task_failed` entry carries the reason; the `_failed` suffix already classifies correctly in `publish_external_actions` and in the frontend's `isRefusedAction`.
   - **SECURITY.md still named three action-log phases `546c3de` deleted**: `image_input_degraded`, `history_trimmed`, `tool_capability_degraded`. Only the paragraph that introduced the consolidation was rewritten; the three places that told the reader which warning to look for were not. Also corrected: `agent_cli` prints four of the five causes, not "the same block" — the tool-rejection clause is desktop-only.
   - **Still open, deliberately**: `hit_round_cap` is wrong in both directions. `rounds` is derived from assistant messages with `tool_calls` in the transcript, but the loop's last iteration runs no tools (so a child that answered cleanly after 8 tool rounds is reported as truncated), and `trim_tool_loop_history` edits that same transcript (so a child whose history was trimmed undercounts and a real cap can read as a clean finish). The fix is for `stream_with_tool_loop` to return the round count and cap flag it already knows rather than have the caller re-derive them, which is a `StageOutcome` change and gets its own round.
   - Rust 543 → 546 (545 passing, 1 ignored); frontend 291 unchanged.
158. **The loop reports its own rounds (2026-09-23)**
   Closing 157's open item. `StageOutcome` gained `tool_rounds` and `hit_round_cap`, both kept by `stream_with_tool_loop` itself; `run_subagent` returns them and `delegate_task_tool` passes them straight to `bound_result`. The old caller-side derivation is deleted, not kept as a fallback.
   - **Why the derivation could not be fixed in place.** It read two facts off the transcript that the transcript does not carry. The last iteration never executes tools, so "used all 8 rounds, then answered" and "wanted a 9th round and was stopped" produce the same messages — the first was being reported to the main Agent as *possibly incomplete*, which is the exact wording the caller is supposed to trust. And `trim_tool_loop_history` deletes the oldest assistant/tool groups from the very list the transcript is the tail of, so the longest runs — the ones most likely to have hit the cap — counted fewest rounds and read as clean finishes.
   - **`tool_rounds` is incremented before the calls run**, not after: a round that Stop interrupts has already spent money, and "how many rounds did this cost" must not shrink because the run was cancelled.
   - **Two tests on the mock provider**, one per direction: `max_iterations = 1` ends with `tool_rounds == 1` and `hit_round_cap == false` (the false positive the old code produced), `max_iterations = 0` ends with `hit_round_cap == true` and the in-band note. The mock's rule that it stops calling tools once a `tool` message follows the last `user` message is what makes both deterministic.
   - Rust 546 → 548 (547 passing, 1 ignored); frontend 291 unchanged.
159. **MCP stops being invisible (2026-09-23)**
   The largest gap in SECURITY.md, taken one step: an MCP call now lands in the **durable** external-action log (`mcp_tool_call` / `mcp_tool_failed` / `mcp_tool_cancelled`) next to browser navigations and `web_fetch`, and the record names the paths its arguments mentioned, split into inside and outside the workspace.
   - **Why visibility before a boundary.** MCP is the biggest side-effect surface in the product and it had *no* persistent record at all — a call existed only in the in-memory action log, so "what did that server do yesterday" had no answer. Nothing about its authority changed here; what changed is that the irreversible part is now auditable. That ordering follows the capability-over-gates decision: the rule is gate what is irreversible **and** invisible, and the cheapest correct move was to delete the "invisible" half.
   - **`McpToolInvoker` now holds the run's `WorkspaceToolPermissions`** instead of a bare cancel flag — same one-source rule the cancel switch already followed, and the record book comes with it. `record_external` became `pub` for exactly this caller.
   - **Paths come from a fixed key table** (`path`, `paths`, `file`, `files`, `file_path`, `filePath`, `directory`, `dir`, `cwd`, …), walked recursively so `{"edits":[{"file_path":"a.ts"}]}` is caught, with `://` values skipped. Sniffing every string was rejected: MCP arguments carry URLs, regexes and whole files, and a log full of guesses is a log nobody reads. Missing a key costs one line of detail, not the record.
   - **It states, it does not refuse.** A key table cannot be an authority — the schema belongs to the server, so renaming a key evades the check — and refusing on a guess would break the filesystem servers a user deliberately pointed at a sibling directory. The reference implementation reaches the same conclusion from the other side: it asks approval for *every* MCP call and applies no path check at all, and its own built-in path policy says hard containment is off on purpose. So: classify lexically (`..` reduced, `repo-old` is not inside `repo`), say it, and add in the same sentence that the write is not in the review area and cannot be undone.
   - **A failed call is recorded too.** The error is the server's word, not proof that nothing was written.
   - Frontend needed no change: the log renders `kind`/`target`/`detail` generically and `isRefusedAction` already keys on the `_failed` / `_cancelled` suffixes.
   - **Still open**: the writes themselves. Making them undoable needs a pre-image, which only `git` can supply for tracked files — the next step is a `git status` diff around each MCP call, publishing changed files as `applied` diffs with their HEAD content. Also unchanged: the deny list, the workspace boundary, and in-flight cancellation.
   - Rust 548 → 551 (550 passing, 1 ignored); frontend 291 unchanged.
160. **An MCP write becomes undoable (2026-09-23)**
   The second half of 159, and the first time a side effect this product did not perform is nevertheless reviewable: the files an MCP call names are **snapshotted before the call and compared after it**, and anything that really changed is published as an `applied` diff with an undo checkpoint — the same path the built-in write tools use.
   - **Why snapshot rather than ask the tool.** An MCP result is free text: the protocol has no "files I wrote" field, and the output schema is `{content, structuredContent, isError, _meta}` with `additionalProperties: false`, so a server *cannot* report one even if it wanted to. The reference implementation concludes from the same fact that MCP gets no checkpoint at all — its rewind artifact requires the tool's own `originalFile` in structured output. Not adopted: if the tool cannot self-report, we take the "before" copy *for* it. Cost is reading the named files twice per call, bounded at 8 files × 1 MiB.
   - **The pre-image is what makes it undoable**, and it is the same field the built-ins fill (`AgentFileWrite.previous`), so the review card, `restore_snapshots` and the 20-deep undo stack all work with no new machinery. Creations (`previous: None` → delete on undo) and deletions (`removed: true`, content kept) are detected too.
   - **The card must not lie about where it came from.** `record_tool_writes` hardcoded a rationale naming `workspace_write_file`; a detected MCP change wearing that sentence sends the user looking for a tool that was never called. A second entry point `record_detected_writes(source, …)` sets `protocol: "mcp_tool"` and a rationale naming the MCP tool *and* saying the change was found by comparison rather than self-reported — because how we know bears on how much to trust it. Both entry points share one body; `AgentFileWrite` grew no field, deliberately: it has 24 construction sites, most of them tests, and threading provenance through all of them would put this change's risk somewhere unrelated to it.
   - **A failed call is compared too.** The server's error is not evidence that nothing was written, and "it had already changed N file(s)" is exactly what the user needs after a half-finished call.
   - **The comparison is a pure function** (`write_from_probe`): each of its four branches has its own way of being wrong — an unchanged file becoming a phantom card, a deletion shown as "emptied", a creation whose undo writes an empty file instead of removing it, and a file that stopped being text being restored from a guess. All four are pinned by one test; sandwiched between two `fs::read` calls none of them would be testable without a live MCP server.
   - **Stated in the record, not just in the docs**: paths outside the workspace, files over 1 MiB, non-UTF-8 files and any file the arguments never named are *not* covered. "1 file(s) changed" without that sentence reads as "that was all of it".
   - Rust 551 → 553 (552 passing, 1 ignored); frontend 291 unchanged.
161. **The two platforms stop being the model's problem (2026-09-23)**
   Two failures that look like "the model wrote a bad command" but are ours: the shell it lands in differs by platform, and the bytes that come back are not necessarily UTF-8. New `services/command_text.rs` owns both, as pure functions.
   - **The output decoding was a live data-loss bug.** Every child's bytes went through `String::from_utf8_lossy`, so on a Chinese Windows machine cargo/MSVC/npm CP936 output became a row of U+FFFD **before** `parse_terminal_problems` read it, before the repair prompt quoted it, and before the model saw it. The failure the user was trying to fix was destroyed on the way in. Now: UTF-8 first (most toolchains emit it even there), else the active code page via `GetACP`, else lossy. `AGENT_IDE_OUTPUT_ENCODING` overrides, because a toolchain can ignore the code page and a user should not need a code change to say so. An unrecognised code page stays lossy on purpose — a wrong single-byte guess produces text that looks fine and is wrong, which is the one outcome worse than replacement characters.
   - **The command is translated, and what cannot be translated is refused with the reason.** `cmd` disagrees with `sh` on three things that have mechanical equivalents: `FOO=bar cmd` → `set "FOO=bar" && cmd`, `'a b.ts'` → `"a b.ts"` (single quotes are not quoting in `cmd`, so the path arrives as two arguments), top-level `;` → `&`. `$(…)`, backticks and mixed quoting have no equivalent and now come back as one sentence saying so, instead of `cmd` reporting that it cannot find a file named `'src/a`.
   - **Refusing beats guessing, and passing through beats both.** The most expensive failure for a translation layer is breaking a command that already worked, so a test pins six ordinary commands as byte-identical, quoted content is never treated as syntax (`rg 'a;b' src` keeps its `;`), and the tokenizer respects quotes — the first version split `MSG='two words' npm run x` on whitespace and rejected its own output as an unclosed quote.
   - **The model is told the platform.** The `workspace_run_command` description now carries `shell_brief()`: which shell, and only the rules that actually differ (`&&`, `|`, `2>&1` are the same on both, so saying so would bury the four that are not). Nothing was said before — the model had to infer the OS from context and was often right, which is the worst kind of unreliable.
   - **Reference comparison**: ZCode solves the same problem by *changing the shell* — it auto-detects Git Bash on Windows so POSIX syntax just works, and tells the model only `Platform: win32` / `Shell: Git Bash` with no rules at all. Not adopted: that moves the requirement into the user's machine (no Git for Windows, no commands), and this product's command surface is an allow-list of the project's own check commands, not a general shell. Its encoding approach *was* adopted in shape (sniff UTF-8, fall back to the code page, allow an override) minus the `chcp` subprocess — `GetACP()` is one call.
   - **Still open**: the same lossy decode remains in the PTY terminal, git diff text and HTTP bodies (`web_fetch` ignores `charset`); a named build/test/lint vocabulary that the model could call without knowing the project's exact command lines; and `.cmd`/`.bat` argument quirks beyond quoting.
   - Rust 553 → 562 (561 passing, 1 ignored); frontend 291 unchanged. New pinned dependency `encoding_rs = "=0.8.35"` (already in the lock file transitively) plus the `Win32_Globalization` feature on `windows-sys`.
162. **The audit of 159-161: undo was broken, and two translations were wrong (2026-09-23)**
   Adversarial audit of `bf9f77b`, `6f51b98`, `21140e8`. All five gates green; six defects, three of them in the undo path this product exists for.
   - **One file, two checkpoints, and the second Undo re-applied the Agent's change (fixed).** `publish_tool_writes` called `record_tool_writes` and then `record_detected_writes`, and each call pushes its own checkpoint while merging only *within* itself. MCP changes `app.ts` A→B, then `workspace_write_file` writes B→C: checkpoint #1 holds `previous = B`, #2 holds `A`. Undo is strictly LIFO, so the first Undo restores A (right) and the second writes **B** back onto disk while reporting "restored" — the Agent's intermediate content resurrected by the undo button. Two `applied` cards for one file, and "wrote N file(s)" counted it twice. Now both logs go through **one** `record_writes_from(Vec<SourcedWrite>)`, the merge key stays `(file, is_move)`, the pre-image is the earliest, and `WriteSources` names every source on the one card. Threading the source through `AgentFileWrite` was rejected again: 24 construction sites, mostly tests.
   - **The snapshot read files a built-in write would have refused (fixed).** `argument_targets` is deliberately lexical — no disk — so `probe.path` had passed neither the deny list nor any symlink check. A committed `notes.txt → ~/.ssh/id_rsa` named in MCP arguments would be *read*, put in a review card and an undo snapshot, and written back through the link on Undo — out of the workspace, in the worst direction. `{"path":".env"}` likewise put credentials on screen. Snapshotting now goes through `resolve_for_agent_write`, the same resolution a real write uses: one authority, both directions closed, and the skipped ones are counted as "not covered".
   - **The comparison cannot establish causation, and now says so.** A file the MCP tool only *read* — `path` is the same key a read tool uses — that the user happened to save during the call produced a card saying "Written while mcp__fs__read_file ran", and Undo would have destroyed the user's own edit. There is no cheap discriminator (mtime lands inside the call window either way), so the honest fix is the wording: the rationale now says the change is *attributed* to that call rather than proven to come from it, and the limit is in SECURITY.md. Publishing it is still right — an invisible change is worse than one with a hedged label.
   - **`FOO="a b" cmd` was refused with a false reason (fixed).** Only single quotes were stripped from an assignment value, so the double-quoted form tripped the "cmd cannot express this" check — for a value `set "FOO=a b"` expresses perfectly. The model was told to rewrite a command that was already correct.
   - **Two conversions changed the meaning of working commands (fixed).** `rg '%PATH%' src` became `rg "%PATH%" src`, and inside `cmd`'s double quotes `%PATH%` *expands* — the search pattern silently became the machine's PATH and the command still succeeded. An argument ending in a backslash (`'src\'` → `"src\"`) has its closing quote escaped away by the argv parser. Both are now refused with the reason instead of converted; `%` in an assignment value too. And `\"` inside double quotes is now tracked as an escape: miscounting it made a top-level `;` look quoted, so the second half of the command silently never ran.
   - **A single bad byte re-decoded the whole stream (fixed).** `decode_child_output` judged the entire buffer, so one CP936 line from MSVC inside `cargo build`'s otherwise-UTF-8 stderr turned **all** of it into GBK mojibake — the exact outcome the module's own comment says it exists to avoid. Decoding is now per line. A truncated trailing sequence from a killed child no longer poisons everything before it.
   - Verified *not* defects, since they were suspected: an apostrophe inside double quotes (`-m "it's fine"`) passes through byte-identical; `;`/`&&` inside quotes are left alone; valid UTF-8 containing a real U+FFFD is untouched; the two reader threads each own a whole stream, so no sequence is split across them; `GetACP` (not `GetConsoleOutputCP`) is right for a piped child.
   - Rust 562 → 566 (565 passing, 1 ignored); frontend 291 unchanged.
163. **A Chinese UI, starting with the chrome (2026-09-23)**
   The app had **no** localisation mechanism and was already mixed-language: `ModeSwitch` shipped English buttons with Chinese tooltips, which is what happens when there is nowhere for a translation to live. New `src/i18n/`: a typed message table, a locale store, and the top bar + mode switch translated end to end.
   - **`ZH` is typed `Record<MessageKey, string>` where `MessageKey = keyof typeof EN`.** A missing Chinese string is a **compile error**, not a silent fallback to English. That is the whole reason for not taking `i18next`/`react-intl`: their runtime fallback is exactly the machinery that produces a half-translated screen, and what this needs is two languages, one namespace and no new dependency. A test also rejects *extra* keys in `ZH` (delete one in English, forget the other, and the next person adds copy to a key nobody reads) and rejects a Chinese value that is still the English sentence.
   - **The language follows the system by default** (`navigator.language` → `zh`), so a Chinese user does not have to find a switch first — the same "defaults that work" rule as elsewhere. The value is normalised, never `as Locale`: it arrives from `localStorage` and the browser, so a stale or unknown value would otherwise walk into a table lookup and render `undefined`. `document.documentElement.lang` is set too — it drives font fallback and line breaking, and Chinese text broken by Western rules breaks at the wrong punctuation.
   - **The switch is one click in the top bar, next to the theme toggle**, not a row in the settings panel. A setting a new user cannot find is a setting that does not exist, and this is the first thing a Chinese-speaking user looks for.
   - **Wording chosen for a first-time reader, not for symmetry with the code.** `Suggest` / `Auto` became 待审查 / 自动 with descriptions saying what actually happens to the file ("改动先进审查区，你点了才落盘" / "改动直接落盘，可以一键撤销"); `Code` / `Plan` became 写代码 / 先做计划 with one-line titles, because "Plan/SDD IDE mode" explains nothing to someone who has never heard of SDD.
   - **Scope, measured rather than guessed**: ~750-850 translatable strings in the frontend, of which this round covers the top bar, the language-server panel and the mode switch (~60). `SettingsPanel.tsx` alone is ~215 — a fifth of the whole surface — and the plan view another ~72. On top of that the backend holds **250-350 user-visible English strings** (action-log summaries, approval dialog text, every `Err(String)`, the degradation report), so a frontend-only translation would leave the Logs panel, the approval prompt and every error banner in English — precisely the surfaces the "see and undo" promise rests on. That part needs the backend to know the locale; it is not a `t()` call away.
   - Frontend 291 → 298 (35 files); Rust unchanged.












































81. **Audit of 80: one door closed, the door beside it still open (2026-09-15)**
   - **`attach_mcp_tools` still took a bare `Arc<AtomicBool>` (fixed).** 80 closed the lease side by signature and left the MCP side on convention, in the same paragraph that called it the last open door. All four commands happened to pass `permissions.cancel_switch()`; a fifth could have passed anything and every test would have stayed green — on the surface whose own comment calls it "本产品最大的副作用面". It now takes `&WorkspaceToolPermissions` and reads the switch itself, so both consumers and the lease are signature-bound to one source.
   - **What is still convention, stated plainly**: `adopt_cancel` can be called *after* a claim, which would move the tools to a new flag while the lease and `CancelRegistry` keep the old one. No production path does it — all four adopt before claiming — and the fix would be a type state (`UnclaimedPermissions` → `ClaimedPermissions`) whose cost is not justified by a hazard nothing currently walks into. Recorded rather than sealed.
   - **Two tests were not travelling the path they claimed.** The image-degradation tests in `commands/agent.rs` built permissions with `new(...)` and never called `adopt_cancel`, so their flag was the derived `Default` one — 80's "the tests now travel the production path" was true of the 13 orchestrator sites only. They adopt now.
   - The audit also noted that `the_lease_and_the_permissions_share_one_cancel_switch` has lost most of its bite: the defect it was written for is now unrepresentable, and what it still pins is the `run_id` assignment. Kept, because a test that can only fail if `adopt_cancel`/`cancel_switch` break is still a test of `adopt_cancel`/`cancel_switch`.
   - Rust 361, unchanged.
